# D7 Land & Property — Phase 3 Verified File

**Domain:** D7 Land & Property
**Domain slug:** landProp
**Cluster:** D (Land, Resources, and Built Environment)
**Display name:** Land & Property *(brief scope per DOMAIN_FRAMEWORK §2: "Housing law lives here including rent regulation, fair housing, and the public-housing apparatus."*)
**Cycle:** M2 v1.2
**Phase 2 assembly date:** 2026-05-03
**Phase 3 verification date:** 2026-05-03
**Substructure date:** 2026-05-02 (lead-approved)
**Sub-domain count:** 7 (within M2 §3 5–8 guidance; no deviation rationale required)

**Verification scope (M2 v1.2 §7).** Web-search-tractable verification across the 101-item Section 4 inventory (63 F-flags + 38 T-flags). Federal regulatory administrative-vulnerability cluster (Cluster A) engaged at high priority given Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability calibration; constitutional / case-law (Cluster B), PA state-level (Cluster C), Philadelphia local statutory and program (Cluster D), and anchor institution disclosure (Cluster E) clusters engaged systematically. Tract-level retrieval surface (Cluster F) and program-portfolio operational data (Cluster G) substantially deferred to subsequent-cycle Track 2 work per M2 v1.2 §7 boundedness ("close, not perfect"); items so deferred documented in `unverified-items_D7_2026-05-03.md` sidecar with disposition. Standard 18 vector candidates surfaced across Cluster H constructed in `temporal-changes_D7_2026-05-03.md` sidecar in Standard 18.A vector format (anchor + dated vector points + analytical consequence) as Phase 1 starting points; subsequent-cycle accumulation is the mechanism by which empirical engagement proceeds. Held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) preserved per substructure §8 question 1 and lead direction 2026-05-03 — Phase 3 does not attempt closure by analytical assertion; Track 2 sequel candidates are surfaced in unverified-items sidecar. F7-SD7-01 (D7-Thread B L&I tract-level retrieval) deferred to subsequent-cycle Track 2 per lead direction 2026-05-03; structural finding (G7-SD7-01) does not depend on tract-level retrieval for analytical content.

**Regime declaration (Standard 1).** Phase 1 + Phase 2 produced under training-data-only regime; Phase 3 verification operates under internet-access regime — web-search retrieval engaged for current-state claims with retrievals citation-tracked in the Citation Registry sidecar at the end of this file. The regime shift between phases is in-bounds per M2 v1.2 §7 (Phase 3 verification is the regime-shift point at which current-state claims become retrievable); intra-phase regime stability is preserved. PA-3-specific Track 2 figures that remain unretrieved within Phase 3 web-search budget are documented in the unverified-items sidecar rather than improvised.

**Domain-level data provenance statement.** PA-3-specific Track 2 claims at the synthesis level are limited to: (a) the documented spatial pattern of zoning use categories across PA-3 sub-areas; (b) anchor-institution tax-exempt holding concentration in West Core and North/Northwest Core (structurally inferable from the OPA layer; quantification F-flagged); (c) gentrification patterns in Cedar Park / Spruce Hill, Brewerytown, Strawberry Mansion, Point Breeze, Mantua / Mill Creek, Germantown Avenue corridor; (d) racial homeownership gap at Philadelphia scale (~25–30 pp; F-flagged for current ACS); (e) racial disproportionality in homeless population (~70–75% Black in a ~44% Black city; F-flagged for current PIT). Tract-level figures across all SDs are not retrieved within Phase 1 or Phase 2; their retrieval is Phase 3 territory or institutional-retrieval territory documented per M2 v1.2 §7 boundedness.

**Phase 2 assembly note.** This OUTPUT file is the Shape A integrated artifact per M2 v1.2 §6 specification: full per-SD content (Sections 1–9 of the M2 §5 Per-SD Template) for SD1 through SD7 embedded verbatim in numerical order, sandwiched between Synthesis Sections 1–3 (composed at assembly time) before the embedded SD content and Synthesis Sections 4–5 (composed at assembly time) after the embedded SD content, with the Self-Assessment footer at the end. Per M2 v1.2 §10 Pattern 14, this file is the integrated artifact, not a manifest pointing at sibling SD files; the Step 7.5 Shape A integrity check is run before Phase 2 is declared complete and is documented in the completion summary surfaced to the lead. The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) is preserved through synthesis-level engagement per substructure §8 question 1 — the seven Phase 1 mechanisms are articulated as a complete inventory at synthesis level without analytical assertion of which is primary.

**Approval status carried forward.** Phase 0 substructure: lead-approved 2026-05-02. Phase 1 SD1: lead-approved at SD1 checkpoint 2026-05-02; SD2–SD7 produced autonomously per M2 §4 protocol with checkpoint marker `D7_landProp_checkpoint-status_2026-05-02.md` remaining in `approved` status throughout. Phase 1 lead review: complete across all seven SDs. Phase 2 OUTPUT lead-reviewed 2026-05-03. Phase 3 verification entered with Step 7.5 Shape A integrity check passed at Phase 2 close.

## Material Changes Summary (Standard 11 four-field format)

*This section is the within-document navigation aid to inline corrections applied at body locations throughout the verified file. Each entry pairs with one or more inline edits per M2 v1.2 §7 Capability 2 + Appendix A. Pattern 12 enforcement (M2 v1.2 Anti-Pattern 12; pa3-structural-quality Step 4) walks each entry against its named body location(s) before release. ### MC-01: AFFH rule rescission (Case 2; ~5 body locations)

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **What was** | "AFFH rule (T7-SD4-03)" framed as 2023 final rule operative or in litigation; the most administratively-volatile element of SD4 architecture. |
| **What is now** | The 2023 proposed AFFH rule was withdrawn by HUD in March 2025 and replaced with an Interim Final Rule (IFR) requiring only self-attestation of fair-housing-promoting actions in connection with Consolidated Plans, Annual Action Plans, and PHA Plans. HUD additionally withdrew eight FHEO guidance documents on April 6, 2026 (90+ Federal Register notice). The IFR remains pending finalization; no successor "equity-plan" rulemaking is currently anticipated at HUD. The administrative-vulnerability finding (G7-SD4-01; Standard 11 operative) is reinforced: the AFFH framework above the statutory floor has been substantively withdrawn at the regulatory level while the FHA § 3608 statutory mandate remains. |
| **When changed** | March 2025 (IFR); April 2026 (FHEO guidance withdrawal). |
| **What it affects** | SD4 §2 Federal Regulatory layer; SD4 §3 Pathway 1 *ICP* operationalization; SD4 §6 (regulatory-architecture conditions analysis); G7-SD4-01 (AFFH operationalization gap) and G7-SD4-02 (disparate-impact framework operationalization gap) calibration; Synthesis Section 1 Cross-cutting Finding D (statutory stability vs. administrative vulnerability); Synthesis Section 2 (Aggregate Finding administrative-vulnerability characterization). Inline correction applied at SD4 §2 (principal anchor) with secondary cross-reference notes at SD4 §6, SD4 §7 G7-SD4-01, Synthesis §1 Finding D, and Synthesis §2. |

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **What was** | "HUD disparate impact rule current status (T7-SD4-01)" — the 2023 reinstatement of the 2013 rule operative pending litigation. |
| **What is now** | Per Executive Order 14281 (April 2025; "Restoring Equality of Opportunity and Meritocracy"), HUD published a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking on January 14, 2026 (91 Fed. Reg. 1475) proposing to remove and reserve 24 CFR Part 100 Subpart G (§ 100.500) entirely — without replacement — leaving disparate impact interpretation under the FHA to courts. Comment period closed February 13, 2026. Final rule pending. Critically: *Texas Dep't of Housing & Cmty. Affairs v. Inclusive Communities Project*, 576 U.S. 519 (2015) remains binding precedent recognizing disparate-impact claims as cognizable under the FHA; rescission of HUD's regulatory burden-shifting framework does not extinguish the cause of action but eliminates the agency-level interpretive framework. State fair-housing laws (including PHRA) authorizing disparate-impact liability remain unaffected. |
| **When changed** | January 14, 2026 (NPRM); February 13, 2026 (comment close); final rule pending. |
| **What it affects** | SD4 §2 Federal Regulatory; SD4 §3 Pathway 1; G7-SD4-02 (disparate-impact framework operationalization gap); Synthesis Section 1 Finding D. Inline correction applied at SD4 §2 (principal anchor) with secondary notes at SD4 §3 Pathway 1 and at Synthesis §1 Finding D. |

FY25 outcomes documentation: PEPP partners served 4,600+ households (1,750+ in RTC ZIP codes); zero default judgments for PEPP-represented tenants (vs. 35–40% for unrepresented); RTC-represented tenants twice as likely to prevail at trial; 80%+ housing-preserved/stabilized/improved outcome rate; eviction filings reduced ~40% citywide via RTC + PEPP + EDP combined. Pennsylvania July 2024 budget appropriation: $2.5 million for the first statewide right-to-counsel program (eligibility ≤125% FPL); PA is now one of six states with statewide RTC. The G7-SD3-05 representation-architecture finding is substantively reinforced and refined: protective function operates substantively where coverage is established; coverage is expanding citywide on a defined trajectory; capacity-vs-need at expanded coverage is the live empirical question (F7-SD3-05 retrieval candidate). |

## Synthesis Section 1: What This Domain Established

The legal architecture governing land, property, and housing in PA-3 — Fifth Amendment Takings doctrine and PA's *Kelo* response; PA Urban Redevelopment Law and the PRA blight-and-condemnation pathway; Philadelphia Zoning Code Title 14 with the ZBA / RCO framework; HMDA / CRA / FHA / ECOA mortgage-market regulation and PHFA / Philadelphia Mortgage Foreclosure Diversion Program supporting infrastructure; the PA Landlord and Tenant Act of 1951 with Philadelphia's TOPA / EDP / Right to Counsel layered tenant-protection architecture; the FHA / *ICP* / AFFH / PHRA fair-housing-and-anti-displacement architecture with CBA private-contract instruments; HOME / CDBG / LIHTC / PA Land Bank / PHARE / PHDC / H.O.M.E. / *Defying Displacement* affordable-housing-development infrastructure; McKinney-Vento Title I/II/IV / SAMHSA PATH / VAWA / HUD-VASH / Philadelphia OHS / PA-500 CoC adult-homelessness infrastructure; and Phila. Code Title 6 / federal Lead Paint Framework / Act 135 code-enforcement and blight-remediation framework — is real, operative, and substantively designed across all seven sub-domains. The seven sub-domains traced this architecture's pathways and documented its protective functions; they also documented, mechanism by mechanism, the structural features that condition framework availability into substantive outcomes. The findings below state what each SD established, with cross-cutting findings derived from the substructure analytical threads named separately. Each finding is labeled by Standard 5 evidence type ([D] / [SD] / [SI]) and confidence ([HIGH] / [MEDIUM] / [LOW]).

**Finding 1 (SD1 — Property Rights, Title & Land Use Governance).** [SD] HIGH. The federal-state-local land-use governance architecture for PA-3 establishes a procedural framework — condemnees have just-compensation rights with appellate review; property owners have ZBA variance pathways with judicial appeal; communities have formal RCO voice in zoning decisions — whose protective function is conditional on civic, financial, and legal infrastructure capable of engaging it. The infrastructure is unevenly distributed across PA-3 in patterns correlating with the historical geography of redlining and urban-renewal displacement. The PRA Urban Redevelopment Law pathway preserves a condemnation route post-*Kelo* through the broad statutory definition of blight, historically applied in racially disparate ways. Most particular to PA-3, anchor land acquisition (Penn / Temple / Drexel / CHOP) operates substantially outside the regulatory pathway through private-market mechanisms, master-plan integration, variable community engagement, and tax-exempt-status maintenance; the regulatory framework was not designed to govern this pathway and substantially does not. Tangled-title infrastructure operates at a cost-and-complexity threshold disproportionately excluding long-tenured Black households whose intergenerational property transfers occurred outside formal probate, tracing to mid-20th-century legal-infrastructure exclusion. The relative magnitude of anchor-driven displacement against alternative mechanisms is held open per G7-SD1-03 as Track 2 empirical territory the substructure-flagged failure mode (coherence-seeking toward anchor-displacement narrative) refuses to close by analytical assertion.

**Finding 2 (SD2 — Housing Finance & the Mortgage Market).** [SD] HIGH. The mortgage-market regulatory architecture provides public lending-data accountability (HMDA), affirmative community-credit-needs obligations on federally insured depository institutions (CRA), antidiscrimination cause of action with private right (FHA § 3605, ECOA), consumer-protection floors (TILA, RESPA, HOEPA via CFPB), low-down-payment access (HUD/FHA mortgage insurance with statutory disavowal of the pre-1968 discriminatory underwriting regime), state-level supportive infrastructure (PHFA Keystone Home Loan, Keystone Advantage, HEMAP), and local foreclosure mediation (Philadelphia Mortgage Foreclosure Diversion Program, Phila. Code Ch. 9-800). Three structural features condition the framework's substantive reach. First, the post-1968 statutory disavowal ended exclusion forward but did not retroactively remediate the foreclosed-wealth historical fact; the present-day Black-white homeownership gap (approximately 25–30 percentage points; F-flagged) is substantially the documented consequence of decades of policy-mediated exclusion, against which the present-day framework operates without dedicated reparative architecture. Second, CRA's bank-only architecture excludes independent mortgage companies, now the majority of mortgage originators; the principal accountability-with-affirmative-obligation mechanism does not cover the principal segment of the present-day mortgage market. Third, the SD1 property-rights infrastructure (tangled title) conditions SD2 credit-access infrastructure: the equity-extraction pathway (refinance, HELOC, cash-out) closes entirely for tangled-title constituents disproportionately concentrated in long-tenured Black households, compounding the racial wealth gap.

**Finding 3 (SD3 — Landlord-Tenant Law & Tenant Protections).** [SD] HIGH. The federal-state-local landlord-tenant architecture provides substantive protections (PA L-T Act security-deposit limits with double-damages remedy, lease-termination notice, retaliatory-eviction prohibitions; *Pugh v. Holmes* implied warranty of habitability) and Philadelphia-layered tenant protections (TOPA right of first refusal at sale; Eviction Diversion Program pre-filing mediation; Right to Counsel program; rental licensing). Three structural features condition the framework's substantive reach. First, PA state-level structural choices — no statutory cause requirement on lease-end termination; preemption of local rent control — close the principal protective routes against rent-increase-driven and lease-end-driven displacement; Philadelphia's substantial municipal architecture operates within these state-level constraints. Second, the Municipal Court eviction docket operates at high volume with structural representation imbalance favoring landlords; outcomes turn on procedural posture as much as merits; Right to Counsel is the principal architectural response and is in phased rollout. Third, the access-to-justice gap that mediates the substantive PA L-T Act provisions is structurally tied to legal-aid capacity (CLS, PLA) annually appropriated and unevenly available across PA-3 sub-areas. The serial-filer pattern — a small number of institutional and large-scale individual landlords accounting for a disproportionate share of filings, concentrated in lower-income Black neighborhoods — operates at scale across the framework's individual-case adjudication unit; Black women are documented as the demographic most severely impacted by eviction in absolute and per-capita terms, a pattern the protective architecture engages without explicit gender-disaggregated impact analysis.

**Finding 4 (SD4 — Fair Housing & Anti-Displacement).** [SD] HIGH. The fair-housing and anti-displacement architecture provides FHA enforcement mechanics (HUD FHEO administrative complaint, private right of action, DOJ pattern-or-practice authority), the *Texas Dept. of Housing v. Inclusive Communities Project* disparate-impact framework with HUD's regulatory codification, the AFFH program-participant-obligation framework, PHRA source-of-income protection enforced through PHRC, Philadelphia's TOPA / EDP / RTC architecture functioning as fair-housing instruments, and the CBA pathway as private-contract anti-displacement mechanism with anchor institutions and major developers. Three structural features condition the framework's substantive reach. First, the toolkit's substantive design value is conditional on capacity infrastructure (legal-aid for FHA private right; nonprofit purchasing capacity for TOPA; mediator and rental-assistance capacity for EDP; PHRC enforcement capacity for source-of-income; CBA-monitoring-coalition capacity for anchor engagement) unevenly resourced across PA-3 in patterns correlating with the historical geography the framework itself addresses. Second, the federal-statutory floor is stable but the regulatory and enforcement architecture above the floor is administratively vulnerable to inter-administration revision (AFFH; disparate-impact rule; FHEO enforcement posture) — a structural feature, not episodic disruption. Third, most particular to PA-3, the principal gentrification-displacement driver — rent-increase-driven displacement at lease renewal in appreciating corridors — operates outside SD4 enforcement reach because of state-level rent-control preemption; the SD4 architecture engages sale events, eviction proceedings, source-of-income discrimination, and protected-class disparate impact, but not the rent-increase event itself. The Both/And designated by substructure §6 holds: substantive infrastructure is real, implementation gaps are real, regulatory vulnerability is real; all three load-bearing for the SD4 representation finding.

**Finding 5 (SD5 — Affordable Housing Development).** [SD] HIGH. The affordable-housing-development architecture combines federal financing (HOME, CDBG, LIHTC, Choice Neighborhoods — engaged at D12 SD4 mechanics), federal protection of displaced persons (URA), state-level enabling and capital (PA Land Bank Act; PHARE), local development-and-preservation infrastructure (Philadelphia Land Bank, PHDC's full program portfolio, PRA, DHCD), city-scale planning architecture (Housing Action Plan 2018; H.O.M.E. initiative; PHA's parallel plan; *Defying Displacement* legislation), and private permanent-affordability infrastructure (CLTs). Three structural features condition the framework's substantive reach. First, the 30-year LIHTC design horizon set in 1986 produces a cyclical affordability-loss pressure now active in PA-3 at scale — approximately 100 of 450 Philadelphia affordable-housing complexes (~22%) set to expire over the next decade per National Housing Preservation Database; the framework was designed for a finite affordability period, and the consequences of that design choice arrive predictably; H.O.M.E. preservation funding is substantive but operates against decade-scale need exceeding any single-cycle preservation budget; the Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio with July 2026 deadline is the active exemplar case. Second, the Land Bank's statutory mandate authorizes strategic disposition without imposing an exclusive-affordable rule; disposition-pace constraints (CDC-capacity asymmetry; political-relationship-dependent dynamics; throughput limits relative to inventory) produce a documented gap between statutory mandate and disposition-pace aggregate. Third, most particular to PA-3 in the current cycle, the H.O.M.E. and *Defying Displacement* architectural developments are recent; substantive achievement at implementation-and-equity-of-distribution phase is the principal forward-looking analytical question. The MC18 Tandem Plan reference could not be confirmed against primary sources within the Phase 1 3-search budget per substructure §6 lead-direction-2026-05-02 protocol; documented as confirmation-of-unresolvability item.

**Finding 6 (SD6 — Adult Homelessness Infrastructure).** [SD] HIGH. The adult-homelessness architecture provides federal program-eligibility scope (McKinney-Vento Title I), federal interagency coordination (Title II / USICH), federal program structure (Title IV — ESG, CoC competitive grant, PSH, RRH, Coordinated Entry, HMIS), federal mental-health outreach (SAMHSA PATH), survivor-protection architecture (VAWA 2022 reauthorization), veteran-targeted joint architecture (HUD-VASH), Housing First guidance via HUD CoC, and Philadelphia OHS administering the PA-500 CoC (one of the largest in the country). Three structural features condition the framework's substantive reach. First, McKinney-Vento and the federal homeless-assistance framework are sized and scoped at the federal-appropriations level rather than the documented-need level; the PSH-to-need gap is the most-visible expression but the broader pattern is that the framework operates against need at a fraction of need scale; the framework was not designed to eliminate homelessness but to manage it within available resources, and the resource-allocation discipline that follows produces the prioritization-as-rationing dynamic. Second, the framework's definitional choices — Title I (HUD/CoC) excludes doubled-up households from the homeless definition while Title VII (education) includes them — embed value choices about whose vulnerability counts most and whose situation counts as homelessness for service purposes; PIT counts and HMIS data systematically undercount housing-instability prevalence; resource-allocation decisions driven by Title I data therefore operate against an undercounted denominator. Third, most particular to the post-2024 cycle, the *Grants Pass v. Johnson* (2024) withdrawal of the federal Eighth-Amendment floor against criminalization-of-homelessness postures has removed a major component of the framework's protective architecture; alternative constitutional protections remain available but their substantive substitution at scale is uncertain. Black individuals represent approximately 70–75% of Philadelphia's homeless population in a city approximately 44% Black; the disproportionality is consistent with national patterns and traces to compounding upstream inequities documented across multiple domains. Homelessness functions as a downstream aggregation of upstream displacement mechanisms documented across SD1, SD3, SD4, SD5, and SD7, positioning SD6 within the held-open displacement-magnitude territory carried through D7.

**Finding 7 (SD7 — Code Enforcement, Housing Quality & Blight).** [SD] HIGH. The code-enforcement and housing-quality architecture provides minimum habitability standards (Phila. Code Title 6), L&I administering rental licensing, lead-paint certification (with 2022 amendments adding dust-wipe testing in elevated-risk neighborhoods at tenant turnover), vacant-property registration, emergency demolition authority, and code-violation enforcement; OpenDataPhilly publishing L&I violation data; the federal Lead Paint Framework (Title X HCDA; HUD/EPA Lead Disclosure Rule; EPA RRP Rule); Act 135 and Neighborhood Blight Reclamation conservatorship pathways; and *Camara* administrative-search framework conditioning L&I inspection authority. Three structural features condition the framework's substantive reach. First, code enforcement requires enforcement-priority decisions whose political logic does not always align with housing-quality severity; D7-Thread B (the "double pattern" articulated in substructure §7) documents two simultaneous racially-disparate patterns operating against different sub-tracts: under-enforcement in disinvested predominantly-Black neighborhoods producing housing-stock deterioration as regulatory disinvestment; selective over-enforcement in marginal/gentrifying corridors operating as displacement mechanism. Second, the federal-level lead-paint architecture operates effectively only where compliance discipline operates at scale; documented gaps in disclosure compliance, RRP contractor certification (disproportionate non-certified contractor utilization in lower-income neighborhoods), and local Lead Paint Certification translate directly to neighborhood-level lead-hazard exposure that produces neighborhood-level childhood BLL elevation per the housing-to-health pathway (D2 cross-reference; MC16). Third, most particular to the post-2022 PA-3 cycle, the recent amendments to Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 represent the principal local-level intensification of the lead-paint framework; compliance and elevated-risk-neighborhood-designation lists are recently implemented and variable. Code enforcement is not a neutral technical activity; its pattern of choices produces materially different outcomes for differently-positioned PA-3 constituents along racial, income, and neighborhood-trajectory lines.

**Cross-cutting Finding A (Anchor institution triple-role frame; DF Thread 1 in D7).** [SD] HIGH. Per Standard 10.B's domain-specific guidance, **real-estate actor is primary** in D7. Penn / Temple / Drexel / CHOP engage SD1 / SD4 / SD5 primarily and SD2 / SD7 secondarily. The cross-references to D9 SD4 (PILOT/PILOET fiscal architecture; MC15) and D10 (employer dimension) carry the other two roles; per Boundary 9, anchors have no home domain and D7 engages them sub-domain by sub-domain at the depth the structural role warrants. Three distinctly-shaped real-estate-actor patterns operate in PA-3: Penn's continuous West Philadelphia steady-state acquisition (Pathway 3a; no discrete moment at which constituent input is solicited or required because the acquisition is continuous rather than project-bound); Temple's North Philadelphia contested-expansion pattern (Pathway 3b; project-level engagement variable; framework's absorption of contestation selective — defeated 2019 athletics stadium proposal; ongoing dormitory and academic-building development without binding CBA covering the broader expansion footprint); Drexel's Schuylkill Yards master-planned-redevelopment (Pathway 3c; most-organized community-engagement channel of the three; Mantua / Mill Creek interface as documented displacement-pressure interface; CBA enforceability the structural test).

**Cross-cutting Finding B (Cumulative racial disadvantage geography; DF Thread 2 in D7).** [SD] HIGH. The historical geography of redlining, urban-renewal displacement, and the legal-infrastructure exclusion of Black households from formal probate / mortgage-credit / estate-planning routes operates as the present-day structural underlay across SD1 (zoning as racial-segregation legal mechanism after *Shelley*; PRA blight-designation displacement history; tangled-title geography), SD2 (HMDA / CRA / FHA mortgage market patterns; HOLC redlining current-outcomes correlation; the documented Black-white homeownership gap), SD4 (FHEO complaint-volume gap vs. likely incidence; AFFH disparate-impact framework; gentrification corridor displacement geography), SD6 (racial disproportionality in homeless population), and SD7 (housing-age distribution correlating with redlined neighborhoods → lead-paint exposure pathway; code-enforcement geography). The pattern is structurally unitary across the legal-architecture decomposition; the architectural disavowal post-1968 ended exclusion forward without retroactively remediating the foreclosed-wealth historical fact, against which present-day frameworks operate without dedicated reparative architecture.

**Cross-cutting Finding C (Federal-state-local layered architecture; DF Thread 3 in D7).** [SD] HIGH. PA-3's land-and-housing legal architecture exhibits the federal-floor + state-supplementation + local-supplementation layered structure that is project-level. The structure is particularly acute in D7 because the federal floor (FHA; HMDA; CRA; URA; McKinney-Vento; Title X HCDA) plus PA state supplementation that supplies in some places (PA L-T Act of 1951; *Pugh v. Holmes*; PHRA; PHFA / HEMAP / PHARE; PA Eminent Domain Code; PA Urban Redevelopment Law; PA Land Bank Act; Act 135) and forecloses in others (preemption of local rent control; no statutory cause requirement on lease-end termination) plus Philadelphia local supplementation (Title 14 Zoning Code; TOPA; EDP; Right to Counsel; H.O.M.E.; *Defying Displacement*; Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 lead-paint amendments; rental licensing; Land Bank; PHDC; OHS / PA-500 CoC) produces a bifurcated power-flow shape: where state-level supplementation supplies, the framework operates with local-level capacity to extend; where state-level supplementation forecloses, the framework operates with local-level capacity foreclosed regardless of architectural will at the local level. The principal example is rent-increase-driven displacement at lease renewal — the principal gentrification-displacement driver in PA-3 — operating outside Philadelphia's substantial anti-displacement architecture by state-level structural choice (G7-SD3-02; G7-SD4-07).

**Cross-cutting Finding D (Statutory stability vs. administrative vulnerability; DF Thread 7 in D7; Standard 11 operative).** [SD] HIGH. D7 is highly active in the Standard 11 two-dimensional classification. Federal statutory floors (FHA; HMDA; CRA; McKinney-Vento; Title X HCDA; URA) are statutorily stable but administratively vulnerable: the AFFH rule (T7-SD4-03), CRA Final Rule (T7-SD2-01), HUD disparate-impact rule (T7-SD4-01), HUD FHEO enforcement-priority posture (T7-SD4-02), CFPB structural and enforcement-priority status (T7-SD2-02), HUD CoC NOFO and McKinney-Vento appropriations posture (T7-SD6-02), HUD Office of Lead Hazard Control funding (T7-SD7-01), and SAMHSA PATH appropriations (T7-SD6-03) are all subject to executive-branch revision. The vulnerability is not episodic disruption; it is a structural feature of the architecture above the statutory floor. Where Standard 11 is operative, Both/And does not apply (per substructure §6 narrow-trigger rule); the vulnerability is itself the finding.

*Verification update (2026-05-03): see principal anchors at SD4 §2 (MC-01 AFFH rescission; MC-02 disparate-impact rule proposed rescission; MC-06 FHEO enforcement-posture shift) and SD2 §2 (MC-03 CFPB structural transformation under OBBBA; MC-04 2023 CRA Final Rule rescission proposed) and SD6 §2 (MC-05 HUD CoC NOFO disruption + Congressional bridge protections). The administrative-vulnerability finding documented at the substructure-and-Phase-2 level has been operationalized between draft date and verification date by simultaneous developments at all five named regulatory frameworks (AFFH; disparate-impact rule; FHEO enforcement; CFPB structure; CRA Final Rule) plus the HUD CoC framework. The convergence is structurally significant: the Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability finding is reinforced rather than refuted; the calibration toward "structural feature, not episodic disruption" is supported by the documented simultaneity. The architecture-above-statutory-floor framing holds — the FHA, HMDA, CRA, McKinney-Vento, and other statutory authorities remain in force at the statutory level; the regulatory architecture above each has been substantively withdrawn or destabilized between draft date and verification date.*

**Cross-cutting Finding E (Federal-floor enforcement / compliance financing structural mismatch; DF Thread 4 in D7).** [SI] MEDIUM. The pattern operates in two D7-specific instances. First, the EPA RRP Rule requires contractor certification for renovation in pre-1978 housing without compliance financing for low-income property owners (G7-SD7-06); non-certified contractors are documented as disproportionately used in lower-income neighborhoods where formal contractor-network access is constrained, with documented risk of creating or worsening lead hazards during renovation. Second, CRA examination obligations operate without federal compliance-financing infrastructure for non-bank lenders, who sit outside CRA entirely (G7-SD2-02) — a different structural gap of the same family: the federal floor exists; the compliance-supportive infrastructure does not match the population the floor was designed to reach.

**Cross-cutting Finding F (Layered household fiscal architecture interface; DF Thread 5 in D7).** [SI] MEDIUM. D7 specifically touches the project-level layered-fiscal-architecture thread for **housing cost burden**. Live in SD3 (rent burden conditioning eviction risk and the eviction Municipal Court entry pathway) and SD2 (mortgage cost burden conditioning foreclosure risk and homeownership access; HEMAP serving as the back-end protective infrastructure). D9 owns the substantive analysis of the layered fiscal architecture; D7 references at the housing-cost interface where housing-cost burden conditions household exposure to D7-architecture's triggering events.

**Cross-cutting Finding G (D7-Thread A — displacement architecture mechanism inventory; substructure §7).** [SD] HIGH (mechanism inventory); G7-SD1-03 magnitude question HELD-OPEN. The seven Phase 1 SDs collectively document a complete mechanism inventory for displacement in PA-3: anchor-driven acquisition (SD1; Pathways 3a / 3b / 3c); foreclosure-driven displacement (SD2; the principal protection of which is the Mortgage Foreclosure Diversion Program operating under G7-SD2-03 capacity gap); no-cause termination + serial filing landlord patterns (SD3; G7-SD3-01 / G7-SD3-06; structural outside-Philadelphia-reach due to state-level no-cause posture); fair-housing enforcement gaps + gentrification corridors (SD4; FHEO volume vs. likely incidence; PHRA source-of-income enforcement; CBA enforceability; documented gentrifying corridors at Point Breeze, Brewerytown, Strawberry Mansion, Cedar Park / Spruce Hill, Germantown Avenue corridor, Mantua / Mill Creek); LIHTC affordability expiration + Land Bank disposition (SD5; G7-SD5-02 / G7-SD5-01; 22% portfolio expiration over decade; Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit July 2026 case); homelessness as downstream aggregation (SD6; ~70–75% Black in ~44% Black city); selective code enforcement in gentrifying corridors (SD7; D7-Thread B selective-over-enforcement direction). The relative magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) — *anchor institution expansion vs. serial eviction filers vs. speculative individual landlord acquisition vs. LIHTC affordability expiration vs. selective code enforcement, what is the relative magnitude of each as a displacement force in PA-3* — is held open per substructure §8 question 1 through Phase 1 and through Phase 2 synthesis-level engagement; no SD's gap analysis or Track 2 finding closes the question by analytical assertion; the synthesis articulates the mechanism inventory collectively without choosing among the mechanisms.

**Cross-cutting Finding H (D7-Thread B — code enforcement double pattern; substructure §7).** [SD] HIGH. SD7-primary and SD4-secondary. The pattern: under-enforcement in disinvested predominantly-Black neighborhoods accelerating housing-stock deterioration AND selective over-enforcement in marginal/gentrifying neighborhoods as displacement mechanism — both racially disparate but cutting opposite directions. The pattern's "two patterns at once, opposite directions" structure is particular to housing code enforcement and does not generalize cleanly to other DOMAIN_FRAMEWORK threads. L&I violation data analyzed against neighborhood market typology and ownership patterns is the Track 2 territory where the thread surfaces (F7-SD7-01 retrieves the empirical documentation).

**Cross-cutting Finding I (MC19 — McKinney-Vento Title I / Title VII definitional gap; foregrounded methodologically per lead direction 2026-05-02).** [D] HIGH. McKinney-Vento Title I (HUD/CoC) excludes doubled-up households from the homeless definition; Title VII (education) includes them. Families doubled up are counted as homeless for educational services but not for CoC services. PIT counts and HMIS data systematically undercount housing-instability prevalence; resource-allocation decisions driven by Title I data therefore operate against an undercounted denominator. The data infrastructure itself reproduces the distortion under analysis — a substantive methodological finding the project's representation framework engages directly. Cross-domain carry-forward: when the D11 Education cycle runs, the McKinney-Vento Title VII SD should engage D7 SD6's definitional-gap finding rather than re-derive it.

---

## Synthesis Section 2: The Aggregate Finding

**Statement (per Standard 4 — structural consequence formulation).** The documented design of PA-3's land-and-housing legal architecture — federal floors (FHA; HMDA; CRA; URA; McKinney-Vento; Title X HCDA; AFFH-as-program-participant-obligation; *ICP* disparate-impact recognition) plus PA state supplementation that supplies at some points (PA L-T Act of 1951; *Pugh v. Holmes*; PHRA source-of-income; PHFA / HEMAP / PHARE; PA Eminent Domain Code post-*Kelo* with broad blight retention; Urban Redevelopment Law; Land Bank Act; Act 135) and forecloses at others (preemption of local rent control; no statutory cause requirement on lease-end termination); plus Philadelphia local supplementation (Title 14 Zoning Code with the ZBA / RCO procedural framework; TOPA; Eviction Diversion Program; Right to Counsel; rental licensing; Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 lead-paint amendments; Land Bank; PHDC; PRA; OHS / PA-500 CoC; H.O.M.E. and *Defying Displacement* post-2024) — produces a structural pattern in which formal procedural availability is broad while substantive protective outcomes are conditional on capacity infrastructure unevenly distributed across PA-3 in patterns correlating with the historical geography the architecture itself acknowledges. The pattern is predictable from the mechanics of the documented design when applied to PA-3's documented income, demographic, and ownership conditions.

**The structural mechanics of the gap between formal availability and substantive protection.** The pattern crystallizes through five structurally distinct mechanisms that cut across the seven sub-domains. *First*, the framework's protective function is conditional on civic-and-legal infrastructure capacity — RCO resources for Title 14 procedural-democracy; legal-aid capacity (CLS, PLA, Philadelphia VIP) for self-asserting-rights enforcement under PA L-T Act and FHA private right; nonprofit purchasing capacity for TOPA; mediator-and-rental-assistance capacity for EDP; PHRC enforcement capacity for source-of-income; HUD-approved-counselor capacity for foreclosure mediation; CDC-and-coalition capacity for Act 135 conservatorship and CBA monitoring; CoC partner-organization capacity for PA-500 PSH / RRH delivery — and this capacity is unevenly resourced across PA-3 in patterns correlating with the historical geography of redlining, urban-renewal displacement, and exclusion the framework itself acknowledges and addresses. The framework presumes infrastructure it does not provide; the infrastructure's distribution does the analytical work the framework does not do explicitly. *Second*, the federal-statutory floor is stable but the regulatory and enforcement architecture above the floor is administratively vulnerable to inter-administration revision — AFFH rule cycles; disparate-impact rule cycles; HUD FHEO enforcement posture; CFPB structural status; CRA Final Rule implementation status; HUD CoC NOFO priorities; HUD Lead Hazard Control funding; SAMHSA PATH appropriations — making the framework's substantive content cycle-dependent at the architectural layer where its protective content is concentrated; the vulnerability is structural, not episodic. *Third*, state-level structural choices in Pennsylvania — the no-cause-termination posture; rent-control preemption; "Tax Fairness" / multi-judicial-decision procedural complexity at Act 135; PA Eminent Domain Code post-*Kelo* response with broad blight retention — close the principal protective routes against rent-increase-driven and lease-end-driven displacement (the principal gentrification-displacement driver in appreciating PA-3 corridors), and Philadelphia's substantial municipal architecture operates within these state-level constraints regardless of architectural will at the local level. *Fourth*, the framework engages government action on real property (Pathways 1 and 2 at SD1; the FHA enforcement architecture at SD4; the protected-class disparate-impact framework at SD4) but engages private-market action only through the antidiscrimination prism (FHA § 3604, § 3605; PHRA; PHRC; HMDA-derived denial-rate analysis); the principal anchor pathway (Pathway 3 — open-market acquisition by tax-exempt institutions) operates substantially outside the regulatory architecture's reach, with no statutory community-engagement floor outside Pathway 2's RCO regime, and CBAs as private contracts whose enforceability and monitoring depend on community-organization capacity not scaling with project timeline. *Fifth*, framework definitional choices embed value choices about whose situation counts — Title I CoC excludes doubled-up households that Title VII education includes; PSH chronic-homelessness priority criteria; Coordinated Entry assessment-tool prioritization with documented racial-disparity risk; HEMAP eligibility excluding certain chronic-shock conditions; CRA's bank-only architecture excluding the principal segment of the present-day mortgage market; LIHTC's 30-year design horizon producing a cyclical affordability-loss pressure now active in PA-3 at scale — and these definitional choices condition the framework's analytical units in ways that systematically undercount or under-engage populations whose vulnerability falls outside the framework's analytical unit.

**Held-open displacement-magnitude question — synthesis-level engagement.** Per substructure §8 question 1, the relative-magnitude question — *anchor institution expansion vs. serial eviction filers vs. speculative individual landlord acquisition vs. foreclosure-driven displacement vs. LIHTC affordability expiration vs. selective code enforcement, what is the relative magnitude of each as a displacement force in PA-3* — remains held-open through Phase 1 and through Phase 2 synthesis-level engagement. The seven Phase 1 mechanisms now form the complete Phase 1 documentation set: anchor-driven acquisition (SD1; Pathways 3a / 3b / 3c with three distinctly-shaped institutional forms); foreclosure-driven displacement (SD2; mediated through the Diversion Program operating at capacity gap to load); no-cause termination plus serial-filer eviction patterns (SD3; structurally outside Philadelphia local reach due to state-level preemption posture); fair-housing enforcement gaps in gentrification corridors (SD4; FHEO complaint volume substantially below likely incidence; CBA enforceability; PHRA source-of-income enforcement; rent-increase-driven displacement outside SD4 reach by state-level preemption); LIHTC affordability expiration plus Land Bank disposition decisions (SD5; ~22% portfolio expiration over a decade; Land Bank disposition pace below inventory-and-need scale); homelessness as downstream aggregation of upstream displacement mechanisms (SD6; ~70–75% Black in ~44% Black city); selective code enforcement in marginal/gentrifying corridors (SD7; D7-Thread B selective-over-enforcement direction). Each mechanism is structurally documented; the mechanism inventory is complete at the level of identified pathways and breakdown points. The synthesis articulates this mechanism inventory collectively and engages the structural-consequence finding that *across all seven mechanisms the formal procedural availability is broad while substantive protective outcomes are conditional* — the cross-cutting finding the substructure projected. The synthesis does not assert which mechanism is primary in aggregate displacement-force terms; the substructure-flagged failure mode (coherence-seeking toward the anchor-displacement narrative; coherence-seeking toward any single-mechanism narrative) is refused at the synthesis level as it is at the SD level. The relative-magnitude question is restated as Track 2 empirical territory the data sources identified across SD1-SD7 can begin to answer (HMDA-derived denial-rate vectors; eviction-filing-rate vectors; PIT-count vectors; PSH-inventory vectors; Land Bank disposition-pace vectors; LIHTC-expiration-cycle vectors; FHEO complaint-volume vectors; per-corridor displacement-rate vectors; tract-level demographic-change vectors at named gentrifying corridors — all anchored at Standard 18 vector format for cross-cycle persistence) but do not presently resolve. The synthesis identifies the mechanism inventory as complete and the magnitude question as structurally documented at synthesis level; the analytical-assertion closure is the failure mode the substructure named, and the synthesis refuses it.

**The aggregate finding's structural-consequence framing.** The pattern is not characterized as a coherent architecture, an intentional design, or a system designed-to-disadvantage; per Standard 4 it is characterized as a structural consequence of documented design features. Five mechanisms operate; each is documented; their compound effect produces the gap between formal procedural availability and substantive protective outcomes; the gap correlates with the historical geography the architecture itself acknowledges. The architecture's protective function is real where capacity infrastructure aligns; the gap between framework availability and substantive outcomes is structurally traceable to design features (capacity-conditional infrastructure; statutory-floor stable but administratively-vulnerable above-floor architecture; state-level-foreclosure of certain protective routes; framework engagement of government action on real property without comparable engagement of anchor private-market action; framework definitional choices conditioning analytical units). The Both/And designations applied at SD3 (security-deposit, retaliatory-eviction, habitability provisions plus TOPA / EDP / RTC layered architecture), SD4 (anti-displacement legal toolkit), and SD5 (Land Bank, PHDC, PHARE) hold at the synthesis level: substantive infrastructure is real *and* implementation gaps are real; both load-bearing for the aggregate finding. The infrastructure exists; the question is whether it is scoped, resourced, coordinated, and politically stable to address the displacement geography its architecture acknowledges and addresses.

---

## Synthesis Section 3: Confidence Calibration

Per Standard 5.A, synthesis confidence does not exceed confidence in the weakest SD finding it synthesizes. The synthesis's findings are calibrated below at three levels of grounding.

**Most securely grounded findings (HIGH confidence; [SD] structurally derived from documented premises across multiple SDs; or [D] directly documented).**

The architecture's protective function is conditional on civic-and-legal infrastructure capacity unevenly distributed across PA-3. *Grounding:* multiple independent SD findings (G7-SD1-04 RCO capacity-and-representativeness; G7-SD3-03 eviction-court representation imbalance / RTC rollout; G7-SD3-04 PA L-T Act access-to-justice; G7-SD3-05 TOPA implementation capacity; G7-SD4-03 FHEO enforcement-volume vs. likely-incidence; G7-SD4-06 anti-displacement toolkit capacity-vs-need; G7-SD5-05 PHARE / PHDC capacity-vs-need; G7-SD6-02 PSH-to-need resource gap; G7-SD7-05 Act 135 conservatorship utilization-vs-blight-volume). Multiple sources support the pattern; the structural inference is observable across the legal-architecture decomposition.

The federal-statutory floor is stable but the architecture above the floor is administratively vulnerable. *Grounding:* multiple independent SD findings under Standard 11 operative discipline (G7-SD4-01 AFFH regulatory-contingency; G7-SD4-02 disparate-impact rule administrative-vulnerability; G7-SD6-06 post-*Grants Pass* enforcement-architecture vulnerability; T-flag inventory across all SDs documenting administration-contingent variables). [D] HIGH on the named regulatory cycles (AFFH 2015 / 2020 / 2023; *Grants Pass v. Johnson* 2024 docketed); [SD] HIGH on the structural-feature framing of vulnerability across the architecture above the statutory floor.

State-level structural choices (no-cause posture; rent-control preemption) close principal protective routes regardless of municipal architectural will. *Grounding:* G7-SD3-01 PA no-cause-termination protection gap; G7-SD3-02 PA local rent-control preemption gap; G7-SD4-07 PA preemption of local rent control gap (parallel). [D] HIGH on the statutory facts (no statutory cause requirement; rent-control preemption); [SD] HIGH on the consequence chain (rent-increase-driven displacement at lease renewal in appreciating corridors operating outside SD3/SD4 enforcement reach).

Cumulative racial disadvantage geography operates as structural underlay across the legal-architecture decomposition. *Grounding:* multiple independent SD findings (SD1 zoning history and tangled-title geography; SD2 HMDA denial-rate gaps and Black-white homeownership gap of ~25–30 pp; SD4 FHEO complaint volume vs. incidence and gentrification corridor displacement geography; SD6 racial disproportionality in homeless population; SD7 housing-age distribution correlating with redlined neighborhoods → lead-paint pathway and code-enforcement geography). [SD] HIGH on the pattern; [SI] MEDIUM on PA-3-specific quantification absent retrieval (F7-SD2-05; F7-SD6-02; F7-SD7-08; tract-level cross-tabulation deferred to Phase 3 / institutional-retrieval).

McKinney-Vento Title I / Title VII definitional gap (MC19) reproduces the distortion under analysis. *Grounding:* G7-SD6-03 directly documented at the federal-statutory level. [D] HIGH on the definitional facts; [D] HIGH on the consequence (Title VII counts that Title I does not).

D7-Thread B "double pattern" of code enforcement (under-enforcement in disinvested predominantly-Black neighborhoods + selective over-enforcement in gentrifying corridors). *Grounding:* G7-SD7-01 directly documented at the analytical-pattern level; [SD] HIGH on the structural feature; [SI] MEDIUM on PA-3-specific tract-level empirical documentation absent F7-SD7-01 retrieval.

**Findings grounded principally in structural inference (MEDIUM confidence; [SD] / [SI]; secured by national research direction with PA-3-specific quantification F-flagged).**

PA-3-specific tract-level disaggregation across all SDs is the principal MEDIUM-confidence territory. The structural patterns are observable across the architecture; tract-level empirical documentation requires retrieval flagged at Phase 3 (HMDA / Reinvestment Fund / NCRC tract-level analyses; ZBA variance grant-rate disaggregation; tangled-title incidence; CRA examination ratings; current TOPA / EDP / RTC outcome data; PHDC program portfolio uptake by neighborhood; Land Bank disposition by sub-area; LIHTC expiration inventory by project; OHS PIT count current cycle; L&I violation tract-level concentration). The inferences are structurally derived from documented patterns at the citywide level; PA-3-specific quantification is conditional on Phase 3 retrieval or institutional-retrieval per M2 v1.2 §7 boundedness.

The CBA enforceability gap (G7-SD4-05) is documented at the structural level; PA-3-specific monitoring-and-compliance documentation across Penn / West Philadelphia, Temple / North Philadelphia, and Drexel / Mantua–Mill Creek interfaces is F-flagged for current institutional-disclosure and community-documentation retrieval (F7-SD4-05; F7-SD1-04 / F7-SD1-05 / F7-SD1-06).

The Coordinated Entry assessment-prioritization equity question (G7-SD6-05) is documented at the design-feature level (standardized assessment tools embed value choices); current Philadelphia / PA-500 tool, prioritization criteria, and outcome equity are F-flagged (F7-SD6-05).

H.O.M.E. plan and *Defying Displacement* implementation-and-equity-of-distribution outcomes (G7-SD5-06) are open at draft date; substantive achievement at implementation-and-equity-of-distribution phase is the principal forward-looking analytical question conditional on subsequent-cycle retrieval.

**Held-open territory (LOW confidence as a single-mechanism magnitude assertion; HIGH confidence as a complete mechanism inventory; G7-SD1-03 HELD-OPEN per substructure §8 question 1 and Phase 2 §10 Pattern 14 substructure-binding discipline).**

The relative-magnitude question — anchor institution expansion vs. serial eviction filers vs. speculative individual landlord acquisition vs. foreclosure-driven displacement vs. LIHTC affordability expiration vs. selective code enforcement, what is the relative magnitude of each as a displacement force in PA-3 — is held open at synthesis level. The mechanism inventory is documented [SD] HIGH; the relative-magnitude assertion across mechanisms is [SI] LOW at the level of which mechanism is primary in aggregate displacement-force terms. The synthesis-level engagement articulates the mechanism inventory collectively and the structural-consequence finding about formal-procedure-vs-substantive-protection across mechanisms; the synthesis does not close the magnitude question by analytical assertion. Closing by analytical assertion is the substructure-flagged failure mode (coherence-seeking toward any single-mechanism narrative) the synthesis refuses. The relative-magnitude question is restated at the synthesis level as Track 2 empirical territory the Standard 18 vector candidates surfaced across SD1-SD7 (T-flag synthesis sub-rows in each SD §9) can begin to answer through Phase 3 verification and subsequent-cycle accumulation; the question is documented as structurally pending rather than analytically resolved.

**Confidence calibration on the cross-cutting findings.**

Cross-cutting Findings A, B, C, D, G, H, I (anchor triple role, racial disadvantage geography, federal-state-local layered architecture, statutory stability vs. administrative vulnerability, displacement architecture mechanism inventory, code-enforcement double pattern, MC19 definitional gap) are calibrated [SD] HIGH or [D] HIGH with the qualifications noted above. Cross-cutting Findings E and F (federal-floor-without-compliance-financing structural mismatch; layered household fiscal architecture interface) are calibrated [SI] MEDIUM — the patterns are structurally consistent with documented evidence but PA-3-specific quantification (RRP contractor certification rates; CRA non-bank coverage proportion; rent-burden conditioning eviction risk in PA-3; mortgage cost-burden conditioning foreclosure risk) is F-flagged for retrieval.

**Standard 17 governmental-score citation discipline note.** No CBO-equivalent governmental score for D7's substantive findings emerged in Phase 1 in a form requiring Standard 17 disposition. Where Phase 3 verification touches governmental scores in cross-domain references (D9 SD4 PILOT cost; D12 SD4 OBBBA architectural finding crossing into D7 housing assistance interface), the Standard 17 discipline applies — original score preserved as primary architectural finding; updated baseline added as supplementary citation with explicit comparator. The carry-forward of D12's CBO July 2025 → CBO February 2026 baseline handling on the OBBBA architectural finding (per M2 v1.2 §7 worked example) operates at the cross-domain interface where the housing-assistance benefit architecture meets the housing law architecture; D7's synthesis does not introduce new governmental scores in Phase 1 / Phase 2.

**Standard 18 vector-format temporal-changes sidecar note.** Per substructure §10 Standard 18 deferral, vector-format temporal-changes sidecar construction is Phase 3 work, not Phase 2. Phase 2 surfaces vector candidates in the Section 4 verification inventory below — including the worker-introduced T7-SD7-06 meta-flag pattern that surfaces the cross-SD vector-candidate aggregation explicitly (and is mirrored in T-flag sub-rows in SD1, SD2, SD3, SD4, SD5, SD6 Section 9 inventories). The vector-format sidecar (`temporal-changes_D7_{date}.md`) is constructed in Phase 3 against the resolved temporal-change inventory.
