# D7 Sub-Domain 1: Property Rights, Title & Land Use Governance

**Domain:** D7 Land & Property
**Sub-Domain:** SD1 of 7
**Cycle:** M2 v1.2
**Draft date:** 2026-05-02

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD1 covers the legal architecture governing land use, property rights, and title in PA-3: Fifth Amendment Takings doctrine and Pennsylvania's *Kelo* response (PA Eminent Domain Code, 26 Pa.C.S. § 101 et seq.); PA Urban Redevelopment Law (35 P.S. § 1701 et seq.) and the PRA blight-and-condemnation pathway; Philadelphia Zoning Code (Title 14) including ZBA variance practice; the Registered Community Organization (RCO) framework; the Philadelphia 2035 Comprehensive Plan; and the anchor-institution real-estate footprint of Penn, Temple, Drexel, and CHOP. Title and inheritance — particularly tangled-title — are engaged where they intersect property-rights pathways. Fair-housing displacement is D7 SD4; mortgage-credit regulation is D7 SD2; landlord-tenant law and PA preemption of rent control are D7 SD3.

**Data provenance regime (Standard 1).** Training-data-only regime. Track 1 legal-framework content draws on the development conversation's statutory and case citations. Track 2 quantitative content draws on prior-pass statistical descriptions and structural inferences from documented patterns; PA-3-specific verified figures (current ZBA approval rates by neighborhood; current Penn PILOT specifics; OPA tax-exempt parcel inventory) are flagged for Phase 3 retrieval rather than asserted. Mixed-regime drafting is not used.

**Provenance scope.** PA-3-specific Track 2 claims are limited to: (a) documented spatial pattern of zoning use categories across PA-3 sub-areas; (b) anchor-institution tax-exempt holdings in West Core and North/Northwest Core (structurally inferable from the OPA layer); (c) gentrification patterns in Cedar Park / Spruce Hill, Brewerytown, Strawberry Mansion, and Point Breeze. Tract-level figures are not retrieved within this SD; their retrieval is a Phase 3 F-flag.

---

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.** Fifth Amendment Takings Clause, applied to states through the Fourteenth, defines the outer limit of governmental authority over land. Doctrine: physical takings per se compensable (*Loretto*, 458 U.S. 419 (1982)); regulatory takings under *Penn Central* multi-factor balancing (438 U.S. 104 (1978)); categorical takings where regulation denies all economically beneficial use absent background nuisance principles (*Lucas*, 505 U.S. 1003 (1992)); public-use doctrine post-*Kelo* (545 U.S. 469 (2005)) permitted economic-development takings under federal minimums while triggering state-level legislative responses including Pennsylvania's. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* LOW (operates through litigation, not rulemaking).

**Federal Statutory and Agency Layer.** Uniform Relocation Assistance and Real Property Acquisition Policies Act (URA, 42 U.S.C. § 4601 et seq.) protects persons displaced by federally funded projects; full URA engaged at D7 SD5. No federal agency is primary actor in SD1; HUD funding-conditions and fair-housing oversight engaged at D7 SD4. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (HUD enforcement-priority and AFFH-rule status carried as T-flags at D7 SD4; DF Thread 7).

**State Statutory Layer.** PA Eminent Domain Code (26 Pa.C.S. § 101 et seq.) provides the procedural framework for condemnation: authorization, condemnation petition and court review, just compensation at fair market value at time of condemnation, condemnee rights, inverse condemnation. Following *Kelo*, PA enacted limitations at 26 Pa.C.S. § 204 restricting condemnation primarily for private use; the post-*Kelo* text includes carve-outs for blight-related takings and certain redevelopment activity, the structural shape of PA's *Kelo* response. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* LOW.

PA Urban Redevelopment Law (35 P.S. § 1701 et seq.) authorizes municipal redevelopment authorities — including PRA — to certify blighted areas, acquire by eminent domain or purchase, and dispose to private developers. The PA blight definition is broad and was applied in racially disparate ways in mid-20th-century Philadelphia urban-renewal clearance, displacing predominantly Black residents in Mantua, Mill Creek, North Philadelphia, and Penn-adjacent neighborhoods — historical pattern shaping current political meaning of blight law in PA-3. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE (PRA discretion in certification and disposition).

PA preempts local rent control. Engaged primarily at D7 SD3; conditions D7 broadly because Philadelphia's anti-displacement architecture must operate without rent stabilization. *Stability:* HIGH.

**State Agency Layer.** PA DCED administers URL framework at state level. PHFA operates statewide in mortgage finance (D7 SD2). *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE.

**Local Statutory Layer.** Philadelphia Code Title 14 (Zoning Code), comprehensively revised effective 2013, establishes use categories — residential (RSA, RSD, RTA, RM); commercial (CMX); industrial (ICMX, I1–I3); overlays (historic, neighborhood conservation, area plans). Distribution across PA-3 is not politically neutral: industrial zoning concentrates adjacent to Black residential neighborhoods in North and West Philadelphia; high-density residential zoning near transit is relatively scarce in lower-income corridors; protective overlays concentrate in higher-income Northwest Philadelphia. Title 14 also establishes the ZBA framework and the RCO notification regime. The Philadelphia 2035 Comprehensive Plan supplies long-range framework and district plans; not legally binding on the ZBA but provides planning rationale. *Stability:* MODERATE-HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE.

**Local Agency Layer.** ZBA hears variances, special exceptions, administrative appeals. Department of Planning and Development administers comprehensive plan and zoning code. PRA administers URL framework locally. L&I administers permitting and code-compliance (primary in D7 SD7). Philadelphia Land Bank (Phila. Code Ch. 16-500) engaged primarily at D7 SD5. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (ZBA practice and PRA discretion are documented variation sites).

**Constituent Layer.** See Section 5.

**Cross-references (Standard 9 format).** D9 SD4: anchor PILOT/PILOET architecture and tax-exempt parcel concentration (MC15). D13 (Verified): historical urban-renewal displacement and infrastructure inheritance. D12 SD4: housing-assistance siting where PHA properties are subject to local zoning. D7 SD3: PA preemption of rent control. D7 SD4: FHA enforcement and *Texas Dept. of Housing v. ICP* disparate-impact framework. D7 SD5: URA-PRA intersection on Penn-adjacent and Temple-adjacent redevelopment.

---

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

Three pathways operate. Each is traced from triggering event through institutional steps to outcome, with breakdown points identified.

**Pathway 1: Eminent domain via blight (URL → PRA → just compensation → appeal).** Initiated when PRA pursues a redevelopment-area designation under 35 P.S. § 1701 et seq., typically tied to district planning under Philadelphia 2035 or anchor-adjacent / transit-oriented redevelopment opportunity. Steps: (a) blight certification (PRA recommendation; City Council ordinance approval typically required); (b) acquisition by purchase or, where purchase fails, condemnation petition under PA Eminent Domain Code; (c) court review; (d) just-compensation determination at fair market value at time of condemnation, with condemnee right to Board of Viewers proceedings; (e) appeal to CCP; (f) PRA disposition to private developer. *Breakdown:* the broad blight definition has historically been applied in racially disparate ways; condemnees have formal rights but face represented agency adversaries while typically lacking counsel; just-compensation valuations can lag market appreciation in gentrifying corridors; § 204 *Kelo*-response carve-outs preserve URL as the primary post-*Kelo* condemnation route. Institutional capacity is asymmetric — representation, valuation expertise, appellate access.

**Pathway 2: ZBA variance (application → RCO notification and meeting → ZBA hearing → decision → CCP appeal).** Steps: (a) application filed with L&I or directly with ZBA; (b) RCO notification — applicants for certain permits and variances must notify Registered Community Organizations covering the location and meet with them; (c) RCO meetings, where community input is formally taken (RCO position is advisory); (d) ZBA hearing, where the applicant must show unnecessary hardship (use variance) or de minimis dimensional impact (dimensional variance) and non-detriment to public interest; (e) ZBA decision; (f) appeal to CCP. *Breakdown:* the RCO step is the primary mechanism through which existing-resident voice is formally embedded, and its protective function depends on RCO capacity (paid staff, technical expertise, organizational continuity) which is unevenly distributed across PA-3 — engaged in §4, §6, and G7-SD1-04. The framework does not provide the civic infrastructure it presumes.

**Pathway 3: Anchor land acquisition (open-market acquisition → master-plan integration → community engagement variability → zoning compliance → tax-exempt status maintenance).** Qualitatively different from Pathways 1 and 2 because anchors operate primarily through private-market transactions. The generic shape: (a) open-market acquisition adjacent to existing campus or in expansion corridor; (b) master-plan integration; (c) community engagement of variable scope — sometimes via community benefit agreement (CBA), sometimes neighborhood meetings without binding CBA, sometimes minimally; (d) zoning compliance, possibly requiring ZBA variances or overlay revisions; (e) tax-exempt-status maintenance subject to PA's *Hospital Utilization Project*-derived "purely public charity" criteria (engaged at D9 SD4). *Generic breakdown:* community engagement at step (c) has no statutory floor outside Pathway 2's RCO regime; CBAs are private contracts whose enforceability and monitoring depend on community-organization capacity; tax-exempt outcome at step (e) reduces the City's property-tax base while expanding the institution's land position (D9 SD4 / MC15). The pathway takes three distinctly-shaped forms in PA-3.

*Pathway 3a — Penn's continuous West Philadelphia acquisition (steady-state pattern).* (a) Acquisition: incremental, multi-decade open-market purchasing in University City core and expanding outward through Powelton Village, Cedar Park, and Spruce Hill; the steady-state form is parcel-by-parcel rather than master-plan-precipitated. (b) Master-plan integration: Penn Connects (initial 2006 plan; subsequent updates) supplies the institutional framework within which acquired parcels are developed for academic, residential, and mixed-use purposes; the master plan operates ex post to organize the acquisition pattern as much as ex ante to direct it. (c) Community engagement: Penn neighborhood compact mechanisms and the University City District BID provide channels of engagement — the Penn Alexander school catchment as an engaged-community anchor; targeted hiring and procurement programs; selective neighborhood-investment commitments — but these are institutional initiatives, not statutory floors. The PILOT/PILOET arrangement is the principal fiscal-engagement instrument and is engaged at D9 SD4 (MC15; F7-SD1-04). (d) Zoning compliance proceeds through Title 14 as-of-right where possible and through ZBA variance or overlay revision where required; documented Penn-adjacent overlay revisions and variance grants over the 2010s 2020s. (e) Tax-exempt maintenance: parcels acquired by the University proper move to tax-exempt status; parcels held by Penn-affiliated entities or for-profit developers subject to commercial transactions raise tax-exempt-status questions adjudicated at the OPA layer. *Pathway 3a breakdown:* the steady-state form has no discrete moment at which constituent input is solicited or required, because the acquisition is continuous rather than project-bound; the absence of a triggering moment is itself the structural feature, not an oversight.

*Pathway 3b — Temple's North Philadelphia expansion (contested-expansion pattern).* (a) Acquisition: Temple's footprint expansion engages the Cecil B. Moore corridor and adjacent Brewerytown, with parcel acquisition for dormitory and academic-building development; the corridor sits in a historically Black neighborhood with active anti-displacement organizing infrastructure. (b) Master-plan integration: Temple's institutional master planning has integrated parcel acquisition into expansion strategy; the 2019 athletics-stadium proposal was the highest-profile project-level instance and was defeated through coalition organizing led by Stadium Stompers and allied community organizations. (c) Community engagement is the load-bearing variable in the contested-expansion pattern: ongoing dormitory and academic-building development proceeds without a binding CBA covering the broader expansion footprint, while individual project-level community engagement is variable in scope and binding effect. (F7-SD1-05 retrieves current Temple-adjacent CBA status; T7-SD1-04 retrieves any current master-plan revision.) (d) Zoning compliance has involved variance and special-exception practice; some Temple projects engage as-of-right capacity, others require ZBA action with corresponding RCO notification. (e) Tax-exempt maintenance follows the standard pattern with PILOT-architecture engagement at D9 SD4. *Pathway 3b breakdown:* the contested-expansion form differs from 3a in that constituent-input moments do exist (project-level engagement sessions; defeated stadium proposal) but the framework's absorption of contestation is selective — defeated projects exit the pipeline; ongoing dormitory and academic-building development that does not trigger comparable mobilization proceeds without binding constraint.

*Pathway 3c — Drexel's Schuylkill Yards (master-planned redevelopment pattern).* (a) Acquisition: the Schuylkill Yards site assembled at the eastern edge of West Philadelphia, abutting Mantua and Mill Creek (historically Black neighborhoods with significant gentrification pressure) and adjacent to 30th Street Station; the site assembly differs from Pathway 3a's parcel-by-parcel pattern in that it is project-defined rather than continuous. (b) Master-plan integration: Schuylkill Yards is itself the master plan, developed in partnership with Brandywine Realty Trust; PIDC (Philadelphia Industrial Development Corporation) provides the public-development partner. (c) Community engagement involved announced affordability commitments in the project's framing, though scope, enforceability, monitoring, and current-phase compliance status are F-flagged for Phase 3 retrieval (F7-SD1-06; T7-SD1-04). (d) Zoning compliance proceeded through district-plan amendments and overlay actions for the redevelopment area as a coordinated package rather than parcel-by-parcel variance practice. (e) Tax-exempt-status questions in Pathway 3c are mixed: portions of the development are commercial and tax-paying; portions tied to Drexel directly may move to tax-exempt status. *Pathway 3c breakdown:* the master-planned form provides the most-organized community-engagement channel of the three pathways but engagement runs at the project-package level rather than the parcel level; affordability-commitment enforceability is the structural test (F7-SD1-06), and the Mantua / Mill Creek interface is the documented displacement-pressure interface adjacent to a project-package whose anti-displacement provisions are the subject of ongoing community-organization monitoring rather than of statutory enforcement.

CHOP's institutional-real-estate footprint (primarily University City and Roberts Center adjacencies) operates through hybrid commercial-and-tax-exempt arrangements engaged at D9 SD4 rather than as a distinct PA-3 expansion-pattern instance at SD1 scale.

---

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**Documented PA-3 patterns.** Track 2 reading of OpenDataPhilly zoning data identifies three structural features: (a) industrial categories (I1–I3, ICMX) concentrate adjacent to Black residential neighborhoods in North/Northwest Core and West Core, reflecting mid-20th-century stratification not revisited in the 2012 Title 14 revision; (b) high-density residential zoning near transit is relatively scarce in lower-income PA-3 corridors, constraining as-of-right capacity in corridors most pressed by displacement; (c) protective overlays concentrate disproportionately in higher-income Northwest Philadelphia (Chestnut Hill; portions of Mt. Airy; East Falls).

**North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.** Dominant SD1 features: Temple's expansion footprint (Cecil B. Moore corridor; defeated 2019 athletics-stadium proposal; ongoing dormitory and academic-building development); the PRA blight-and-redevelopment legacy of mid-20th-century clearance; the gentrification corridor from Fairmount/Art Museum northward through Brewerytown (active) toward Strawberry Mansion (anticipatory speculative acquisition); industrial-residential adjacency constraining residential capacity. Tract-level disaggregation of ZBA variances or anchor tax-exempt concentration is an F-flag.

**West Philadelphia Core.** Dominant SD1 features: Penn's continuous land-acquisition strategy (University City / Powelton Village / Cedar Park / Spruce Hill); University City District BID and Penn Alexander school catchment effects on adjacent property values; Drexel's Schuylkill Yards at the eastern edge, abutting Mantua and Mill Creek (historically Black neighborhoods under significant gentrification pressure); the Penn-adjacent zoning environment incrementally revised through ZBA variance practice and overlay adoption. Anchor engagement HIGH per substructure; anchor real-estate footprint structurally primary.

**Northwest Philadelphia.** Dominant SD1 features: dense protective-overlay coverage (Chestnut Hill historic district; portions of Mt. Airy; East Falls); a more variegated Germantown picture combining housing-stock deterioration with emerging gentrification along the Germantown Avenue corridor; limited anchor footprint. The SD1 representation question presents differently — the procedural framework operates more nearly as designed where civic infrastructure is denser, itself a documented sub-area pattern of unequal procedural-democracy capacity.

**South/Southwest Philadelphia.** Dominant SD1 features: the Point Breeze gentrification trajectory, one of the most documented contemporary Philadelphia cases of racial displacement, driven by rowhouse conversion to condominiums and commercial development along Passyunk Avenue; no major anchor campus footprint; SD1 displacement closer to a market-and-no-cause-termination pattern than an anchor-driven pattern (no-cause termination at D7 SD3).

**Limitations stated explicitly per Standard 6.** Tract-level disaggregation of ZBA variance approval rates by neighborhood; tract-level Penn / Temple / Drexel tax-exempt parcel inventory; and quantified anchor-acquisition pace by sub-area are not retrieved within this SD. Retrieval is F-flagged in §9.

---

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile A: Longtime Black homeowner in West Philadelphia Core (Cedar Park / Spruce Hill) facing Penn-adjacent acquisition pressure.** *Type:* row home held in family two-plus generations, assessed at sub-area median, no active mortgage, Penn Alexander school catchment. *Triggering event:* an unsolicited cash purchase offer well above the constituent's prior sense of value, in a market where comparable parcels have been transferring at appreciating prices to Penn-affiliated buyers (Pathway 3a steady-state pattern). *Pathway from constituent side:* the receiving side of Pathway 3a contains no constituent-side procedural step. This is the load-bearing structural feature of the profile and it warrants explicit articulation. Pathway 1 (eminent domain via blight) provides condemnees with formal rights to just compensation, Board of Viewers proceedings, and CCP appeal — the procedural framework is asymmetric in capacity but it exists. Pathway 2 (ZBA variance) provides neighborhood residents with formal RCO notification and meeting rights — the framework is conditional on RCO capacity but it exists. Pathway 3a as it operates on the homeowner side has no analogous procedural moment: there is no notification trigger, no formal hearing, no advisory body, no judicial-review pathway, no statutory framework within which the constituent's position is procedurally legible. The transaction is private; the decision is private; the framework's role on the homeowner side is reduced to recording the deed once the private decision is made. *Decision points and conditioning factors:* the sell/hold decision is conditioned by property-tax appreciation following neighborhood reassessment (city-level OPA dynamics; D9 cross-reference); deferred-maintenance costs accumulating against fixed-income or limited-income household budgets; capital-gains tax exposure on multi-decade-held property where IRC § 121 primary-residence exclusion partially shelters the gain but multi-decade appreciation typically exceeds the exclusion ceiling, leaving taxable gain (D9 interaction the profile names without resolving); absence of institutional holding-support infrastructure — no shared-equity vehicle, no legal-aid resource for evaluating offer terms, no community-land-trust footprint at meaningful scale in West Core, no statutory home-improvement-financing program adequate to the scale of deferred-maintenance backlogs in long-held properties. *Outcome:* absent holding-support infrastructure, the constituent typically sells; the household is displaced; the next-use is consistent with the anchor-adjacent acquisition pattern. The structural feature this profile exposes is not the homeowner's vulnerability — it is the absence of a procedural framework within which the homeowner has a position to occupy. The framework engages real property when government acts on it (Pathways 1 and 2); when private acquisition acts on it, the framework's role is largely to ratify the result.

**Profile B: RCO leader in North/Northwest Philadelphia Core navigating ZBA variance pipeline.** *Type:* leader of an RCO in a historically Black North Philadelphia neighborhood under emerging gentrification pressure; organization with limited paid staff, no in-house land-use counsel, membership including longtime residents and recently-arrived market-rate tenants. *Sub-area:* North/Northwest Core (e.g., Temple's Cecil B. Moore corridor or Brewerytown). *Triggering event:* RCO notice for a proposed dimensional variance and special exception on an adjacent parcel, requiring an RCO meeting within the statutory window. *Pathway:* Pathway 2. *Breakdown:* the RCO leader faces substantive zoning issues (FAR; setback; use intensity; parking) without legal counsel while the applicant has counsel; RCO position is advisory and the ZBA is not bound by it; CCP appeal is open in principle but in practice depends on funds the organization typically lacks. *Illustrative figures (Track 2):* substructure documents that ZBA outcomes reflect applicant resources and neighborhood organization; tract-level confirmation of variance grant rates by neighborhood is F7-SD1-03. *Outcome:* the variance is typically granted; RCO procedural participation is documented but not protective in substantive outcome; if part of a larger pattern, this contributes to D7-Thread A across SD1 / SD3 / SD4 / SD5 / SD7.

**Profile C: Heir-property owner with clouded title.** *Type:* a Black homeowner who inherited a row-home without a probated estate; property in predecessor's name; taxes current; constituent in continuous occupancy. *Sub-area:* North/Northwest Core or West Core (tangled-title pattern documented across both, correlated with neighborhoods of historical exclusion from formal financial and legal infrastructure). *Triggering event:* an action requiring clear title — refinance, city home-repair program, sale listing, response to a code-enforcement order, response to tax-sale notice — fails because of clouded title.

*Historical pattern (structural inference; F7-SD1-08).* Tangled title is not an aggregate of individual decisions to omit probate; it is a structural feature of property-rights infrastructure in Black neighborhoods of Philadelphia. The pattern is grounded in mid-20th-century exclusion of Black households from formal financial and legal infrastructure: redlining (HOLC mapping; D13 inheritance) restricted access to mortgage credit, formal estate planning, and the legal and financial professionals through whom property routinely transferred via probate in white middle-class households; intergenerational property transfer accordingly proceeded informally, often through verbal agreement among siblings or by long-standing occupancy without formal title transfer, even where the underlying ownership relations were stable and uncontested. The geography of tangled-title incidence in present-day PA-3 correlates with the geography of mid-20th-century redlining: clouded title is denser in historically redlined neighborhoods because the legal-infrastructure exclusion that produced informal transfer was concentrated in those neighborhoods. The historical cause is the exclusion; the present-day form is the title cloud.

*Institutional infrastructure.* Pennsylvania's heir-property law context combines standard intestate-succession provisions (20 Pa.C.S.) with the practical reality that inherited property held by multiple heirs without probate raises partition, occupancy, and consent issues that escalate with the number of heirs and the time elapsed since the predecessor's death. The legal-aid infrastructure addressing this includes Community Legal Services (CLS), whose tangled-title work has long engaged the issue, and Philadelphia VIP, whose dedicated "Tangled Title" project provides pro-bono representation specifically for clearing clouded title for owner-occupants in continuous occupancy. The infrastructure exists but is capacity-limited relative to the prevalence of the underlying condition (F7-SD1-08); demand exceeds supply, with intake and waitlist constraints documented by both organizations. The structural inference is that absent legal-aid intervention, the typical outcome is that the title remains clouded and the constituent's procedural position remains constrained.

*Time-bound failure mode.* When the triggering event is not time-bound (refinance for home improvement; sale listing initiated by the household), the tangled-title constraint is an inconvenience and barrier to opportunity but not an immediate property-loss risk; the household can pursue legal-aid intake and proceed when capacity permits. When the triggering event is time-bound, the failure mode escalates. A code-enforcement order under L&I authority requiring repair or demolition (D7 SD7) requires response within statutory timelines; failure to respond can lead to municipal demolition or sheriff's-sale conversion. A tax-sale notice triggered by accumulated delinquency (whose own occurrence in a tangled-title context is itself frequently an artifact of the title cloud — utility termination, insurance lapse, or deferred tax-relief enrollment because of the title problem) operates on the same time-bound logic. A municipal action requiring property-owner response (eminent-domain notice; municipal code-violation notice with response window) does likewise. In each case the inheritance-pathway barrier escalates from inconvenience to property-loss risk, and the legal-aid intake capacity constraint becomes load-bearing for the outcome. *Outcome:* the constituent typically cannot complete the triggering action without legal-aid intervention; in time-bound cases, the parcel faces elevated risk of forced sale or loss to code-enforcement-related demolition (cross-reference D7 SD7).

*The structural feature this profile exposes:* tangled title is legible as a structural feature of property-rights infrastructure in Black neighborhoods of PA-3, not as an individual misfortune. Formal property rights operate at a procedural threshold not uniformly accessible; substantive ownership and continuous occupancy are not the same as procedural position to act on the property. The legal-aid infrastructure partially addresses the gap but does not close it; the gap closure is conditional on capacity that does not match prevalence (G7-SD1-05).

---

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The structural shape of land-use governance in PA-3 is not fully captured by the formal architecture of the Philadelphia Zoning Code, the PA Eminent Domain Code, and the URL/PRA framework. Those instruments are real, and the procedural framework they establish — RCO notification, ZBA hearing, just-compensation rights, comprehensive plan as planning rationale — is operative. But for large parts of PA-3, particularly West Core and Temple-adjacent corridors, the most consequential land-use actor is not the city through its formal regulatory pathway; it is the anchor institution operating through private-market acquisition, master-plan integration, variable community engagement, and tax-exempt-status maintenance. Per Standard 10.B, the real-estate role is primary in this sub-domain. The regulatory framework governs the city's *public* land-use decisions while the anchor footprint reshapes the *private market* in which adjacent residents make decisions, with feedback loops between them — anchor expansion affects adjacent property values; property-value changes affect homeowner sale decisions and rental-tenant displacement (D7 SD3 and SD4); acquired parcels pass to tax-exempt status (D9 SD4 / MC15) while expanding the institution's land position.

The substructure holds open, deliberately, a question this section does not resolve: how anchor-driven displacement compares in magnitude to other displacement mechanisms operating in PA-3. The alternative mechanisms are documented and not minor. Serial eviction filers operating across multiple properties produce displacement at scale through formal court process (engaged at D7 SD3). Speculative individual landlord acquisition — small-scale investor purchasing in gentrifying corridors, often with rapid rent increases or no-cause termination at lease end — operates outside both the institutional-anchor frame and the formal-court frame (D7 SD3 and SD4). LIHTC affordability expiration converts subsidized affordable units to market-rate as compliance periods elapse (D7 SD5). Each is a structural displacement force in PA-3; their aggregate weight relative to anchor expansion is an empirical question.

The discipline this SD applies to that question is what the substructure (§6 SD1; §8 question 1) names as *hold-open-magnitude*. The discipline is distinct from the Both/And protocol applied at D7 SD4: Both/And holds open *between* two well-defined alternative explanations where each has analytical purchase; hold-open-magnitude holds open the *relative weight* of mechanisms whose individual operation is documented but whose aggregate-level comparison requires evidence not available within Track 2 reach. The development conversation's prior-pass self-assessment specifically cautioned against coherence-seeking toward an anchor-displacement narrative — the failure mode in which available evidence of anchor expansion as significant force gets read as evidence of anchor primacy among displacement mechanisms. The discipline names that failure mode and refuses it.

The relative-magnitude question is therefore restated rather than closed. G7-SD1-03 carries the question forward as held-open Track 2 empirical territory; Section 4 sub-area variation documents the structural shape of anchor footprint without claiming primacy; Profile A documents the receiving side of one anchor-pathway form without claiming it as the dominant form of West Core displacement. The SD presents anchor real-estate footprint as one structural driver among multiple, with its full structural weight engaged where it operates and without analytical assertion as to its primacy. Closing the magnitude question by analytical assertion is the substructure-flagged failure mode this SD does not commit.

The third feature this note makes visible is procedural-democracy unevenness within Philadelphia's zoning system itself. The RCO framework presumes civic infrastructure capable of engaging substantive zoning questions on roughly equal footing with applicants and their counsel. Substructure and development conversation document that this infrastructure is unevenly distributed across PA-3: in higher-income, predominantly white sub-areas of Northwest Philadelphia, RCOs function as effective development brakes; in lower-income, predominantly Black sub-areas of North/Northwest Core and parts of West Core, RCOs may be less active, under-resourced, or unrepresentative of existing residents. The framework is the same on its face; what varies is the capacity of the infrastructure that makes it operative. The representation question this raises is whether procedural-democracy mechanisms whose protective function depends on resourced civic infrastructure can be assessed without engaging the unequal distribution of that infrastructure as the structural fact it is.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G7-SD1-01: ZBA variance neighborhood/racial-outcome data gap.** [SI] MEDIUM. The procedural framework does not produce systematically published outcome data disaggregated by neighborhood income, racial composition, or applicant type; application-and-outcome data is accessible through OpenDataPhilly and L&I, but neighborhood-level analysis of grant rates is a public-record gap. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §4; Profile B. *Implication:* the structural inference of unequal procedural-democracy capacity in zoning practice cannot be confirmed at neighborhood level without this data.

**G7-SD1-02: PA *Kelo*-response gap (statutory protection vs. URL blight-condemnation pathway).** [SD] HIGH. PA's post-*Kelo* limitations (26 Pa.C.S. § 204) restrict condemnation primarily for private use but preserve carve-outs for blight-related takings; the URL framework's broad blight definition, historically applied in racially disparate ways, remains the operative pathway for redevelopment-area condemnations. *Evidence trace:* §2 Constitutional and State Statutory; §3 Pathway 1. *Implication:* the formal post-*Kelo* protection does not extend to the redevelopment-condemnation pathway that historical PRA practice principally engaged.

**G7-SD1-03: Anchor-institution displacement magnitude (HELD-OPEN).** [SI] LOW; structurally bracketed per substructure §6 SD1 and §8 question 1. Anchor real-estate footprint is documented as one structural displacement force in PA-3, particularly West Core (Penn) and North/Northwest Core (Temple, Drexel adjacency); the relative magnitude versus no-cause termination (SD3), serial eviction filing (SD3), speculative individual landlord acquisition, and LIHTC affordability expiration (SD5) is not resolved by available evidence. *Evidence trace:* §3 Pathway 3; §4; Profile A; §6. *Implication:* the SD presents anchor expansion as a documented structural force without claiming primacy over alternative mechanisms; closing the magnitude question by analytical assertion is the substructure-flagged failure mode. *Carried forward as held-open Track 2 empirical territory, not as a closed finding.*

**G7-SD1-04: RCO capacity-and-representativeness gap.** [SD] HIGH. The Title 14 RCO framework presumes civic infrastructure whose protective function depends on RCO capacity; documented sub-area distribution of that capacity is uneven, weaker in lower-income PA-3 corridors than higher-income corridors. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §3 Pathway 2; §4; Profile B. *Implication:* the procedural-democracy mechanism formally available to all neighborhoods is substantively available in proportion to civic-infrastructure resources, themselves unevenly distributed.

**G7-SD1-05: Tangled-title infrastructure gap.** [SI] MEDIUM. Legal infrastructure for clearing tangled title (probate; heirship; quiet-title) operates at a cost-and-complexity threshold that disproportionately excludes long-tenured Black households whose intergenerational property transfers occurred outside formal probate; the inheritance-pathway barrier prevents action even where occupancy and tax compliance are uninterrupted. *Evidence trace:* Profile C. *Implication:* formal property rights operate at a procedural threshold not uniformly accessible; substantive ownership is not the same as procedural position to act on it.

**G7-SD1-06: Anchor tax-exempt fiscal architecture gap (MC15 cross-domain).** [D] MEDIUM, deferred to D9 SD4. The fiscal dimension of the anchor triple role — tax-exempt parcel concentration; PILOT/PILOET arrangements; "purely public charity" framework — is structurally documented but engaged substantively at D9 SD4 (verified). *Evidence trace:* §6; cross-reference to D9 SD4 and substructure MC15. *Implication:* fiscal cost of anchor real-estate-and-tax-exempt operation is load-bearing for the SD1 narrative but quantified at D9 SD4; this SD documents the cross-reference and does not duplicate.

---

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** The federal-state-local architecture documented in §2 — Fifth Amendment Takings, the PA Eminent Domain Code (including post-*Kelo* protections at 26 Pa.C.S. § 204), the PA Urban Redevelopment Law, Philadelphia Zoning Code (Title 14) with ZBA variance framework and RCO notification regime, and the Philadelphia 2035 Plan — establishes a procedural framework: condemnees have rights to just compensation and appellate review; property owners have ZBA variance pathways with judicial appeal; communities have formal RCO voice in zoning decisions; the comprehensive plan supplies planning rationale against which decisions are evaluated.

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** The framework operates as designed in proportion to civic, financial, and legal infrastructure available to engage it. RCO participation is substantively protective where RCOs are well-resourced and broadly representative; in PA-3 sub-areas where RCO capacity is weaker, the procedural floor of notification-and-meeting is met without the substantive protection the framework presumes. Eminent-domain condemnees have formal rights but typically face represented agency adversaries without counsel; just-compensation valuations at "fair market value at time of condemnation" can lag market appreciation in gentrifying corridors. Anchor land acquisition operates outside the regulatory pathway, with no statutory community-engagement floor outside Pathway 2's RCO regime. Heir-property owners with clouded title encounter inheritance-pathway barriers that prevent action even where ownership and occupancy are uninterrupted.

**What accounts for the gap.** Three structural features. First, the framework's protective function is conditional on civic-infrastructure capacity (RCO resources; legal-aid availability; appellate access) unevenly distributed across PA-3 in a pattern correlating with the historical geography of redlining and urban-renewal displacement. Second, the URL blight-and-redevelopment pathway preserves a condemnation route post-*Kelo* through the broad statutory definition of blight, historically applied in racially disparate ways. Third — most particular to PA-3 — anchor land acquisition operates substantially outside the regulatory pathway through private-market mechanisms, master-plan integration, variable community engagement, and tax-exempt-status maintenance; the regulatory framework was not designed to govern this pathway and substantially does not. Per Standard 10.B: the real-estate role of Penn / Temple / Drexel / CHOP is primary in this sub-domain; employer role engages at D10; fiscal role engages at D9 SD4 (MC15). Relative magnitude of anchor-driven displacement against alternative mechanisms is held open per G7-SD1-03, not closed by analytical assertion.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** The legal architecture for land-use governance in PA-3 is real and operative; the gap between formal procedural availability and substantive protective outcomes is structurally traceable to design features (procedural floor without civic-infrastructure floor; URL pathway carve-outs; the framework's limited engagement with anchor-driven private-market acquisition). The infrastructure exists; the question is whether it is resourced, scoped, and designed to meet the land-use governance challenge PA-3 — half-renter, gentrifying in its margins, anchored by four major institutions whose real-estate footprint reshapes adjacent markets — presents. The representation finding this SD supplies to synthesis: the framework's protective function is conditional on infrastructure unevenly distributed, and the anchor-driven pathway operates substantially outside the framework's reach.

---
