# D7 Sub-Domain 4: Fair Housing & Anti-Displacement

**Domain:** D7 Land & Property
**Sub-Domain:** SD4 of 7
**Cycle:** M2 v1.2
**Standards:** M0 v2.1
**Draft date:** 2026-05-03

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD4 covers the fair-housing enforcement architecture and anti-displacement legal toolkit conditioning protected-class housing access and displacement-pressure response in PA-3: Fair Housing Act enforcement mechanics under 42 U.S.C. §§ 3610–3614 (HUD FHEO administrative complaint; private right of action; DOJ pattern-or-practice); the disparate-impact framework recognized in *Texas Dept. of Housing v. Inclusive Communities Project*, 576 U.S. 519 (2015) and codified in HUD's disparate-impact rule; the Affirmatively Furthering Fair Housing (AFFH) regulatory regime (2015 / 2020 / 2023 rule cycles; current status politically contingent — T-flag territory under Standard 11 / DF Thread 7); the Pennsylvania Human Relations Act source-of-income protection (PHRA, 43 P.S. § 951 et seq.; MC02 — D7 SD4 owns this cross-domain item); community benefit agreements (CBAs) as private-contract anti-displacement mechanism with documented enforceability gap; PA-3 gentrification documentation across Point Breeze, Brewerytown, Strawberry Mansion, Cedar Park / Spruce Hill, and Germantown corridors. FHA framework basics (protected classes, prohibited practices, scope) are engaged at D12 SD4 (verified) and not duplicated here. HCV mobility-of-vouchers is engaged at D12 SD4; D7 SD4 owns the source-of-income enforcement architecture conditioning that mobility. Anchor-institution real-estate footprint is engaged primarily at D7 SD1 (Pathway 3a/3b/3c); SD4 engages anchor-adjacent gentrification corridors and CBA architecture without re-establishing the SD1 pathway analysis.

**Data provenance regime (Standard 1).** Track 1 / Track 2 mixed regime, kept distinct. Track 1 legal-framework content draws on the development conversation's statutory and case citations. Track 2 quantitative content draws on documented HUD FHEO and PHRC complaint patterns, Reinvestment Fund market-typology documentation, ACS demographic-change indicators at tract level for documented gentrifying corridors, and structural inferences from documented PA-3 displacement patterns; current FHEO and PHRC complaint volumes, current AFFH regulatory status, current CBA compliance documentation, and tract-level current gentrification-pressure indicators are F-flagged for Phase 3 retrieval rather than asserted at specific values.

**Provenance scope.** PA-3-specific Track 2 claims are limited to: (a) documented gentrification patterns in named corridors (Point Breeze; Brewerytown; Strawberry Mansion as anticipatory-displacement-horizon case; Cedar Park / Spruce Hill; Germantown); (b) the documented pattern of HUD FHEO and PHRC complaint volume substantially below likely incidence as inferred from HMDA testing and research (engaged in §6 and §7); (c) documented patterns of HCV-holder source-of-income discrimination by Philadelphia landlords despite PHRA prohibition (Section 5 Profile A; G7-SD4-04). Tract-level point estimates and current-cycle complaint volumes are F-flag candidates.

---

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.** Fourteenth Amendment Equal Protection conditions disparate-impact analysis under FHA. The Supreme Court's recognition in *Texas Dept. of Housing v. Inclusive Communities Project*, 576 U.S. 519 (2015) that the Fair Housing Act encompasses disparate-impact liability is the central constitutional-statutory anchor for SD4 enforcement architecture. The *ICP* burden-shifting framework: (1) plaintiff demonstrates significant statistical disparity and identifies specific policy causing it; (2) defendant demonstrates legitimate, non-discriminatory justification; (3) plaintiff can counter with a less-discriminatory-alternative showing. *Stability:* HIGH (constitutional doctrine and statutory recognition stable). *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (HUD's disparate-impact regulation has been subject to inter-administration revision; T7-SD4-01).

**Federal Statutory Layer.** **FHA enforcement mechanics** (42 U.S.C. §§ 3610–3614) provide three enforcement routes operating in parallel. *Administrative complaint* (§ 3610): complaint to HUD FHEO within one year of alleged violation; HUD investigates; if reasonable cause is found, HUD issues Charge of Discrimination; either party may elect federal district court (DOJ prosecutes) over ALJ proceedings; ALJ can award injunctive relief, damages, and civil money penalties; federal court adds jury trial availability and broader damage potential. *Private right of action* (§ 3613): civil action in federal district court within two years of alleged violation (tolled during HUD proceedings); relief includes permanent and temporary injunction, actual and punitive damages, attorney's fees. The private right is a primary enforcement mechanism in practice because government enforcement resources are insufficient to reach more than a fraction of violations. *DOJ enforcement* (§ 3614): DOJ can bring civil actions for "pattern or practice" violations or denials of rights to a group raising an issue of general public importance. Philadelphia-area DOJ actions have included cases against landlords, lenders, and homeowners' associations. *Stability:* HIGH (statutory level). *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (HUD enforcement-priority and FHEO staffing levels are administration-contingent; T7-SD4-02).

**Federal Regulatory and Agency Layer.** **HUD FHEO** is the principal administrative enforcement agency; **DOJ Civil Rights Division Housing and Civil Enforcement Section** handles pattern-or-practice litigation; **HUD Office of General Counsel** supports the administrative-charge framework. The **AFFH regulatory regime** is the most administratively-vulnerable element of SD4 architecture (DF Thread 7 acute case; Standard 11 operative discipline per substructure §6 — Both/And explicitly does NOT apply to AFFH-contingency analysis). Regulatory history: 2015 AFFH Rule (Obama administration) required HUD program participants to conduct Assessments of Fair Housing analyzing local impediments and contributing factors, incorporating goals in Consolidated Plans, and using HUD-provided data tools — substantive analytical-and-planning obligation. 2020 rule revision (Trump administration) substantially weakened AFFH, replacing the substantive assessment with certification and largely eviscerating the analytical requirements. 2023 AFFH Rule (Biden administration) restored substantive obligations with modifications. Current status is among the most politically contested housing regulatory frameworks (T7-SD4-03 and T7-SD4-04 carry the rule-and-disparate-impact-rule status). HUD's disparate-impact rule (codifying the *ICP* framework) has been subject to parallel inter-administration revision (T7-SD4-01). *Stability:* HIGH (agencies continue). *Vulnerability:* HIGH (AFFH and disparate-impact regulatory content; FHEO enforcement posture).

*Verification update (2026-05-03): per Material Changes entry MC-01 (AFFH rule rescission). "2023 AFFH Rule (Biden administration) restored substantive obligations with modifications. Current status is among the most politically contested housing regulatory frameworks (T7-SD4-03 and T7-SD4-04 carry the rule-and-disparate-impact-rule status)" → corrected to: "The 2023 proposed AFFH rule was withdrawn by HUD on January 16, 2025 (HUD Federal Register notice of withdrawal); on March 5, 2025 HUD issued an Interim Final Rule (IFR) titled 'Restoring Affirmatively Furthering Fair Housing Definitions and Certifications' that requires HUD grantees to self-attest to fair-housing-promoting actions in connection with their Consolidated Plans, Annual Action Plans, and Public Housing Agency Plans. The IFR retains the AFFH definition and certification requirement but removes the substantive Assessment of Fair Housing (AFH) analytical-and-planning obligation that the 2015 rule required and the 2023 proposed rule would have re-established (under the 'equity plan' framing). The AFFH regulatory framework above the FHA § 3608 statutory mandate has been substantively withdrawn at the regulatory level. On April 6, 2026 HUD additionally withdrew eight FHEO guidance documents in a Federal Register notice. The IFR remains pending finalization; no successor 'equity plan' rulemaking is currently anticipated at HUD" (sources: nlihc.org "HUD Releases Weakened AFFH Interim Final Rule" March 2025; nationalfairhousing.org AFFH issue page; nlihc.org April 13 2026 FHEO guidance withdrawal coverage). The Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability finding (G7-SD4-01) is reinforced; the AFFH framework is now operatively at the certification-formality level the substructure-flagged vulnerability anticipated.*

*Verification update (2026-05-03): per Material Changes entry MC-02 (HUD disparate-impact rule proposed rescission). "HUD's disparate-impact rule (codifying the *ICP* framework) has been subject to parallel inter-administration revision (T7-SD4-01)" → corrected to: "On April 23, 2025 President Trump issued Executive Order 14281, 'Restoring Equality of Opportunity and Meritocracy,' directing federal agencies to review and revise or rescind regulations imposing disparate-impact liability. On January 14, 2026 HUD published a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (91 Fed. Reg. 1475; 'HUD's Implementation of the Fair Housing Act's Disparate Impact Standard') proposing to remove and reserve 24 CFR Part 100 Subpart G (§ 100.500) entirely — without replacement — and proposing to delete the cross-reference in § 100.5(b) tying part 100 illustrations to discriminatory-effect liability. The 30-day comment period closed February 13, 2026; final rule pending. The Supreme Court's holding in *Texas Dep't of Housing & Cmty. Affairs v. Inclusive Communities Project*, 576 U.S. 519 (2015) — that disparate-impact claims are cognizable under the FHA — remains binding precedent; rescission of HUD's regulatory burden-shifting framework eliminates the agency-level interpretive infrastructure but does not extinguish the cause of action. State fair-housing laws authorizing disparate-impact liability (including PHRA) are unaffected. The 2023 reinstatement that the substructure baseline reflected is now under proposed rescission" (sources: federalregister.gov 2026-00590; consumerfinancemonitor.com "HUD proposes to remove its Fair Housing Act disparate impact rule" January 14 2026; nlihc.org "HUD Seeks to Eliminate Disparate Impact Regulations"). Combined with MC-06 (HUD FHEO enforcement memo and guidance withdrawal), the overall SD4 regulatory-and-enforcement architecture above the statutory floor has been substantively narrowed; G7-SD4-02 calibration is reinforced in the administrative-vulnerability direction the substructure anticipated.*

*Verification update (2026-05-03): per Material Changes entry MC-06 (HUD FHEO enforcement posture). The vulnerability characterization "AFFH and disparate-impact regulatory content; FHEO enforcement posture" is operationalized between draft date and verification date by four documented administrative actions: (a) September 16, 2025: HUD FHEO Guidance Memorandum on Fair Housing Enforcement and Prioritization of Resources stating that the office will prioritize claims of intentional discrimination over disparate-impact theory claims; (b) November 25, 2025: HUD Secretary Turner letter to PHAs and assisted-housing owners on criminal screening (the "One Strike and You're Out" framing) substantially changing screening protocols; (c) December 9, 2025: DOJ final rule rescinding disparate-impact provisions in the implementing regulations for Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964; (d) April 6, 2026: HUD withdrew eight FHEO guidance documents in a Federal Register notice. The administrative-route protective function in Pathway 2 (FHEO administrative complaint) is operationally narrowed without statutory amendment; the FHA private-right-of-action under § 3613 (Pathway 1 private route) and *Inclusive Communities* disparate-impact precedent remain operative but the agency-level interpretive infrastructure that conditioned the administrative route's substantive content has been substantially withdrawn. The G7-SD4-01 administrative-route capacity-vs-likely-incidence gap is reinforced (sources: naahq.org January 28 2026 "Federal Regulatory Changes Seek to Limit Disparate Impact Liability"; nlihc.org April 13 2026 FHEO guidance withdrawal coverage; justice.gov/crt rule Title VI rescission December 9 2025).*

**State Statutory Layer.** **Pennsylvania Human Relations Act (PHRA), 43 P.S. § 951 et seq.** extends housing fair-housing protections beyond the federal FHA. Most significant for PA-3: PHRA includes **source-of-income protection** — landlords cannot refuse to rent to tenants on the basis of their payment source, including Housing Choice Vouchers. The protection is operationally critical because Philadelphia has approximately 20,000 HCV holders (D12 SD4 verified inventory), and the mobility value of those vouchers — their ability to enable holders to rent in lower-poverty, higher-opportunity neighborhoods — depends entirely on whether private landlords accept them. Landlord refusal to accept vouchers in Philadelphia's more affluent neighborhoods is documented despite PHRA's prohibition. The protection is the principal cross-domain item D7 SD4 owns relative to D12 SD4's HCV-mobility analytics (MC02). *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE (PHRC enforcement posture is annually appropriated at state level; F7-SD4-03 retrieves current protected-classes scope and enforcement posture).

**State Agency Layer.** **Pennsylvania Human Relations Commission (PHRC)** enforces PHRA housing discrimination including source-of-income; receives complaints; conducts investigations; issues findings. Capacity-relative-to-likely-incidence is engaged in §6 and G7-SD4-04. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE.

**Local Statutory Layer.** **Tenant Opportunity to Purchase Act (TOPA, Phila. Code Ch. 9-1100)** functions as fair-housing mechanism in SD4 framing — its rights-assignment feature (engaged primarily at D7 SD3 Pathway 3) enables community organizations to acquire buildings that would otherwise convert to market rate, preserving affordability in displacement-pressure neighborhoods. The anti-displacement potential is greatest where nonprofit organizations can respond quickly to NOIS notices; SD4 cross-references SD3 for the pathway mechanics and develops the fair-housing-and-anti-displacement framing. **Eviction Diversion Program and Right to Counsel** (Phila. Code Ch. 9-3900 and RTC ordinance; engaged at SD3) operate as anti-displacement instruments where they reach. **Philadelphia Office of Homeless Services / Department of Planning and Development displacement-mitigation programs** provide additional municipal architecture engaged at programmatic level. *Stability:* HIGH (ordinances). *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (annually appropriated capacity).

**Local Agency Layer.** **Philadelphia Commission on Human Relations (PCHR)** enforces local fair-housing protections complementing PHRC and HUD FHEO. **Department of Planning and Development** engages anti-displacement programs at policy level. **PHFA, PRA, PHDC** engage anti-displacement mechanics through development financing (cross-reference D7 SD5). **Community organizations** (Philadelphia Association of CDCs; CDCs operating in named gentrifying corridors; tenant unions) provide non-governmental anti-displacement infrastructure. *Stability:* MODERATE-HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH.

**Constituent Layer.** See Section 5.

**Cross-references (Standard 9 format).** D12 SD4 (Verified): FHA framework basics; HCV mobility-of-vouchers (MC02). D9 SD4 (Verified): anchor-institution PILOT and fiscal dimension of triple-role (MC15). D7 SD1: anchor real-estate footprint (Pathway 3a/3b/3c) and held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03). D7 SD3: TOPA, EDP, RTC layered anti-displacement architecture; no-cause termination as gentrification mechanism. D7 SD5: LIHTC affordability expiration as displacement mechanism; Land Bank disposition; CLT architecture. D7 SD7: code-enforcement-as-displacement-mechanism (D7-Thread B). D2 (Verified): environmental justice intersection where displacement geography correlates with environmental burden. D8 (Planned): commercial corridor displacement parallel (MC14; pending D8 cycle). D11 (Planned): Penn Alexander school catchment displacement effect. D13 (Verified): HOLC mapping and infrastructure inheritance.

---

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

Three pathways operate.

**Pathway 1: FHA enforcement (alleged violation → HUD FHEO administrative complaint OR private federal-court action OR DOJ pattern-or-practice action → investigation/litigation → relief or dismissal).** The pathway operates in three parallel-but-distinct routes. *Administrative route:* (a) alleged violation; (b) HUD FHEO complaint within one year; (c) HUD investigation; (d) reasonable-cause determination; (e) Charge of Discrimination if reasonable cause found; (f) party election of ALJ or federal district court; (g) ALJ relief (injunctive; damages; civil money penalties) or federal-court relief (with jury trial; broader damages). *Private-right route:* (a) alleged violation; (b) civil action in federal district court within two years (tolled during HUD proceedings); (c) litigation; (d) relief (injunction; damages; attorney's fees). *DOJ route:* (a) alleged pattern-or-practice violation; (b) DOJ investigation and pattern-or-practice action; (c) federal-court litigation; (d) systemic relief. *Breakdown points:* government enforcement resources are insufficient to reach more than a fraction of violations (the documented gap between FHEO/PHRC complaint volume and likely incidence inferred from HMDA testing and research; G7-SD4-04). The private right of action is a primary enforcement mechanism in practice; its protective function depends on plaintiff access to legal counsel willing to take FHA cases on contingency or with attorney's-fee shifting, a capacity-infrastructure question. Disparate-impact claims under the *ICP* framework operate through the burden-shifting structure but the burden-shift itself depends on plaintiff access to statistical-evidence development that is resource-intensive. The administrative-vulnerability of the disparate-impact rule (T7-SD4-01) and AFFH framework (T7-SD4-03) conditions the regulatory-content of the pathway in cycles where rules are weakened.

**Pathway 2: AFFH program-participant compliance (HUD funding receipt → AFFH obligation → assessment / planning / certification → HUD review → potential consequence).** Initiated when a HUD program participant (local government receiving CDBG/HOME; PHA) accepts federal funding. Steps: (a) funding receipt triggers AFFH obligation; (b) assessment / planning / certification depending on currently-operative rule (substantive analysis under 2015 / 2023 frameworks; certification-only under 2020 framework — current status is T7-SD4-03); (c) HUD review of submission; (d) potential withholding of funding for non-compliance (rarely exercised in practice); (e) program implementation against AFFH-identified goals where applicable. *Breakdown:* the protective function of AFFH depends entirely on the operative rule's substantive content and on HUD's enforcement willingness; both have been subject to substantial inter-administration variation. Per substructure §6 designation, Both/And does NOT apply to AFFH-regulatory-contingency analysis — Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability is the operative discipline because the vulnerability *is* the finding rather than a feature alongside substantive delivery. The pathway in low-rule-content cycles operates as a certification formality without analytical-or-planning obligation; in high-rule-content cycles operates as substantive obligation; the pathway's operational meaning is structurally regulation-cycle-dependent.

**Pathway 3: CBA negotiation and enforcement (institutional development project → community organizing → CBA negotiation → execution → monitoring → enforcement of breach).** Initiated when an anchor institution or major private developer announces a development project (cross-reference SD1 Pathway 3a/3b/3c for anchor-institution generic forms). Steps: (a) project announcement; (b) community organizing in affected sub-area, frequently led by CDC, tenant organization, or coalition; (c) CBA negotiation between coalition and institution/developer, often formalized through City Council, planning commission, or other municipal venue; (d) CBA execution as private contract specifying local hiring, affordable housing development, contracting set-asides, and other commitments; (e) ongoing monitoring of compliance; (f) enforcement of breach where commitments are not met. *Breakdown points (the documented enforceability gap):* CBAs are private contracts, not regulatory requirements. Their monitoring depends on community-organization capacity that does not scale with project scope or with the multi-decade implementation timeline of major developments. Penalties for non-compliance are often absent or weak in agreement language. Institutional resistance to binding commitments is documented in negotiations and in subsequent compliance posture. The gap between CBA commitments and measured outcomes is a recurring thread in Philadelphia civic documentation across Penn / West Philadelphia, Temple / North Philadelphia, and Drexel / Mantua interfaces (F7-SD4-05 retrieves current CBA status documentation per institution). Per substructure §6: Both/And does NOT apply to anchor-institution displacement analysis (this pathway's documented breakdown is a documented gap finding); the held-open discipline applies to relative-magnitude framing rather than to acknowledging the enforceability gap itself. CBAs as a class are simultaneously the principal anchor-institution-side anti-displacement mechanism *and* the principal documented enforceability gap — the analytical frame holds both without coherence-seeking toward either pole.

---

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**Documented PA-3 patterns (Track 2).** Five documented gentrification-and-displacement corridors structure the SD4 sub-area variation. *Point Breeze / Passyunk Square (South/Southwest Philadelphia):* rapid gentrification with documented displacement of long-term Black residents; new construction; rowhouse conversion to condominiums; Passyunk Avenue commercial development. One of the most documented contemporary cases of racial displacement in Philadelphia. *Brewerytown (North/Northwest Core):* North Philadelphia neighborhood bounded by Girard and Fairmount, 29th–33rd Street corridor; rapid gentrification pressure from Fairmount/Art Museum southward; documented increases in property values and rents; longtime Black residents facing displacement through both rent increases (Pathway 2 SD3 — no-cause termination) and sale pressure. *Strawberry Mansion (North/Northwest Core):* north of Brewerytown; higher poverty and vacancy rates suggesting speculative acquisition in advance of anticipated displacement wave rather than immediate active gentrification; the dynamic is *displacement-threat-horizon* rather than current acute crisis, requiring anticipatory policy response (F7-SD4-06 retrieves current gentrification indicators). *Cedar Park / Spruce Hill (West Core):* documented gentrification along and adjacent to Baltimore Avenue from 42nd to 50th Street, driven by Penn proximity and University City District expansion; the Penn Alexander school effect (documented property-value increases in the school's catchment area) operates as a specific displacement accelerant (D11 cross-reference). *Germantown (Northwest):* more variegated picture — large historically significant neighborhood with both housing-stock deterioration in some areas and emerging gentrification pressure in others, particularly along Germantown Avenue commercial corridor; institutional-density and community-diversity create different dynamics than neighborhoods with less institutional density.

**HUD FHEO and PHRC complaint volume vs. likely incidence gap.** A documented analytical pattern: FHEO and PHRC complaint volumes are substantially below the likely incidence of housing discrimination as inferred from HMDA-testing studies, paired-tester research, and HMDA disparity analysis (cross-reference D7 SD2 G7-SD2-01). The gap suggests significant under-enforcement; the pathway through which constituents might file complaints (Pathway 1 administrative or private-right route) is structurally available but practically constrained by access-to-legal-counsel infrastructure and by the documentation-and-evidence threshold the framework requires (G7-SD4-04).

**North/Northwest Core.** Dominant SD4 features: Brewerytown active gentrification corridor; Strawberry Mansion anticipatory-displacement-horizon; Temple-area sub-corridors with anchor-displacement-pressure interaction (cross-reference SD1 Pathway 3b); CBA architecture engagement around Temple (F7-SD4-05). HCV source-of-income enforcement engaged primarily on rental-side displacement defense (Profile A).

**West Core.** Dominant SD4 features: Cedar Park / Spruce Hill anchor-adjacent gentrification (Penn) with Penn Alexander school effect; Mantua / Mill Creek interface with Schuylkill Yards; CBA architecture engagement around Penn and Drexel (F7-SD4-05). The interaction of anchor-pathway (SD1 3a/3c) with SD4 enforcement architecture is structurally most acute in West Core. Penn-affiliated student-housing market produces a distinct rental-side dynamic engaged at SD3 with secondary SD4 fair-housing implications.

**Northwest Philadelphia.** Dominant SD4 features: Germantown variegated pattern; lower overall displacement pressure than Cores in stable-tenancy areas; engagement primarily on source-of-income protection where HCV holders attempt mobility into higher-opportunity zip codes.

**South/Southwest Philadelphia.** Dominant SD4 features: Point Breeze most-documented displacement corridor in PA-3; Southwest Philadelphia with continued credit-access and rental-market constraint patterns paralleling N/NW Core. SD4 enforcement architecture engages primarily through tenant-side complaints and TOPA-mediated affordability preservation.

**Limitations stated explicitly per Standard 6.** Tract-level current gentrification-pressure indicators by sub-area; current HUD FHEO and PHRC Philadelphia complaint volumes; current AFFH regulatory status; per-institution current CBA compliance documentation; and per-corridor displacement-rate vectors are not retrieved within this SD. Retrieval is F-flagged in §9.

---

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile A: HCV holder seeking voucher mobility into higher-opportunity neighborhood — encountering source-of-income discrimination.** *Type:* Black household with active HCV from PHA (D12 SD4 architecture); voucher-eligible for unit in Northwest Philadelphia or West Core sub-corridor with lower poverty rate and stronger school catchment than currently resided neighborhood; household composition includes school-age children; voucher utilization-clock active (statutory window for unit identification before voucher returns to PHA). *Sub-area:* most directly engaged in attempts to move from N/NW Core or West Core to Northwest Philadelphia or to lower-poverty West Core sub-corridors. *Triggering event:* identification of suitable rental unit; landlord inquiry about payment source. *Pathway:* Pathway 1 administrative or private-right route under PHRA source-of-income protection; cross-reference D12 SD4 voucher-mobility architecture. *Breakdown points constituent encounters:* (a) landlord refusal to accept voucher despite PHRA prohibition is documented in Philadelphia's more affluent neighborhoods; the refusal may be explicit (refusal stated in terms of payment source) or pretextual (other reasons cited); (b) PHRC complaint pathway is available but requires constituent to identify the refusal as discrimination, document it adequately, and pursue the multi-month investigation — all while the voucher utilization clock continues; (c) private right of action under PHRA requires legal counsel infrastructure and time-horizon compatible with utilization clock; (d) the cumulative effect is documented voucher-mobility constraint despite statutory prohibition (G7-SD4-04). *Cross-domain interaction:* the protective architecture exists at PHRA / PHRC level (state); the operational consequence runs through D12 SD4 voucher-mobility outcomes; the SD4 enforcement architecture and the D12 voucher administration interact at the landlord-acceptance step (MC02). *Outcome:* the constituent who encounters source-of-income refusal typically rents within current sub-area or in neighborhoods with documented landlord-acceptance pattern, foreclosing the higher-opportunity-mobility outcome the voucher was designed to enable; the structural feature is that statutory prohibition does not translate to operational acceptance in absence of enforcement infrastructure adequate to incidence.

**Profile B: Black household displaced from gentrifying corridor (Point Breeze, Brewerytown, Cedar Park / Spruce Hill).** *Type:* long-tenured Black household (15+ years in current residence) in named gentrifying corridor; renter or homeowner-with-modest-equity status; in-place social-and-institutional infrastructure (school catchment; medical provider; faith community; family network); income level constrains ability to absorb either rent increases (renter side, Pathway SD3 P2 no-cause termination at lease end) or appreciation-driven property tax increases (homeowner side, Pathway SD2 P3 refinance / SD1 P3a anchor-adjacent acquisition pressure). *Sub-area:* Point Breeze, Brewerytown, Cedar Park / Spruce Hill, Germantown corridor — the named gentrification corridors. *Triggering event:* lease-non-renewal notice (renter) or unsolicited cash purchase offer (homeowner) or property-tax-reassessment notice (homeowner). *Pathway:* the constituent's primary procedural pathways are at SD2 (foreclosure / refinance), SD3 (eviction / no-cause termination / TOPA), and SD1 (anchor-adjacent acquisition pressure). SD4's enforcement architecture engages where the displacement implicates a protected class (FHA disparate-impact framework via *ICP*) or where source-of-income discrimination conditions mobility. *Breakdown:* the *ICP* disparate-impact framework requires plaintiff to demonstrate significant statistical disparity *and* identify specific policy causing it — a threshold the constituent in any individual case typically cannot meet alone. Class-action or systemic-disparate-impact claims require statistical-evidence development infrastructure (research organizations; civil-rights legal organizations; academic partners) that has engaged some PA-3 cases but not at scale matching documented displacement incidence. *Outcome:* the constituent typically experiences displacement as a private market event without procedural-enforcement engagement; the framework's protective architecture exists at the systemic-disparity level but the constituent experiences the framework as not-engaged in the particular event; the displacement happens; the framework documents it as part of a pattern with delayed and contingent enforcement response. *The structural feature this profile exposes:* SD4's enforcement architecture engages displacement at the systemic-pattern level under disparate-impact framework; individual constituents experience displacement as private market events absent class-action or pattern-or-practice-DOJ engagement, which itself is rare relative to documented incidence.

**Profile C: Community organization negotiating or monitoring CBA with anchor institution.** *Type:* coalition of CDCs, tenant organizations, faith communities, and unaffiliated residents in anchor-adjacent neighborhood (West Philadelphia for Penn; North Philadelphia for Temple; Mantua / Mill Creek for Drexel); organizational capacity variable across coalition members; some paid staff; varying technical-infrastructure resources. *Sub-area:* West Core (Penn / Drexel); N/NW Core (Temple); cross-cutting because anchor real-estate footprint defines the territorial scope. *Triggering event:* anchor-institution announcement of major development project (master-plan revision; specific project; expansion announcement; cross-reference SD1 Pathway 3a/3b/3c). *Pathway:* Pathway 3 — CBA negotiation and enforcement. *Breakdown points:* (a) negotiation-phase capacity asymmetry between coalition (volunteer-heavy; limited paid staff; episodic engagement) and institution (in-house counsel; planning staff; lobbying capacity; multi-decade institutional memory); (b) execution-phase agreement language often lacks specific monitoring mechanisms, performance-metric definitions, or breach-consequences; (c) monitoring-phase coalition capacity does not scale with project's multi-decade timeline — coalitions formed around announced projects typically dissipate before project completion, leaving monitoring to whichever organizations persist; (d) enforcement-phase legal action against breach requires the same legal-counsel infrastructure and statistical-evidence development as Profile B's class-action pathway. *Documented PA-3 cases:* Penn / West Philadelphia, Temple / North Philadelphia, Drexel / Mantua-Mill Creek interfaces have all generated CBA-related negotiation-and-monitoring activity; current CBA status, parties, monitoring, and documented compliance gaps are F7-SD4-05. *Outcome:* CBAs typically achieve negotiated agreement at execution phase; monitoring-phase compliance varies; enforcement-phase consequences for breach are rarely realized in legal action. *Both/And:* CBAs are substantive anti-displacement instruments where they include enforceable provisions and where coalition capacity matches project timeline *and* the documented enforceability gap is real and load-bearing for the SD4 representation finding; both held without coherence-seeking toward either the toolkit-celebration pole or the gap-only pole.

---

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

Philadelphia has developed one of the more robust anti-displacement legal toolkits of any major American city operating without rent control: the Tenant Opportunity to Purchase Act, the Eviction Diversion Program, Right to Counsel, the PHRA source-of-income protection, and Fair Housing Act enforcement architecture with the *ICP* disparate-impact framework. Each instrument's design-level protective function is substantive. TOPA's rights-assignment feature enables nonprofit acquisition of buildings facing market-rate conversion. EDP produces documented filing-volume reduction for non-payment evictions. RTC produces documented improved tenant outcomes where coverage attaches. PHRA source-of-income protection establishes the legal floor for HCV mobility. The FHA disparate-impact framework provides the systemic-pattern enforcement mechanism for facially-neutral policies producing statistically disproportionate effects. *And* — the Both/And the substructure designates as primary for SD4 — the gap between legal availability and protective outcomes is itself analytically significant: nonprofit purchasing capacity for TOPA does not match NOIS volume; rental-assistance and mediator capacity for EDP is annually appropriated and cyclically constrained; RTC coverage by zip code is in phased rollout; PHRA source-of-income enforcement runs through PHRC capacity inadequate to documented incidence; FHEO and disparate-impact-framework enforcement is documented at volume substantially below likely incidence. Both load-bearing. The substructure's narrow Both/And designation reflects the analytical care required: the toolkit's substantive infrastructure is real, and the implementation gaps are real; one-sided framing in either direction distorts the analysis.

The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) is most foregrounded here in SD4 because the prior development conversation's handoff characterized Penn, Temple, and Drexel as "the most active displacement forces in the district." That characterization is a strong claim. Available evidence documents these institutions as significant displacement forces in their adjacent corridors (Pathway 3a/3b/3c at SD1; Profile B above). Whether they are quantitatively larger than serial eviction filers (G7-SD3-06), speculative individual landlord acquisition, no-cause termination at lease end (G7-SD3-01), foreclosure (D7 SD2 Pathway 2), or LIHTC affordability expiration (D7 SD5 forthcoming) is a Track 2 empirical question that available data sources can begin to answer but do not presently resolve. The discipline this SD applies parallels SD1's hold-open-magnitude protocol: anchor-institution displacement is documented and substantively engaged through the CBA pathway (Pathway 3) with documented enforceability gap; relative magnitude versus alternative mechanisms is preserved as held-open analytical territory rather than asserted as a comparative claim. Per substructure §6: Both/And does NOT apply to anchor-institution displacement analysis specifically — the discipline is hold-open-magnitude, distinct from Both/And.

The AFFH regulatory-contingency analysis warrants explicit gap-framing rather than Both/And because the administrative-vulnerability *is* the structural finding (Standard 11 operative discipline; DF Thread 7 acute case). The regulatory framework cycles through substantive-obligation (2015; 2023) and certification-only (2020) postures across administrations; the framework's protective function in any given period is conditional on the operative rule's content. This is not a both-sides phenomenon. The structural finding is the regulatory-vulnerability itself: an obligation whose substantive content varies with executive-branch political composition cannot be relied on as stable architecture. The same vulnerability characterizes the disparate-impact rule and HUD FHEO enforcement-priority posture (T7-SD4-01 and T7-SD4-02). The federal-statutory floor (FHA itself; *ICP* recognition of disparate-impact liability) is statutorily stable; the regulatory and enforcement architecture above the floor is administratively vulnerable.

*Verification update (2026-05-03): see principal anchor at SD4 §2 (Federal Regulatory and Agency Layer) for the AFFH rule rescission status (Material Changes entry MC-01), HUD disparate-impact rule proposed rescission status (Material Changes entry MC-02), and HUD FHEO enforcement-posture changes (Material Changes entry MC-06). The Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability discipline this paragraph applies has been confirmed by the regulatory developments between draft date and verification date in exactly the direction the substructure flagged: the AFFH framework above the FHA § 3608 statutory mandate has been substantively withdrawn at the regulatory level (March 2025 IFR; April 2026 FHEO guidance withdrawal); the disparate-impact rule has been proposed for rescission without replacement (January 2026 NPRM); FHEO enforcement priorities have shifted to intentional-discrimination claims (September 2025 memo). The structural finding "the architecture above the floor is administratively vulnerable" is operationalized rather than disconfirmed.*

The PA preemption of local rent control parallel from SD3 carries forward into SD4: rent-increase-driven displacement in gentrifying corridors operates without local-protective-tool engagement available because the principal protective tool is preempted at state level. Philadelphia's anti-displacement architecture operates within the constraint this preemption sets; gap framing is primary for the preemption question (per substructure §6 designation). The SD4 toolkit addresses sale events (TOPA), non-payment events (EDP), eviction proceedings (RTC), source-of-income discrimination (PHRA), and protected-class disparate impact (FHA / *ICP*) — but does not address the rent-increase event that drives Pathway 2 SD3 outcomes in gentrifying corridors. The principal driver of gentrification-displacement operates outside the SD4 enforcement reach.

D7-Thread A — the displacement architecture cutting across SD1 / SD3 / SD4 / SD5 / SD7 — is most live in SD4 because SD4's gentrification documentation provides the principal corridor-level descriptive infrastructure for the thread. The thread's mechanisms accumulate: anchor expansion (SD1 3a/3b/3c); foreclosure (SD2 P2); no-cause termination + serial filing (SD3 P2 + G7-SD3-06); now SD4's gentrification-corridor documentation and CBA-enforceability gap; SD5 will add LIHTC affordability expiration and Land Bank disposition; SD7 will add code-enforcement-as-displacement-mechanism (D7-Thread B). The synthesis-level integration is the natural place for relative-magnitude resolution if Phase 3 evidence supports it; SD4 contributes documented mechanisms without comparative-magnitude assertion.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G7-SD4-01: AFFH regulatory-contingency gap.** [SD] HIGH. The AFFH framework's substantive content cycles across administrations (2015 / 2020 / 2023 rule cycles); the framework's protective function in any given period is conditional on the operative rule. Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability is the operative discipline; Both/And does not apply. *Evidence trace:* §2 Federal Regulatory; §3 Pathway 2; §6. *Implication:* the principal program-participant fair-housing-planning architecture has structurally unstable substantive content; reliance on it as stable architecture is precluded.

**G7-SD4-02: Disparate-impact rule administrative-vulnerability.** [SD] HIGH. HUD's disparate-impact rule (codifying *ICP*) has been subject to inter-administration revision parallel to AFFH; the rule's evidentiary framework operationalizes the *ICP* burden-shifting structure but the rule's operative content varies. *Evidence trace:* §2 Constitutional / Federal Regulatory; T7-SD4-01. *Implication:* the principal regulatory operationalization of *ICP* disparate-impact liability is administratively vulnerable; the underlying statutory recognition is stable.

**G7-SD4-03: FHEO enforcement-volume vs. likely-incidence gap.** [SD] HIGH. HUD FHEO and PHRC complaint volumes are documented at levels substantially below likely incidence inferred from HMDA testing and paired-tester research. The framework's enforcement mechanism is structurally available but practically constrained by access-to-legal-counsel infrastructure and documentation-evidence thresholds. *Evidence trace:* §2 Federal Regulatory; §3 Pathway 1; §4. *Implication:* the framework's protective function operates substantively at the volume of cases where capacity infrastructure aligns; aggregate volume falls substantially below incidence.

**G7-SD4-04: PHRA source-of-income protection enforcement gap (MC02).** [SD] HIGH. Landlord refusal to accept HCVs in Philadelphia's more affluent neighborhoods is documented despite PHRA prohibition; voucher-mobility outcomes are constrained relative to design (D12 SD4 cross-reference). *Evidence trace:* §2 State Statutory; §3 Pathway 1 (PHRA route); Profile A; cross-reference D12 SD4. *Implication:* statutory prohibition does not translate to operational acceptance absent enforcement infrastructure adequate to incidence; the protection's design value is constrained by enforcement-capacity gap.

**G7-SD4-05: CBA enforceability gap (cross-reference G7-SD1-03 held-open territory).** [SD] HIGH. CBAs as private contracts with community organizations face documented enforceability constraints: monitoring depends on community-organization capacity not scaling with project timeline; agreement language often lacks specific breach-consequences; institutional resistance to binding commitments is documented; gap between commitments and outcomes recurring. The principal anchor-institution-side anti-displacement mechanism is simultaneously the principal documented enforceability gap. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local; §3 Pathway 3; Profile C; F7-SD4-05. *Implication:* the SD4 anchor-institution-engagement architecture operates through the same instrument that documents its own enforcement constraint; relative magnitude of the gap against anchor-displacement-pressure is held-open territory per G7-SD1-03.

**G7-SD4-06: Anti-displacement toolkit capacity-vs-need gap (Both/And applies).** [SD] HIGH. Philadelphia's anti-displacement toolkit (TOPA, EDP, RTC, source-of-income protection, FHA enforcement) is substantive infrastructure with documented protective effect where capacity matches need; capacity does not match documented displacement-pressure incidence in named gentrifying corridors. Both/And applies per substructure §6 primary designation. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local; §3 Pathway 1; §4; Profile A; §6. *Implication:* the toolkit's substantive design value is real and the implementation-capacity gap is real; both load-bearing for the SD4 representation finding.

**G7-SD4-07: PA preemption of local rent control gap (parallel to G7-SD3-02).** [SD] HIGH. Rent-increase-driven displacement (the principal gentrification-displacement driver per Pathway 2 SD3) operates without local-protective-tool engagement because rent control is preempted at state level. Gap framing primary; Both/And does not apply per substructure §6. *Evidence trace:* §2 State Statutory (cross-reference SD3); §6. *Implication:* the principal gentrification-displacement driver operates outside SD4 enforcement reach by state-level structural choice.

---

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** The federal-state-local architecture documented in §2 provides a substantive framework: FHA enforcement mechanics through HUD FHEO administrative complaint, private right of action in federal district court, and DOJ pattern-or-practice authority; the *ICP* disparate-impact framework with HUD's regulatory codification; the AFFH program-participant-obligation framework; the PHRA source-of-income protection enforced through PHRC; Philadelphia's TOPA, EDP, and RTC architecture (engaged at SD3, functioning as fair-housing instruments here); the CBA pathway as private-contract anti-displacement mechanism with anchor institutions and major developers. The framework is statutorily stable at the floor (FHA; *ICP* recognition; PHRA) and administratively vulnerable above the floor (AFFH; disparate-impact rule; FHEO enforcement-priority).

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** The framework operates as designed in proportion to capacity infrastructure unevenly resourced across PA-3 and the regulatory-contingency status of administratively-vulnerable elements. HCV holders attempting voucher-mobility encounter documented source-of-income refusal in more affluent neighborhoods despite PHRA prohibition (G7-SD4-04). Black households experiencing displacement in gentrifying corridors typically experience displacement as private market events without procedural-enforcement engagement; the *ICP* disparate-impact framework engages systemic patterns rather than individual cases, with class-action and DOJ pattern-or-practice infrastructure rare relative to documented incidence (G7-SD4-03). Community coalitions negotiating CBAs achieve substantive agreements at execution phase; monitoring-phase compliance varies; enforcement-phase consequences for breach are rarely realized (G7-SD4-05). The AFFH framework operates in regulation cycles where its substantive content varies (G7-SD4-01); the disparate-impact rule operates similarly (G7-SD4-02). The principal gentrification-displacement driver — rent-increase-driven displacement — operates outside the SD4 enforcement reach because of state-level rent-control preemption (G7-SD4-07).

**What accounts for the gap.** Three structural features. First, the toolkit's substantive design value is conditional on capacity infrastructure (legal-aid for FHA private right; nonprofit purchasing for TOPA; mediator and rental-assistance for EDP; PHRC enforcement capacity for source-of-income; CBA-monitoring-coalition capacity for anchor engagement) unevenly resourced across PA-3 in patterns correlating with the historical geography of redlining and exclusion the framework itself addresses. Second, the federal-statutory floor is stable but the regulatory and enforcement architecture above the floor is administratively vulnerable to inter-administration revision (AFFH; disparate-impact rule; FHEO enforcement posture) — a structural feature, not episodic disruption (G7-SD4-01; G7-SD4-02; T7-SD4-01 through T7-SD4-04). Third — most particular to PA-3 — the principal gentrification-displacement driver (rent-increase-driven displacement at lease renewal in appreciating corridors) operates outside SD4 enforcement reach because of state-level rent-control preemption; the SD4 architecture engages sale events, eviction proceedings, source-of-income discrimination, and protected-class disparate impact, but not the rent-increase event itself (G7-SD4-07). Per Standard 10.B: anchors engage SD4 through the CBA pathway and corridor-specific gentrification-pressure documentation; real-estate-actor primary role at SD1; cross-references to D9 SD4 (PILOT; MC15) and D10 (employer) carry other roles. Relative magnitude of anchor-driven displacement against alternative mechanisms is held open per G7-SD1-03, not closed by analytical assertion. Both/And applies to the anti-displacement toolkit analysis per substructure §6 primary designation — substantive infrastructure is real *and* implementation gaps are real; both load-bearing.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** The legal architecture for fair housing and anti-displacement in PA-3 is real and operative; the gap between framework availability and substantive outcomes is structurally traceable to documented design features (toolkit capacity unevenly resourced; regulatory-vulnerability above statutory floor; state-level preemption foreclosing the rent-increase pathway; CBA enforceability constraints inherent to private-contract architecture). The infrastructure exists; the question is whether it is scoped, resourced, and politically stable to address the displacement geography its architecture acknowledges and addresses. The representation finding this SD supplies to synthesis: the framework's protective function is conditional on capacity (G7-SD4-03; G7-SD4-04; G7-SD4-06); on regulatory stability (G7-SD4-01; G7-SD4-02); on cross-domain coordination with D7 SD3 (TOPA, EDP, RTC integration); and on state-level structural choices (rent-control preemption — G7-SD4-07) outside Philadelphia municipal capacity to address. The Both/And: substantive infrastructure is real, implementation gaps are real, regulatory vulnerability is real; all load-bearing. The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) carries forward through SD4 with anchor-institution displacement documented and substantively engaged through CBA pathway without comparative-magnitude assertion.

---
