# D7 Sub-Domain 5: Affordable Housing Development

**Domain:** D7 Land & Property
**Sub-Domain:** SD5 of 7
**Cycle:** M2 v1.2
**Standards:** M0 v2.1
**Draft date:** 2026-05-03

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD5 covers the affordable-housing-development legal-and-programmatic architecture in PA-3: Philadelphia Housing Development Corporation (PHDC) program portfolio (BSRP, AMP, Weatherization, RIF, RRR, Philly First Home, PHLHousing+ pilot, Turn the Key, targeted construction with DHCD); Philadelphia Redevelopment Authority (PRA) intersection with the Uniform Relocation Assistance Act (URA, 42 U.S.C. § 4601 et seq.); Philadelphia Land Bank (Phila. Code Ch. 16-500, 2013; PA Land Bank Act, Act 153 of 2012); Community Land Trusts (CLTs); construction tax abatement (post-2022 reform with affordability set-aside option); PHARE (Act 105 of 2010); LIHTC preservation at affordability expiration (the Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio approaching July 2026 deadline as exemplar case); inclusionary zoning status (limited mandatory infrastructure; voluntary Mixed-Income Housing Bonus); Mayor's Housing Opportunities Made Easy (H.O.M.E.) initiative ($2B / 30,000-unit target; $400M first-phase bond); the *Defying Displacement* legislative package (Councilmember Gauthier; expedited-permit, ZBA-affordability-conditioned-variance, ADU-by-right, density-payment-redirection ballot measure). LIHTC mechanics, HOME, CDBG, Section 8 PBA are engaged at D12 SD4 (verified) and not duplicated. Tax architecture of abatement and LIHTC equity pricing engaged at D9 (MC12). PRA / URA condemnation and urban-renewal-displacement legacy engaged at D7 SD1 and D13 (verified).

**Data provenance regime (Standard 1).** Track 1 / Track 2 mixed regime, kept distinct. Track 1 legal-framework content draws on the development conversation's statutory and program citations and on time-boxed Phase 1 search results retrieved 2026-05-03 for the H.O.M.E. initiative, *Defying Displacement* legislation, and Neighborhood Restorations LIHTC-preservation case. Track 2 quantitative content draws on documented PHDC program-output reports, Land Bank annual reports, DHCD Housing Action Plan progress documentation, National Housing Preservation Database LIHTC-expiration inventory, and structural inferences from documented PA-3 development patterns; current Land Bank disposition data, current PHARE Philadelphia-area awards, current PHDC pipeline figures, current Philadelphia CLT inventory, current abatement-with-set-aside utilization, and tract-level affordability-pressure indicators are F-flagged for Phase 3 retrieval rather than asserted at specific values.

**Provenance scope and MC18 disposition.** PA-3-specific Track 2 claims are limited to: (a) documented LIHTC-expiration concentration with approximately 100 of 450 city affordable-housing complexes set to expire over a decade per National Housing Preservation Database; (b) documented Land Bank disposition-pace gap relative to acquisition-pace and inventory; (c) documented Housing Action Plan progress (41,165 of 100,000-unit 10-year goal as of 2023); (d) documented H.O.M.E. plan structure. **MC18 (Tandem Plan) disposition:** per substructure §6 lead direction (2026-05-02), Phase 1 SD5 drafting attempted resolution within a 3-search budget across PHDC, PRA, and DHCD primary sources on 2026-05-03; "Tandem Plan" was not confirmed against primary sources within the budget. Finding documented as unresolvable; F7-SD5-MC18 carries to Phase 3 verification as confirmation-of-unresolvability item only. The "in tandem" PHA-H.O.M.E. coordination phrasing in current primary documents may have been the prior-worker reference source; this is structural inference, not confirmed identification.

---

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.** Fifth Amendment Takings Clause (engaged primarily at D7 SD1) provides the constitutional context for URA's just-compensation framework where federal funding produces displacement. Equal Protection conditions disparate-impact analysis where affordable-housing-development pathways produce protected-class-disparate outcomes (engaged primarily at D7 SD4 via *ICP*). *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* LOW.

**Federal Statutory Layer.** **Uniform Relocation Assistance and Real Property Acquisition Policies Act (URA, 42 U.S.C. § 4601 et seq.)** applies whenever federally funded projects displace residential occupants or acquire property. Requires written notice to affected persons; comparable replacement housing at comparable cost; actual moving cost reimbursement; advisory services. URA protections are procedurally complex; affected persons frequently need legal assistance to realize their full entitlements. In PA-3 — where federally funded redevelopment historically displaced Black communities without adequate relocation support (D13 cross-reference) — URA compliance is both legal obligation and politically significant commitment. **HOME Investment Partnerships Program** (42 U.S.C. § 12741 et seq.); **Community Development Block Grant (CDBG)** (42 U.S.C. § 5301 et seq.); **LIHTC** (26 U.S.C. § 42) are engaged at D12 SD4 (verified) for mechanics; D7 SD5 extends to LIHTC preservation, non-LIHTC dev tools, Land Bank, CLTs (MC02). **CHOICE Neighborhoods Initiative** funds redevelopment of HUD-assisted housing; PHA's Bartram Village Phase 1 receives a $50M Choice Neighborhood Implementation Grant. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (URA enforcement-priority; HUD funding levels; Choice Neighborhoods authorization annually appropriated).

**Federal Agency Layer.** **HUD CPD** (Community Planning and Development) administers HOME, CDBG, and ESG; **HUD Office of Public and Indian Housing** administers Choice Neighborhoods and PHA programs; **IRS** administers LIHTC at federal level (D9 ownership of tax-architecture analysis; cross-reference D9). *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* HIGH (HUD CPD enforcement-priority and funding levels are administration-contingent; T7-SD5-01 carries this).

**State Statutory Layer.** **PA Land Bank Act, Act 153 of 2012** authorizes municipal land banks to consolidate publicly-owned and tax-delinquent vacant properties for strategic disposition. **PHARE, Act 105 of 2010** establishes the Pennsylvania Housing Affordability and Rehabilitation Enhancement Fund — funded primarily through realty-transfer-tax proceeds, administered by PHFA, supplementing federal HOME and CDBG with flexible state capital for housing development, rehabilitation, and homebuyer assistance (F7-SD5-04 retrieves current funding level and Philadelphia-area awards). **PA Eminent Domain Code** (engaged primarily at D7 SD1) interfaces with URA where federal funding triggers URA in addition to state condemnation procedure. **PA UTPCPL** provides consumer-protection cause of action for some affordable-housing-development cases involving deceptive practices. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE (PHARE funding fluctuates with realty-transfer-tax revenue; F7-SD5-04).

**State Agency Layer.** **PHFA** is the LIHTC allocating agency (D9 cross-reference for tax architecture; D7 SD5 references for development-pipeline implications); administers PHARE; operates Keystone Home Loan and HEMAP (engaged at D7 SD2). **PA Department of Community and Economic Development (DCED)** administers state-level community-development programs. *Stability:* HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE.

**Local Statutory Layer.** **Philadelphia Land Bank Act (Phila. Code Ch. 16-500, 2013)** consolidates city-owned and tax-delinquent vacant properties in a single entity with a mandate for strategic disposition; statutory mandate emphasizes affordable housing and community development. **Construction tax abatement (post-2022 reform):** Philadelphia's 10-year tax abatement for new residential construction and major rehabilitation was reformed by City Council in 2022 to phase in the full assessment over 10 years (10% of assessment taxed in Year 1, increasing 10% per year for new construction); rehabilitation abatement maintained; **affordability set-aside option** added for developers incorporating affordable units (F7-SD5-05 retrieves utilization data). **Mixed-Income Housing Bonus** (voluntary density-bonus program) enables developers to increase project density beyond base zoning by either building price-restricted affordable units or making payment to Housing Trust Fund "in lieu"; documented underutilization with payment-in-lieu mechanism producing redirected revenue (~$36M for 2019–2023; partial redirect to HTF; ballot-measure addressing remainder allocation). **Housing Action Plan (DHCD, 2018)** establishes the 100,000-unit / 10-year city-level target; ~41,165 created or preserved by 2023 reporting; provides citywide architecture within which subsequent programmatic decisions operate. **H.O.M.E. (Housing Opportunities Made Easy) initiative (2024 authorizing legislation; first $400M bond issuance early 2026)** establishes a $2B plan to create or preserve 30,000 housing units; first annual budget ~$277M for ~30 programs; serves households up to 120% AMI; first phase preservation budget $46.1M. **Defying Displacement legislative package (2025; Bill 250043 and related ordinances)** compels L&I to expedited-review (5-business-day) affordable-housing zoning permits; empowers ZBA to condition variance approvals on developer affordable-housing inclusion; expands ADU by-right in select council districts; ballot-measure mechanism for density-payment redirection to HTF. *Stability:* MODERATE-HIGH (post-2022 abatement and post-2024 H.O.M.E. recent enactments). *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH (annual appropriations; H.O.M.E. multi-year bond financing schedule; F7-SD5-06).

*Verification update (2026-05-03): per Material Changes entry MC-08 (H.O.M.E. plan implementation milestones). The "H.O.M.E. (Housing Opportunities Made Easy) initiative (2024 authorizing legislation; first $400M bond issuance early 2026)" framing → updated to the documented Year 1 implementation sequence: (a) March 24, 2025: Mayor Cherelle Parker formally unveiled the plan in special City Council session, with the $2 billion structure (30,000 units = 13,500 new construction + 16,500 preservation; $800M in city bonds in two $400M tranches; $1B in publicly owned land contributions; supplementary federal HOME/CDBG/HOPWA/ESG, City General Fund, Housing Trust Fund, philanthropic and private streams). (b) June 2025: City Council approved initial bond authorization. (c) Fall 2025: Council passed an amended budget resolution prioritizing low-income households (≤30% AMI emphasis) over the administration's broader workforce-housing scope (up to 100% AMI for some programs), requiring redo of the bond ordinance. (d) January 22, 2026: Council unanimously reapproved the amended bond authorization in the first session day of the year, with Mayor Parker signing the bill into law the same afternoon. (e) Late March / early April 2026: First $400 million tranche issued (the preceding finance-director schedule had projected late March). (f) Year 1 budget allocation: $277 million across 30+ programs, including significant allocations to Basic Systems Repair Program (eligibility expanded to 100% AMI in some streams; the program provides free home repairs for low-income homeowners — electrical, plumbing, heating, roofing, structural), Turn the Key (first-time-homebuyer program using city-owned land for workforce housing, with Mayor Parker's stated intent to put the program "on steroids"), affordable-housing preservation gap-funding, eviction prevention, and homelessness prevention. (g) Adjacent legislative vehicles in early 2026: streamlined-disposition legislation for ~1,000 city-owned parcels (passed January 2026); Brith Sholom House senior-housing redevelopment partnership (announced January 2026 with PHA and building trades unions); modular-housing manufacturing facility plan for the Logan Triangle (RFI issued January 2026 with March deadline). The G7-SD5-06 H.O.M.E. plan implementation gap is partially refined: Year 1 implementation has begun on a defined schedule with bond issuance executed; the plan has progressed from authorizing legislation to active deployment; equity-of-distribution-across-PA-3-sub-areas remains the principal forward-looking analytical question (F7-SD5-06 retrieval candidate as Year 1 program outputs accumulate). The Both/And designation holds: substantive infrastructure is now operationally active, *and* the implementation-gap risk at year-by-year program execution remains real. (Sources: phila.gov March 25, 2025 Parker administration H.O.M.E. announcement; whyy.org H.O.M.E. plan coverage June 12, 2025 / December 12, 2025 / January 23, 2026 / March 30, 2026; inquirer.com January 22, 2026 bond reapproval; nlc.org December 4, 2025 Brooks article; cityandstatepa.com modular-housing announcement.)*

**Local Agency Layer.** **DHCD** (Division of Housing and Community Development) administers HOME, CDBG, ESG, the Housing Trust Fund, and oversees affordable-housing pipeline RFP/RFQ processes. **PHDC** (operationally integrated with PRA and Philadelphia Land Bank since 2019; staffed by PHDC employees) administers BSRP, AMP, Weatherization, RIF, RRR, Philly First Home, PHLHousing+ (cash-rental-assistance pilot 2022–2026), Turn the Key (first-time-homeowner construction); the integrated PHDC-PRA-PLB structure is operationally significant because the three legally-distinct entities share staff while retaining independent boards. **PRA** functions as full-service lender, parcel assembler, and project finance underwriter; stewards approximately 30% of city's land inventory. **Philadelphia Land Bank** holds inventory and conducts strategic disposition. **PHA** (independent agency; cross-reference D12 SD4) operates parallel $6.3B / 20,000-unit plan coordinating with H.O.M.E. **CDCs, CLTs, LISC Philadelphia, Reinvestment Fund, Philadelphia Accelerator Fund** comprise the non-governmental development-and-finance infrastructure. *Stability:* MODERATE-HIGH. *Vulnerability:* MODERATE-HIGH.

**Constituent Layer.** See Section 5.

**Cross-references (Standard 9 format).** D12 SD4 (Verified): LIHTC, HOME, CDBG, Section 8 PBA mechanics; HCV (MC02). D9 (Verified): construction tax abatement tax architecture; LIHTC equity pricing (MC12). D13 (Verified): PRA / urban-renewal displacement legacy. D7 SD1: PRA condemnation pathway and anchor-adjacent redevelopment; held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03). D7 SD2: PHFA Keystone Home Loan and Keystone Advantage interaction with first-time-homebuyer pathway. D7 SD3: TOPA assignment-to-nonprofit feature operationally interacts with CLT acquisition pathway. D7 SD4: AFFH disparate-impact framework for siting analysis; CBA pathway interaction with anchor-adjacent affordable-development. D7 SD7: PHDC BSRP and AMP interface with code-enforcement-deferred-maintenance pathway.

---

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

Three pathways operate.

**Pathway 1: PHDC homeowner-preservation (eligibility → application → assessment → repair / modification completion).** Initiated when a low-income owner-occupant homeowner experiences a major-system failure (BSRP — electrical, plumbing, heating, structural, roofing emergency) or accessibility barrier (AMP — disability-related modification need) or weatherization deficiency. Steps: (a) eligibility determination (income-qualification; owner-occupancy; tax-and-water-bill-current status; structural soundness for AMP); (b) application submission via PHDC; (c) assessment and scope-of-work development by PHDC contractors; (d) repair/modification completion; (e) outcome — preserved homeownership, prevented-displacement, accessibility-modification. *Breakdown points:* the eligibility requirement that taxes and water bills be current excludes the constituents with the most acute deferred-maintenance need where deferred maintenance and tax/water arrears are correlated outcomes of constrained household budget; intake capacity does not match documented demand (BSRP had $20.16M in completed properties year-to-date 2025 with $20.58M in construction; AMP $2.35M completed with $4.62M in construction — substantial throughput, but waitlist and intake-capacity questions are F-flagged at F7-SD5-01); subcontractor capacity and quality conditions outcome — RRP Rule contractor certification interacts (D7 SD7 / D10 cross-reference). The pathway is operationally well-developed (PHDC's most-mature program portfolio); the structural feature is that demand exceeds capacity at scale matching documented PA-3 deferred-maintenance backlog.

**Pathway 2: Land Bank strategic disposition (acquisition → strategic-disposition decision → RFP/RFQ → award → development → affordability outcome).** Initiated when the Philadelphia Land Bank acquires a parcel through tax-delinquency conveyance, city-owned-property transfer, or other consolidation routes. Steps: (a) acquisition or inventory consolidation; (b) strategic-disposition determination (affordable housing; community development; market-rate disposition; hold-for-future-need); (c) RFP/RFQ issuance for developer/CDC/CLT applications; (d) award decision by Land Bank board (with City Council practical involvement in council-district-specific dispositions); (e) development phase by awarded entity; (f) affordability outcome (deed-restriction; CLT ground lease; LIHTC compliance period; market-rate). *Breakdown points (Both/And applies per substructure §6 designation — Land Bank is the most acute Both/And case):* the statutory mandate for strategic affordable-housing-prioritized disposition is real, *and* the documented disposition-pace gap relative to inventory and acquisition-pace is real (annual reports document the gap; F7-SD5-02). The political-interference dimension at council-district-specific decisions is documented in Philadelphia journalism. CDC and CLT capacity to respond to RFPs depends on technical-and-financial infrastructure unevenly distributed. Disposition to for-profit developers without affordability conditions occurs alongside affordable-priority disposition; the statutory mandate does not impose an exclusive affordable-disposition rule. The throughput question — Land Bank disposition pace below what would be needed to materially address vacancy at scale — is an aggregate-level structural finding parallel to the Land Bank's individual-disposition-decision representation question (whose interests are represented in disposition decisions).

**Pathway 3: LIHTC preservation at affordability expiration (project approaching extended-use-period end → preservation-strategy decision → priority-bid / refinancing / conversion).** Initiated when a LIHTC-financed project approaches the end of its 30-year extended-use period (15-year compliance period plus 15-year extended use). Steps: (a) approach to extended-use-period end with notice to tenants and to the city; (b) owner decision among preservation refinancing (re-syndicating with LIHTC equity for new compliance period), market-rate conversion, sale to mission-aligned preservation buyer, or sale to general market; (c) priority-bid window in some statutory/agreement contexts (Pennsylvania does not have a statutory state-of-PA-wide LIHTC preservation right of first refusal, but specific projects may carry agreement-based priority-bid rights); (d) tenant-side response — TOPA may apply where building structure meets Phila. Code Ch. 9-1100 coverage (D7 SD3 cross-reference); rent stabilization unavailable (PA preemption per D7 SD3); (e) outcome — preservation, conversion, or sale. *Breakdown points (gap framing primary per substructure §6):* approximately 100 of 450 LIHTC-financed affordable-housing complexes in Philadelphia are set to expire over the next decade per National Housing Preservation Database (Track 2 documented). The Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio approaching a July 2026 deadline is the exemplar PA-3 case: a private developer's portfolio of subsidized rental units across more than a dozen neighborhoods including gentrifying areas; the developer offered the portfolio to the city for preservation but the practical capacity to assemble the priority-bid package within the statutory window is the active question; H.O.M.E. first-phase preservation budget ($46.1M) is a substantive resource against the documented gap, but the gap (~12% of subsidized units potentially affordability-loss-vulnerable over a decade) exceeds any single-cycle preservation-budget capacity. The pathway breakdown is structural: federal LIHTC's 30-year design horizon was set in 1986; the affordability consequences of that horizon arrive cyclically as projects mature, with the cycle now active in PA-3 (G7-SD5-02; cross-reference G7-SD1-03 held-open displacement-magnitude as one mechanism in the alternative-mechanism set).

---

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**Documented PA-3 patterns (Track 2).** Four structural features ground the SD5 sub-area variation. (a) Land Bank inventory is concentrated in high-displacement-pressure neighborhoods (North/Northwest Core; portions of West Core; Southwest Philadelphia) — the geography of historical disinvestment is the geography of present-day public-land inventory; (b) LIHTC affordability-expiration concentration follows Philadelphia's 1990s LIHTC development cycle, with units concentrating in named gentrifying corridors (D7 SD4 cross-reference) — the temporal cycle of LIHTC and the spatial cycle of gentrification are now intersecting; (c) PHDC homeowner-preservation programs (BSRP, AMP, Weatherization) operate citywide with concentration in lower-income sub-areas reflecting deferred-maintenance correlation with constrained household budget; tract-level utilization is F-flagged (F7-SD5-01); (d) construction tax abatement post-2022 reform — the affordability set-aside option is the principal new development tool that conditions abatement availability on affordability inclusion; utilization data is F-flagged (F7-SD5-05).

**North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.** Dominant SD5 features: substantial Land Bank inventory; LIHTC-expiration pressure in several documented projects; Brewerytown / Strawberry Mansion gentrification interfacing with affordable-housing-development decisions; Temple-area CBA architecture (D7 SD4 cross-reference). H.O.M.E. preservation funding most directly relevant to N/NW Core LIHTC-expiration cases. Defying Displacement legislation expedited-permit and ZBA-affordability-conditioning provisions operationally tested in N/NW Core development pipeline.

**West Philadelphia Core.** Dominant SD5 features: University City Townhomes case (formerly subsidized; multi-year displacement contestation with ~70 families displaced; resolved 2023–2024) as exemplar of LIHTC/Section 8 expiration interfacing with anchor-adjacent gentrification; Penn-adjacent CBA architecture (D7 SD4); Schuylkill Yards CBA commitments (cross-reference SD1 Pathway 3c) including affordability components whose enforceability is engaged at SD4. Mantua and Mill Creek with continued Land Bank inventory and CDC/CLT presence.

**Northwest Philadelphia.** Dominant SD5 features: relatively lower Land Bank inventory than Cores; some LIHTC stock with expiration consideration; Germantown corridor with documented gentrification pressure raising affordability-preservation question; relatively lower H.O.M.E.-Year-1 program-portfolio engagement than Cores in projected resource flow.

**South/Southwest Philadelphia.** Dominant SD5 features: Point Breeze gentrification interfacing with affordable-housing-development decisions; Southwest Philadelphia with LIHTC-expiration concentration; Land Bank inventory concentration in Southwest tracts. The Neighborhood Restorations portfolio includes several South/Southwest properties; the July 2026 deadline operationally tests the H.O.M.E. preservation architecture.

**Limitations stated explicitly per Standard 6.** Tract-level current Land Bank inventory and disposition data; current LIHTC-expiration timeline by project; current PHDC program uptake disaggregated by neighborhood and demographics; current PHARE Philadelphia-area awards; current abatement-with-set-aside utilization; current CLT inventory and acquisition pipeline are not retrieved within this SD beyond aggregate-level documented patterns. Retrieval is F-flagged in §9.

---

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile A: Low-income Black homeowner accessing PHDC homeowner-preservation infrastructure.** *Type:* long-tenured Black homeowner (15+ years' occupancy) in N/NW Core or West Core neighborhood; row home held with tangled-title status uncommon (or, where present, cross-reference D7 SD1 G7-SD1-05 / D7 SD2 G7-SD2-05); income at or below 80% AMI; major-system failure (roof leak; electrical hazard; heating-system failure) or disability-related accessibility need; taxes and water bill current. *Sub-area:* most directly engaged in N/NW Core and West Core. *Triggering event:* major-system failure or disability-related modification need. *Pathway:* Pathway 1. *Breakdown points constituent encounters:* (a) eligibility determination — taxes-and-water-current requirement excludes the constituents with the most acute correlated deferred-maintenance and bill-arrears burden; (b) application capacity — PHDC documented program-output is substantial (>$20M BSRP year-to-date 2025 plus matching pipeline) but waitlist documentation and intake-to-completion timelines are F-flagged; (c) scope-of-work — PHDC programs address emergencies (per BSRP definition: "leaking or broken sewer lines"; "dangerous electrical conditions"; "roof leaks which have caused a 4 sq. ft. or larger section of ceiling to collapse"); deferred-maintenance below emergency threshold falls outside; (d) cross-domain interaction with code enforcement (D7 SD7) — L&I orders may trigger application; code-enforcement-deferred-maintenance pathway (D7-Thread B) interacts. *Outcome:* the constituent who meets eligibility and reaches completion typically achieves preservation of homeownership; the constituent in tax/water-arrears or deferred-maintenance-below-emergency-threshold status falls outside framework reach. *The Both/And:* PHDC's homeowner-preservation infrastructure is substantive (program output documented at scale) *and* the framework's reach into the constituents with the most acute correlated need is constrained by eligibility-design choices; both load-bearing.

**Profile B: CDC pursuing Land Bank disposition for affordable-housing development.** *Type:* community development corporation operating in N/NW Core or West Core neighborhood; mission-aligned with affordable-housing development; technical-financial capacity adequate for small-to-medium-scale development (single-property or small-portfolio); existing relationship with DHCD, PHDC, LISC Philadelphia, and Reinvestment Fund or Philadelphia Accelerator Fund. *Sub-area:* most directly engaged in Land Bank inventory concentration sub-areas (N/NW Core; portions of West Core; Southwest Philadelphia). *Triggering event:* Land Bank RFP/RFQ issuance for parcels in CDC's service area, or proactive identification of Land Bank inventory parcels suitable for affordable-housing development. *Pathway:* Pathway 2. *Breakdown points:* (a) RFP-response capacity — preparing a competitive RFP response requires development-finance underwriting, architectural-and-engineering feasibility analysis, and project-pro-forma development that smaller CDCs may not have in-house; LISC Philadelphia, Reinvestment Fund, and Philadelphia Accelerator Fund provide capacity-building infrastructure but coverage is partial; (b) award process — Land Bank board decisions interface with City Council in council-district-specific dispositions; political-relationship-dependent variation in award outcomes is documented; (c) financing — affordable-housing-development financing requires assembly of LIHTC (D9 / D12 SD4 cross-references), HOME, CDBG, PHARE, possibly Accelerator Fund, possibly H.O.M.E. funding (post-2024); financing-stack assembly is the principal capacity bottleneck for smaller CDCs; (d) construction and lease-up; (e) ongoing-affordability compliance. *Both/And:* the Land Bank's statutory mandate authorizing community-development-prioritized disposition is real *and* the disposition-pace-relative-to-inventory gap is real *and* the small-CDC-capacity constraint conditions which entities can respond to RFPs at scale; all three load-bearing per substructure §6 most-acute Both/And designation. *Outcome:* the well-resourced CDC with capacity-building support typically achieves successful Land Bank-sourced development at modest scale; the smaller CDC without capacity-building support may not respond to RFPs at all, structurally privileging larger or for-profit developers in the disposition pipeline.

**Profile C: Tenant in LIHTC-expiring affordable-housing property facing affordability loss.** *Type:* low-to-moderate-income tenant household in LIHTC-financed affordable rental property approaching the end of its 30-year extended-use period; multi-year tenure (5–15 years); rent at LIHTC-restricted level substantially below market rate; school-age children; in-place social-and-institutional infrastructure tied to current residence. *Sub-area:* most directly engaged where LIHTC-expiration concentration intersects gentrification pressure — N/NW Core, parts of West Core (the Neighborhood Restorations portfolio includes properties in West Philadelphia among other neighborhoods). *Triggering event:* notice from owner of approaching extended-use-period end and pending preservation-or-conversion decision. *Pathway:* Pathway 3. *Breakdown points constituent encounters:* (a) notice-receipt and intelligibility — the technical complexity of LIHTC extended-use-period mechanics is substantial; tenant-side organizing infrastructure (Tenants Union; West Philadelphia tenant-organizing networks active in the Neighborhood Restorations case) provides interpretation capacity but coverage is partial; (b) preservation-side outcome — depends on owner decision (sale to mission-aligned buyer; re-syndication with new LIHTC equity; conversion) and on city's priority-bid capacity; H.O.M.E. preservation funding is the principal new resource against this gap; the Neighborhood Restorations July 2026 deadline operationally tests the architecture; (c) conversion-side outcome — if owner converts to market rate, tenants face rent increase the household typically cannot meet (rent-control unavailable per PA preemption — D7 SD3 cross-reference); (d) TOPA interaction — Phila. Code Ch. 9-1100 may apply for buildings within the 2-15-unit coverage band, providing tenant right of first refusal and assignment-to-nonprofit feature (D7 SD3 cross-reference) — this is the direct interaction of the SD3 protective architecture with the SD5 development pathway. *Outcome:* the tenant in a property where preservation is achieved (mission-aligned buyer; re-syndication; H.O.M.E.-supported priority bid) typically retains affordable tenancy; the tenant in a property where conversion occurs faces displacement under the same no-cause-equivalent dynamic engaged at D7 SD3 Pathway 2. *Cross-reference held-open territory:* LIHTC-affordability-expiration is one of the documented displacement mechanisms in PA-3 named in the held-open G7-SD1-03 magnitude question; SD5 documents the mechanism without analytical assertion of its primacy versus alternative mechanisms.

---

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The structural shape of affordable-housing development in PA-3 takes three distinct analytical forms across the SD5 architecture, each warranting its own analytical treatment per the substructure's narrow Both/And designation. The Philadelphia Land Bank, PHDC, and PHARE constitute the principal Both/And triple — substantive infrastructure delivering documented output *and* documented capacity-relative-to-need gaps. Land Bank annual reports document the gap between acquisition pace and disposition throughput; the statutory mandate for strategic affordable-housing-prioritized disposition is real and the disposition-pace gap relative to inventory is also real (G7-SD5-02). PHDC's program portfolio (BSRP, AMP, Weatherization, RIF, RRR, Philly First Home, PHLHousing+, Turn the Key) operates at substantial scale — 2025 year-to-date BSRP completion exceeding $20M with matching pipeline — and the framework's reach into the constituents with the most acute deferred-maintenance need is constrained by eligibility-design choices (taxes-and-water-current requirement; emergency-threshold scope of work). PHARE supplements federal HOME and CDBG with flexible state capital subject to realty-transfer-tax revenue fluctuation; current funding levels and Philadelphia-area awards are F-flagged. The Both/And applies most acutely to Land Bank because the statutory-mandate-vs-disposition-pace gap is the structural finding rather than a feature alongside it.

Construction tax abatement, inclusionary zoning, and LIHTC preservation each warrant gap-framing rather than Both/And per substructure §6 designation. Construction tax abatement subsidized luxury and market-rate development at the cost of school-district revenue until the 2022 reform; the reform phased in full assessment over 10 years (10% taxed Year 1, increasing 10% per year); the affordability set-aside option is recent and underutilized (F7-SD5-05). The gap framing is primary because the abatement's structural function (subsidizing development that would have occurred without the subsidy) is the structural finding. Inclusionary zoning is engaged through Philadelphia's voluntary Mixed-Income Housing Bonus rather than mandatory IZ; documented underutilization of the affordable-construction option in favor of payment-in-lieu (~$36M for 2019–2023; partial redirect to Housing Trust Fund; ballot-measure for full redirect); state-preemption concerns have constrained mandatory IZ ordinance development. The gap framing is primary because the absence of the policy is the finding. LIHTC preservation operates against the 30-year design horizon set in 1986; approximately 100 of 450 city affordable-housing complexes are set to expire over the next decade per National Housing Preservation Database; the Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio with the July 2026 deadline is the exemplar PA-3 case; the gap framing is primary because units approaching expiration without active preservation investment is the structural finding rather than a feature alongside substantive preservation infrastructure.

The H.O.M.E. (Housing Opportunities Made Easy) initiative is the principal new architectural development in the SD5 framework since the prior development conversation. Mayor Parker's $2B plan targets 30,000 units across creation and preservation; first $400M bond issuance early 2026 funds Year 1 of the multi-year program; first annual budget allocates ~$277M across ~30 programs (~$46.1M dedicated to affordable-housing preservation); program eligibility extends to households up to 120% AMI. The plan consolidates existing programs (BSRP; Turn the Key; PHLHousing+; PHARE-supplementation) under the H.O.M.E. umbrella plus new program development. The plan operates in tandem with PHA's parallel $6.3B / 20,000-unit plan (PHA modernizing/redeveloping ~13,000 conventional public housing units; building/acquiring ~7,000 more). The plan's implementation gap — whether $2B funding plus PHA's $6.3B aggregate are scoped to the documented 64,500-unit extremely-low-income deficit; whether the 30,000-unit target is substantively achievable; whether the equity-of-distribution across PA-3 sub-areas matches displacement-pressure geography — is the open question this SD documents without resolving (G7-SD5-06). The Tandem Plan reference from the prior development conversation could not be confirmed within the Phase 1 search budget; possibly an artifact of the "in tandem" PHA-H.O.M.E. coordination phrasing now visible in primary documents (MC18; F7-SD5-MC18).

The *Defying Displacement* legislative package (Councilmember Gauthier; 2025) is a parallel architectural development. Bill 250043 compels L&I expedited review (5-business-day) of affordable-housing zoning permits. A companion bill empowers ZBA to condition variance approvals on developer affordable-housing inclusion when the request is initiated by applicant or Coordinating RCO with documented community-meeting discussion. ADU-by-right expansion in select council districts (1st, 2nd, 3rd, 7th — covering parts of Center City, South Philadelphia, West Philadelphia, North Philadelphia) is among the package. A May 2025 ballot measure addresses Mixed-Income Housing Bonus density-payment redirection to Housing Trust Fund. The package is operationally novel; its impact on affordable-housing-development volume is an empirical question the SD does not resolve (F7-SD5-08).

The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) carries forward through SD5. LIHTC affordability expiration is documented as a displacement mechanism in PA-3 (the 100-of-450 expiration concentration; the Neighborhood Restorations exemplar case) without analytical assertion of its primacy versus anchor-driven acquisition (SD1), foreclosure (SD2), no-cause termination and serial filing (SD3), or the gentrification-corridor mechanism cluster (SD4). Land Bank disposition decisions are similarly part of the held-open territory: the disposition-pace-vs-inventory gap operates as a structural feature of PA-3 affordable-housing supply without comparative-magnitude assertion against alternative displacement mechanisms.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G7-SD5-01: Land Bank disposition-pace vs. inventory gap (Both/And applies; substructure §6 most-acute case).** [SD] HIGH. The Philadelphia Land Bank's statutory mandate for strategic affordable-housing-prioritized disposition is real; the disposition pace relative to inventory and acquisition pace is documented at scale below what would be needed to materially address vacancy. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §3 Pathway 2; §6; Profile B. *Implication:* the principal public-land-disposition vehicle for affordable-housing-development operates substantively in individual cases while the throughput aggregate falls short of need scale; both findings load-bearing.

**G7-SD5-02: LIHTC preservation gap (gap framing primary).** [SD] HIGH. Approximately 100 of 450 Philadelphia affordable-housing complexes (~22%) are set to expire over the next decade per National Housing Preservation Database; the Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio with July 2026 deadline is the active exemplar; H.O.M.E. first-phase preservation budget ($46.1M) is substantive but below decade-aggregate need. *Evidence trace:* §3 Pathway 3; §4; §6; Profile C; cross-reference G7-SD1-03 held-open territory. *Implication:* a documented decade-scale displacement-pressure pipeline operates against a preservation-infrastructure capacity that is improving (H.O.M.E.) but documented below decade-scale need; the SD documents the mechanism without comparative-magnitude assertion.

**G7-SD5-03: Inclusionary zoning gap (gap framing primary).** [SD] HIGH. Philadelphia operates the voluntary Mixed-Income Housing Bonus rather than mandatory IZ; documented underutilization with payment-in-lieu mechanism producing density payments that have only partly flowed to Housing Trust Fund (general-fund retention of remainder; ballot-measure addressing); state-preemption concerns have constrained mandatory IZ ordinance development. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §6. *Implication:* the principal market-driven affordable-housing-creation tool available to localities operates voluntarily in PA-3, with documented developer preference for payment-in-lieu over affordable construction.

**G7-SD5-04: Construction tax abatement post-2022 reform engagement gap (gap framing primary).** [SD] MEDIUM. The 2022 reform phased in full assessment over 10 years for new construction; affordability set-aside option is recent and underutilized (F7-SD5-05). The reform's net effect on affordable-housing creation depends on set-aside utilization rate. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §6. *Implication:* the abatement framework's affordability function is conditional on set-aside-option uptake; uptake data is F-flagged.

**G7-SD5-05: PHARE / PHDC capacity-vs-need gap (Both/And applies).** [SI] MEDIUM. PHARE and PHDC operate substantive program portfolios at documented scale; capacity relative to documented need (60,000+ extremely-low-income unit deficit per Penn analysis) does not match. *Evidence trace:* §2 State Statutory and Local Agency; §3 Pathway 1; §6; Profile A. *Implication:* substantive infrastructure is real; aggregate need exceeds capacity by structural margin.

**G7-SD5-06: H.O.M.E. plan implementation-and-equity-of-distribution gap.** [SI] MEDIUM. The $2B / 30,000-unit plan structure, $400M first-phase bond, and ~$277M Year 1 budget across ~30 programs are documented; the substantive achievement of unit targets and the equity of distribution across PA-3 sub-areas relative to displacement-pressure geography is an open implementation question. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §6; F7-SD5-06. *Implication:* the framework's adequacy at implementation phase is the principal forward-looking analytical question; the question is open rather than closed at draft date.

**G7-SD5-07: CLT inventory and capacity gap.** [SI] MEDIUM. Philadelphia has several active CLT organizations and CLT-adjacent programs; the ecosystem is not fully catalogued in a single public source; permanent-affordability-via-ground-lease structure is the principal alternative to time-limited LIHTC affordability — engaged most directly at the LIHTC preservation interface (Pathway 3). *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Agency; §6; cross-reference G7-SD5-02. *Implication:* the structural alternative to LIHTC's 30-year horizon (CLT permanent affordability) operates in PA-3 at modest scale; scaling capacity is a documented question.

---

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** The federal-state-local architecture documented in §2 provides a substantive framework: URA establishes federal-funding-displacement protections; HOME, CDBG, LIHTC, and Choice Neighborhoods provide federal development financing (engaged at D12 SD4 mechanics); the PA Land Bank Act enables municipal land banks for strategic disposition; PHARE provides flexible state capital; the Philadelphia Land Bank, PHDC (with its full program portfolio), PRA, and DHCD constitute the local development-and-preservation infrastructure; the Housing Action Plan (2018) establishes the 100,000-unit / 10-year framework; the H.O.M.E. initiative ($2B / 30,000-unit) and PHA's parallel $6.3B / 20,000-unit plan are the principal post-2024 architectural developments; *Defying Displacement* legislation provides expedited-permit, ZBA-affordability-conditioning, ADU-by-right, and density-payment-redirection tools; CLTs provide permanent-affordability infrastructure; the post-2022 abatement reform with affordability set-aside option provides a conditional-affordability development tool.

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** The framework operates as designed in proportion to capacity, eligibility design, and program reach. PHDC homeowner-preservation programs operate at substantial scale and deliver substantive output; the framework's reach into the most-acute deferred-maintenance constituents is constrained by eligibility design (G7-SD5-05; Profile A). Land Bank disposition operates substantively in individual cases while aggregate throughput falls short of inventory and need scale (G7-SD5-01; Profile B). LIHTC-expiration pressure generates active displacement risk for ~22% of the city's subsidized portfolio over a decade against preservation-infrastructure capacity that is improving (H.O.M.E.) but below decade-scale need (G7-SD5-02; Profile C; the Neighborhood Restorations July 2026 case). Inclusionary zoning operates voluntarily with documented underutilization of the affordable-construction option (G7-SD5-03). Construction tax abatement post-2022 reform conditions affordability function on set-aside uptake (G7-SD5-04). H.O.M.E. plan implementation is the principal forward-looking question (G7-SD5-06). The MC18 (Tandem Plan) reference could not be confirmed against primary sources within the Phase 1 search budget.

**What accounts for the gap.** Three structural features. First, the 30-year LIHTC design horizon set in 1986 produces a cyclical affordability-loss pressure now active in PA-3 at scale; the framework was designed for a finite affordability period and the consequences of that design choice arrive predictably; H.O.M.E. preservation funding is substantive but operates against decade-scale need exceeding any single-cycle preservation budget. Second, the Land Bank's statutory mandate authorizes strategic disposition without imposing an exclusive-affordable rule; disposition-pace constraints (CDC-capacity asymmetry; political-relationship-dependent dynamics; throughput limits relative to inventory) produce the documented gap. Third — most particular to PA-3 in current cycle — the H.O.M.E. and *Defying Displacement* architectural developments are recent; substantive achievement at implementation-and-equity-of-distribution phase is the open analytical question. Per Standard 10.B: anchors engage SD5 through CBA architecture (D7 SD4; SD1) and PRA / URA condemnation bordering anchor expansion; real-estate-actor primary at SD1; D9 SD4 (PILOT; MC15) and D10 (employer) carry other roles. Relative magnitude of LIHTC affordability expiration and Land Bank disposition decisions as displacement mechanisms against alternative mechanisms is held open per G7-SD1-03, not closed by analytical assertion. Both/And applies to Land Bank, PHDC, and PHARE per substructure §6; gap framing applies to abatement, IZ, and LIHTC preservation per substructure §6.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** The legal architecture for affordable-housing development in PA-3 is real and operative; substantive new architectural development (H.O.M.E.; *Defying Displacement*) is in active implementation phase. The gap between framework availability and substantive outcomes is structurally traceable to documented features (Land Bank disposition-pace constraints; LIHTC 30-year design horizon producing cyclical pressure now active; voluntary IZ producing payment-in-lieu preference; abatement-affordability conditional on set-aside uptake; H.O.M.E. implementation-phase question open). The infrastructure exists and is expanding; the question is whether the expanded infrastructure is scoped, equitably distributed, and implementation-capable to address the displacement geography its architecture acknowledges and addresses. The representation finding this SD supplies to synthesis: the framework's protective function operates substantively in cases where program-and-policy alignment occurs; its protective function aggregates below need scale across the documented displacement-pressure pipeline; and its post-2024 architectural development (H.O.M.E.; *Defying Displacement*) is the principal forward-looking analytical territory.

---
