## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile A: Black tenant in pre-1940 row home with deferred-maintenance code violations (under-enforcement pattern; D7-Thread B operation).** *Type:* Black tenant household in pre-1940 row home in N/NW Core or West Core disinvested-area sub-tract; multi-year tenancy (3+ years); income at or below 50% AMI; documented deferred-maintenance conditions (heating-system failures; plumbing leaks; electrical hazards; roof leaks; pest issues); landlord-of-record may be small individual landlord, mid-tier corporate landlord, or out-of-area absentee landlord with documented compliance gap pattern. *Sub-area:* most directly engaged in N/NW Core and parts of West Core. *Triggering event:* deferred-maintenance accumulation reaching habitability threshold; tenant complaint to L&I or City Hall; tenant rent-withholding decision; eviction filing by landlord. *Pathway:* Pathway 1 plus D7 SD3 tenant-remedy pathway interaction. *Breakdown points constituent encounters:* (a) reporting friction — tenants may face fear of retaliatory eviction (D7 SD3 protection exists but enforcement gap operates) and may not report; (b) under-enforcement pattern means even reported violations may face slow inspection-and-resolution timeline; (c) rent-withholding under *Pugh v. Holmes* requires escrow and procedural sophistication unavailable to most low-income tenants without legal aid; D7 SD3 RTC coverage is limited; (d) the structural finding: the framework provides remedies whose access depends on legal-aid resources unavailable at scale to the affected population, and whose institutional execution depends on enforcement-priority decisions that have historically deprioritized this sub-area. *Outcome:* the well-resourced tenant achieves remedy through coordinated legal-aid representation; the under-resourced tenant typically does not, with deferred maintenance continuing to accumulate. *The structural feature this profile exposes:* under-enforcement is a regulatory-disinvestment mechanism — the absence of effective code enforcement is itself a public-sector failure with material consequences for housing quality, health (D2 BLL pathway), and ultimately housing-stock deterioration that contributes to vacancy and blight concentration.

**Profile B: Black homeowner in gentrifying corridor receiving heightened code-enforcement pressure (selective-enforcement pattern; D7-Thread B operation).** *Type:* long-tenured Black homeowner (15+ years' occupancy) in gentrifying corridor (e.g., Point Breeze; Brewerytown; West Philadelphia near Penn periphery; portions of Germantown); pre-1940 row home with deferred-maintenance conditions accumulated over years of constrained household budget; fixed-or-low income household typical; new market-rate development on same block or in adjacent blocks. *Sub-area:* gentrifying corridors across multiple PA-3 sub-areas. *Triggering event:* L&I inspection initiated through complaint (often by new neighbor or developer-affiliated complainant) or routine inspection cycle re-activated for the area; multiple violation notices in close succession; fine accumulation; possible escalation to lien attachment or tax-foreclosure interface. *Pathway:* Pathway 1 plus interaction with D7 SD2 tax-foreclosure pathway and D7 SD1 acquisition-by-displacement pathway. *Breakdown points:* (a) compliance pressure — constituent may be unable to fund required repairs from constrained income; (b) PHDC BSRP eligibility may be available (D7 SD5) but eligibility design (taxes-and-water-current; emergency-threshold scope) may exclude; (c) fine-and-lien accumulation generates financial pressure interfacing with property-acquisition-by-displacement architecture (D7 SD1 Pathway 2; D7 SD2 foreclosure pathway); (d) outcome may be sale to acquisition-pipeline actor at price below fair market value because constituent lacks alternatives — selective enforcement operates as displacement mechanism rather than housing-quality intervention. *Outcome:* the constituent with access to BSRP, legal aid, or family-network financial resources may achieve compliance and retain property; the constituent without access faces sale-under-pressure with displacement consequences. *The structural feature this profile exposes:* selective enforcement is the second half of the D7-Thread B double pattern — code enforcement is not absent but differentially applied; the differential application aligns with development interest rather than housing-quality severity; the framework's protective function inverts to displacement function under selective-enforcement conditions.

**Profile C: Child in pre-1978 rental property with lead-paint-exposure pathway.** *Type:* child age 0–6 in family rental household in pre-1978 row home with documented or undocumented lead-paint hazards; rental property may or may not have current Lead Paint Disclosure and Certification; family rents from individual or corporate landlord; sub-area concentration in pre-1940 housing geography. *Sub-area:* most directly engaged in N/NW Core and parts of West Core. *Triggering event:* routine pediatric BLL screening at primary-care visit (D2 cross-reference) returning elevated BLL result; or, alternatively, family-discovered hazard prompting health-department investigation. *Pathway:* Pathway 2 plus interaction with D2 childhood-lead-poisoning surveillance and federal Lead Disclosure Rule disclosure architecture. *Breakdown points:* (a) Lead Disclosure Rule operates at transaction (sale or new tenancy) but does not automatically operate during ongoing tenancy where hazards develop or are discovered; (b) 2022 amendments to Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 added dust-wipe testing at tenant turnover in elevated-risk neighborhoods, but compliance is variable (G7-SD7-03; F7-SD7-02); (c) RRP Rule (40 C.F.R. Part 745) requires contractor certification for renovation, but non-certified contractors are documented as disproportionately used in lower-income neighborhoods where formal contractor-network access is constrained, with documented risk of creating or worsening lead hazards (G7-SD7-06; D10 cross-reference); (d) lead-violations × BLL combined dataset (F7-SD7-08) documents that properties with lead-violation history concentrate in same census tracts as elevated childhood BLL rates — the housing-to-health pathway is documented at neighborhood scale. *Outcome:* the family with effective intervention support and remediation capacity achieves hazard removal and child outcome management; the family without such support faces continuing exposure with documented neurological and developmental consequences (D2 cross-reference). *The structural feature this profile exposes:* the federal-state-local lead-paint framework is substantively designed but its operational effectiveness depends on enforcement intensity, RRP Rule compliance in renovation activity, and Lead Paint Certification compliance — each of which has documented gaps that disproportionately affect children in lower-income, predominantly Black PA-3 neighborhoods; the cross-domain housing-to-health pathway is the analytical center of this profile.

---

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The structural shape of code enforcement, housing quality, and blight remediation in PA-3 is conditioned by D7-Thread B — the documented double pattern of code enforcement that the substructure designates as having its primary analytical home in this sub-domain. The pattern operates simultaneously rather than sequentially: under-enforcement in disinvested predominantly-Black neighborhoods (N/NW Core; parts of West Core; Southwest Philadelphia) where code enforcement was historically deprioritized, allowing housing-stock deterioration to accelerate as regulatory disinvestment that has contributed directly to current vacancy and blight concentrations; and selective over-enforcement in gentrifying corridors (Point Breeze; Brewerytown; portions of West Philadelphia near anchor-institution periphery; Germantown corridor; Newbold) where code enforcement has been weaponized as displacement tool through enforcement intensity disproportionate to violation severity, with documented outcome of property clearance for redevelopment. The two patterns operate against different sub-tracts under different enforcement-priority logics: under-enforcement where political priority is low; over-enforcement where development interest is high. The framing the substructure mandates is gap framing primary — the framework's protective function operates substantively where enforcement is calibrated to housing-quality severity, and inverts to displacement function where enforcement is calibrated to development interest; the analytic finding is the inversion, not a balance.

The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) carries forward through SD7. Selective code enforcement is one documented displacement mechanism in the SD1-SD7 mechanism set: anchor-driven acquisition (SD1 Pathway 3a/3b/3c); foreclosure (SD2 Pathway 2); no-cause termination plus serial filing (SD3); fair-housing enforcement gaps and gentrification corridors (SD4); LIHTC affordability expiration plus Land Bank disposition (SD5); homelessness as downstream aggregation (SD6); selective code enforcement (SD7 D7-Thread B). With SD7's contribution, the mechanism set reaches Phase 1 documentation maximum. SD7 documents selective enforcement without analytical assertion of its primacy versus the other six documented mechanisms; the magnitude question's evidentiary base is now near-complete and synthesis-level integration becomes available at Phase 2 per substructure §9.

The federal Lead Paint Framework (Title X HCDA; HUD/EPA Lead Disclosure Rule; EPA RRP Rule) represents one of the most substantively-designed federal housing-quality frameworks. Its operational effectiveness in PA-3 depends on three interlocking elements: Lead Paint Disclosure Rule compliance at sales and new tenancies (variable per documentation); RRP Rule contractor certification for renovation in pre-1978 housing — overwhelming majority of PA-3 housing — where non-certified contractors are documented as disproportionately used in lower-income neighborhoods with documented risk of creating or worsening lead hazards (G7-SD7-06; D10 cross-reference); and local Lead Paint Disclosure and Certification compliance (Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 and 2022 amendments) where the dust-wipe-testing requirement at tenant turnover in elevated-risk neighborhoods is recently implemented and variable. The housing-to-health lead pathway (F7-SD7-08 lead-violations × BLL combined dataset) is the cross-domain integration linking SD7 to D2 — properties with lead-violation history concentrate in the same census tracts as elevated childhood BLL rates, providing documented evidence at neighborhood scale that housing-quality framework gaps translate directly to children's health outcomes.

Vacant property and blight remediation operates against documented PA-3 vacancy concentration in specific N/NW Core and West Core sub-tracts at well above the citywide ~12.1% rate. The vacant-property-registration framework provides operational architecture, but the gap between actual vacancy and registered vacancy is documented enforcement gap (G7-SD7-02). Emergency demolition operates as double-edged tool — addressing immediate safety hazards but accelerating vacancy and disinvestment in occupied-adjacent-property neighborhoods (G7-SD7-04). Act 135 conservatorship provides a supplemental tool used by CDCs and community organizations but with substantial procedural complexity (adjacent-owner standing; Court of Common Pleas litigation; cost-recovery uncertainty); utilization rate relative to documented blight volume is substantial enforcement gap (G7-SD7-05). The Land Bank consolidation pathway (D7 SD5) is the principal post-blight-remediation disposition vehicle with its own documented disposition-pace gap (G7-SD5-01).

Anchor-institution engagement at SD7 is secondary per substructure designation: anchors are not primary regulated entities for code enforcement but appear as housing-stock owners where applicable (Penn-, Drexel-, Temple-, and CHOP-owned properties function as institutional landlords with code-compliance regimes substantially different from the small-individual-landlord and absentee-corporate-landlord regimes that dominate D7-Thread B under-enforcement geography). The anchor code-compliance baseline operates as comparison rather than direct regulatory subject. Real-estate-actor primary engagement at SD1 produces upstream-pathway interactions with SD7 (acquisition pipeline interfaces with selective-enforcement pattern at G7-SD7-01); D9 SD4 (PILOT; MC15) and D10 (employer; RRP contractor certification) carry other roles per Standard 10.B.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G7-SD7-01: D7-Thread B double-pattern code enforcement (under-enforcement + selective over-enforcement; primary structural finding).** [SD] HIGH. Code enforcement in PA-3 documents two simultaneous racially-disparate patterns: under-enforcement in disinvested predominantly-Black neighborhoods producing housing-stock deterioration as regulatory disinvestment; selective over-enforcement in gentrifying corridors operating as displacement mechanism. The two patterns can be simultaneous and operate against different sub-tracts. *Evidence trace:* §3 Pathway 1; §4; §6; Profiles A and B; cross-reference G7-SD1-03 held-open territory (SD7 contribution to mechanism set). *Implication:* SD7 is the primary analytical home of D7-Thread B; the framework's protective function inverts to displacement function under selective-enforcement conditions.

**G7-SD7-02: Vacant property registration enforcement gap.** [SD] HIGH. The gap between actual vacancy and registered vacancy is documented enforcement gap; properties that are vacant but not registered escape the more-frequent-inspection regime and fine architecture. *Evidence trace:* §3 Pathway 3; §4; §6; F7-SD7-05. *Implication:* the vacant-property-management framework operates against an undercount of its operational subject; the documented citywide ~12.1% vacancy rate corresponds to a registry coverage rate substantially below 100%.

**G7-SD7-03: Lead Paint Certification compliance gap.** [SD] HIGH. Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 Lead Paint Disclosure and Certification, including 2022 amendments adding dust-wipe testing in elevated-risk neighborhoods at tenant turnover, operates against a housing-stock distribution that is overwhelmingly pre-1978; framework reach depends on enforcement intensity at scale; documented compliance is variable. *Evidence trace:* §2 Local Statutory; §3 Pathway 2; §6; Profile C; F7-SD7-02. *Implication:* the local lead-paint framework is the principal local intensification of the federal Lead Paint Framework; its operational effectiveness conditions the housing-to-health lead pathway documented at G7-SD7-06 and F7-SD7-08.

**G7-SD7-04: Emergency demolition double-edged structural feature.** [SI] MEDIUM. Emergency demolition addresses immediate safety hazards but accelerates vacancy and disinvestment when concentrated in occupied-adjacent-property neighborhoods; the tool's protective function in safety terms operates alongside its accelerant function in vacancy-cascade terms. *Evidence trace:* §3 Pathway 3; §6. *Implication:* the framework's most-coercive remediation tool has structural costs that condition its appropriate scope of use; the documented concentration of emergency demolition in N/NW Core sub-tracts illustrates the tool's interaction with neighborhood-stability geography.

**G7-SD7-05: Act 135 conservatorship utilization-vs-blight-volume gap.** [SI] MEDIUM. Act 135 provides a tool used by CDCs and community organizations but with substantial procedural complexity (adjacent-owner standing; Court of Common Pleas litigation; cost-recovery uncertainty); utilization relative to documented blight volume is substantial gap. *Evidence trace:* §2 State Statutory; §3 Pathway 3; §6; F7-SD7-06. *Implication:* a substantively-designed remediation tool operates at a scale below documented need; the procedural-complexity feature limits utilization to well-resourced CDCs in relationships with sympathetic community courts.

**G7-SD7-06: RRP Rule contractor certification gap (D10 cross-reference).** [SD] HIGH. EPA RRP Rule requires contractor certification for renovation in pre-1978 housing; non-certified contractors are documented as disproportionately used in lower-income neighborhoods where formal contractor-network access is constrained, with documented risk of creating or worsening lead hazards during renovation. *Evidence trace:* §2 Federal Regulatory; §6; Profile C; cross-reference D10. *Implication:* the federal lead-paint framework's renovation-activity safeguard is documented as systematically circumvented in the neighborhoods most affected by lead-paint hazard concentration; the RRP Rule's protective function depends on contractor-certification compliance discipline whose documented gap aligns with neighborhood income geography.

**G7-SD7-07: Rental licensing self-certification compliance gap.** [SI] MEDIUM. Rental-license self-certification option creates documented compliance gap because self-certification does not require third-party inspection; properties operating as rentals without licenses constitute documented gap. *Evidence trace:* §3 Pathway 2; F7-SD7-03. *Implication:* the rental-licensing framework operates with a self-certification compliance assumption that reduces administrative burden but produces gap-relative-to-actual-rental-activity; the gap conditions Profile A under-enforcement pattern operation.

---

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** The federal-state-local architecture documented in §2 provides a substantive framework: Phila. Code Title 6 establishes minimum habitability standards; L&I administers rental licensing, lead-paint certification (with 2022 amendments adding dust-wipe testing in elevated-risk neighborhoods), vacant-property registration, emergency demolition authority, and code-violation enforcement; OpenDataPhilly publishes L&I violation data enabling neighborhood-level analysis; the federal Lead Paint Framework (Title X HCDA; HUD/EPA Lead Disclosure Rule; EPA RRP Rule) provides the federal-level lead-hazard architecture; Act 135 and the Neighborhood Blight Reclamation and Revitalization Act provide supplemental conservatorship-and-blight pathways; the *Camara* administrative-search framework conditions L&I inspection authority.

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** The framework operates as designed in proportion to enforcement intensity, compliance discipline, and neighborhood political-and-development priority. D7-Thread B documents two simultaneous patterns: under-enforcement in disinvested predominantly-Black neighborhoods producing housing-stock deterioration (G7-SD7-01; Profile A); selective over-enforcement in gentrifying corridors operating as displacement mechanism (G7-SD7-01; Profile B). Lead-paint certification compliance is variable (G7-SD7-03; Profile C); RRP Rule contractor certification is documented as circumvented in neighborhoods with most acute lead-paint hazard concentration (G7-SD7-06; D10 cross-reference). Vacant-property registration coverage is documented below actual vacancy (G7-SD7-02). Emergency demolition operates as double-edged tool (G7-SD7-04). Act 135 conservatorship operates at scale below documented blight volume (G7-SD7-05). Rental-license self-certification produces compliance gap (G7-SD7-07). The L&I OpenDataPhilly violation dataset provides one of the richest public datasets for housing-quality and blight analysis; its analytical use depends on framing discipline.

**What accounts for the gap.** Three structural features. First, code enforcement requires enforcement-priority decisions whose political logic does not always align with housing-quality severity; the documented D7-Thread B double pattern is the structural expression — under-enforcement where political priority is low; selective over-enforcement where development interest is high. The pattern operates against the racial-equity framing engaged at D7 SD4 *ICP* disparate-impact and produces racially disparate outcomes traceable through Civil Rights Act jurisprudence. Second, federal-level lead-paint architecture (Title X HCDA; Lead Disclosure Rule; RRP Rule) operates effectively only where compliance discipline operates at scale; documented gaps in disclosure compliance, RRP contractor certification, and local Lead Paint Certification translate directly to neighborhood-level lead-hazard exposure that produces neighborhood-level childhood BLL elevation per F7-SD7-08 (D2 cross-reference); the framework's design-vs-implementation gap is the structural finding. Third — most particular to the post-2022 PA-3 cycle — the recent amendments to Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 represent the principal local-level intensification of the lead-paint framework but compliance and elevated-risk-neighborhood-designation lists are recently implemented and variable. Per Standard 10.B: anchors engage SD7 secondarily as institutional landlords with code-compliance regimes structurally different from D7-Thread B geography; real-estate-actor primary at SD1; D9 SD4 (MC15) and D10 (employer; RRP) carry other roles. Relative magnitude of selective code enforcement as displacement mechanism against alternative mechanisms is held open per G7-SD1-03, not closed by analytical assertion. Both/And does not apply per substructure §6; gap framing is primary throughout.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** The legal architecture for code enforcement, housing quality, and blight remediation in PA-3 is real and operative; Phila. Code Title 6, the federal Lead Paint Framework, and Act 135 are substantively designed; the L&I OpenDataPhilly violation dataset provides analytical infrastructure for documenting enforcement-pattern variation. The gap between framework availability and substantive outcomes is structurally traceable to documented features (D7-Thread B double pattern; lead-paint compliance variation; vacant-registry coverage gap; emergency-demolition double-edged structural feature; Act 135 procedural-complexity utilization barrier; RRP contractor-certification circumvention; self-certification rental-license gap). The representation finding this SD supplies to synthesis is structural: code enforcement is not a neutral technical activity but an instrument of representation choice — whose interests are protected through enforcement intensity, whose interests are not protected through enforcement deferral, whose interests are advanced through enforcement intensification — and the pattern of those choices produces materially different outcomes for differently-positioned PA-3 constituents along racial, income, and neighborhood-trajectory lines. With SD7 complete, the D7 Phase 1 mechanism set (SD1-SD7) for the held-open G7-SD1-03 displacement-magnitude question reaches Phase 1 documentation maximum; synthesis-level integration becomes available at Phase 2.

### Cluster H: Standard 18 vector candidates — moved to sidecar at Phase 3

*Phase 3 update (2026-05-03): Per M2 v1.2 §7 Capability 2 and per the substructure §10 Standard 18 deferral, the six per-SD vector-candidate meta-flags (T7-SD2-05, T7-SD3-05, T7-SD4-06, T7-SD5-06, T7-SD6-06, T7-SD7-06) are now constructed as TC-NN entries in the `temporal-changes_D7_2026-05-03.md` sidecar in Standard 18.A vector format (anchor + dated vector points + analytical consequence). The vector-format sidecar is the cumulative cross-cycle persistence record; subsequent verification cycles append new dated points to existing vectors rather than overwriting. ### Phase 3 verification priority sequencing notes

The clusters above are intended as Phase 3 verification work-organizing groupings, not as enforcement-order constraints. Per M2 v1.2 §7, Phase 3 verification is bounded to web-search-tractable sources; the boundedness discipline ("close, not perfect") engages each flag with web-search retrieval where feasible and documents institutional-retrieval territory in the unverified-items sidecar where it is not. The sequencing the substructure projects, on which the consolidated inventory operationalizes:

1. *Cluster A federal regulatory administrative-vulnerability* engages first, as multiple SD findings calibrate against current rule status; the AFFH rule (T7-SD4-03) and post-*Grants Pass* litigation (T7-SD6-01) are the highest-volatility items.
2. *Cluster C PA state-level legislative-action* engages next, capturing any session-period activity affecting no-cause termination, rent-control preemption, Land Bank Act, or Act 135.
3. *Cluster D Philadelphia local statutory and program developments* engages H.O.M.E. plan implementation, *Defying Displacement* legislation, and Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 amendments — the principal post-2024 architectural-development territory.
4. *Cluster B constitutional / case-law developments* engages *Grants Pass* litigation aftermath and *Camara* doctrine developments.
5. *Cluster E anchor institution disclosure* engages CBA status documentation across Penn / Temple / Drexel — the principal institutional-retrieval-likely territory per M2 v1.2 §7 boundedness.
6. *Clusters F, G* engage the program-portfolio and tract-level retrieval surface; substantial overlap exists at the data-source layer (OpenDataPhilly L&I; ACS; Reinvestment Fund; phila.gov/ohs); Phase 3 retrieval consolidates against the four-sub-area framework per Standard 6.
7. *Cluster H Standard 18 vector candidates* are the territory in which the Phase 3 vector-format temporal-changes sidecar is constructed.

Per Pattern 12 enforcement (M2 v1.2 §7 Capability 2 + Appendix A), each Material Changes Summary entry produced from this consolidated inventory must correspond to inline application at the body locations affected. The consolidated inventory above is the navigation map; the inline-correction discipline applies at the embedded SD content (Sections 1–9 across SD1-SD7 below).

## Synthesis Section 5: What Carries Forward

Per Standard 9, this section names cross-domain carry-forward findings by domain, sub-domain, and finding rather than as general observations. Findings are organized by receiving domain, with cross-cutting (multi-domain) carry-forwards named separately. The cross-domain-notes file at finalization records each entry per Standard 15.

### To D2 Public Health (Verified 2026-04-29) — finding to be incorporated at next D2 reverification cycle

**MC16 cross-domain finding (D7 SD7 → D2).** *Lead paint enforcement framework (D7 SD7) ↔ childhood BLL outcomes (D2).* D7 SD7 covers the enforcement framework: Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 Lead Paint Disclosure and Certification including 2022 amendments adding dust-wipe testing in elevated-risk neighborhoods at tenant turnover (G7-SD7-03); EPA RRP Rule contractor certification with documented disproportionate non-certified contractor utilization in lower-income neighborhoods (G7-SD7-06); HUD/EPA Lead Disclosure Rule; rental licensing self-certification compliance gap (G7-SD7-07). D7 SD7 documents the housing-to-health lead pathway at the enforcement-framework layer; D2's BLL outcome data carries the health-pathway downstream. The L&I OpenDataPhilly violation dataset combined with DPH childhood blood-lead-level data by census tract (F7-SD7-08) is the principal cross-domain analytical infrastructure. *Cross-reference protocol:* D2 reverification engaging lead-paint-related findings should engage D7 SD7 G7-SD7-03 / G7-SD7-06 / G7-SD7-07 rather than re-derive the enforcement-framework analysis.

### To D8 Commerce & Industry (Planned)

**MC14 cross-domain finding (D7 SD4 → D8).** *Commercial corridor displacement parallel.* D7 SD4 owns residential displacement in PA-3 gentrifying corridors — Point Breeze, Brewerytown, Cedar Park / Spruce Hill, Germantown Avenue corridor, Mantua / Mill Creek, Strawberry Mansion. D8 will own commercial-tenant protections (or lack thereof) and commercial real-estate market dynamics. D7 SD4 explicitly bounded out D8 territory per substructure §8 question 8. *Cross-reference protocol:* when D8 cycle runs, D8 commercial-corridor-displacement SD should engage D7 SD4's residential-displacement findings (G7-SD4-06 anti-displacement toolkit capacity-vs-need; G7-SD4-07 PA preemption of local rent control; the documented gentrification corridors) and the D7-Thread A displacement architecture mechanism inventory rather than re-derive the residential-side analysis. The rent-control-preemption finding (G7-SD3-02 / G7-SD4-07) carries forward to D8 because PA-level preemption affects commercial-tenant protection structurally similarly.

**Additional D7 → D8 carry-forward (D7 SD1 → D8).** *Commercial zoning architecture.* D7 SD1 covers Title 14 Zoning Code commercial use categories (CMX) and overlays; D8 carries the substantive analysis of commercial-corridor land-use patterns. *Cross-reference protocol:* D8 engaging Title 14 commercial categories should engage D7 SD1's pathway tracing (Pathway 2 — ZBA variance) and procedural-democracy findings (G7-SD1-04 RCO capacity-and-representativeness) without re-deriving the framework.

**Additional D7 → D8 carry-forward (D7 SD7 → D8).** *Code enforcement on commercial buildings.* D7 SD7 covers Phila. Code Title 6 minimum habitability standards and L&I enforcement architecture for residential housing; D8 carries any commercial-building code-enforcement analysis. The D7-Thread B "double pattern" finding (G7-SD7-01) may have a commercial-corridor parallel; D8 evaluates structural extension at its substructure phase.

### To D9 Finance & Taxation (Verified)

**MC15 cross-domain finding (D7 SD1 + SD4 → D9 SD4).** *Anchor institution land use / PILOT architecture.* D7 owns land-use and displacement analysis of anchor real-estate footprint (Penn / Temple / Drexel / CHOP); D9 SD4 owns the tax-exempt fiscal architecture (PILOT/PILOET). D7 documents the real-estate-actor primary role per Standard 10.B; D9 SD4 carries the fiscal-actor dimension. *Cross-reference protocol:* D9 SD4 reverification engaging anchor PILOT architecture should engage D7 SD1 G7-SD1-06 (anchor tax-exempt fiscal architecture gap, deferred to D9 SD4 substantively); D7 SD1 G7-SD1-03 held-open displacement-magnitude territory (the synthesis-level mechanism inventory and the structural restraint on closing the magnitude question by analytical assertion); and the substructure §4 anchor-engagement table per anchor institution. F7-SD1-04 (Penn PILOT current arrangement), F7-SD1-05 (Temple-area CBAs), F7-SD1-06 (Schuylkill Yards / Drexel community-benefit commitments), and F7-SD4-05 (current CBA status documentation) feed D9 SD4 cross-reference precision.

**MC12 cross-domain finding (D7 SD5 → D9 SD3).** *Construction tax abatement architecture.* D9 SD3 owns tax architecture (assessment regime, abatement structure post-2022 reform, AVI / LOOP / BIRT / property tax); D7 SD5 covers abatement as an affordable-housing development tool with affordability set-aside option. *Cross-reference protocol:* D9 SD3 engaging post-2022 abatement reform should engage D7 SD5 G7-SD5-04 (construction tax abatement post-2022 reform engagement gap) and F7-SD5-05 (affordability set-aside option utilization data); D7 SD5 references D9 SD3 for the underlying tax architecture without duplicating.

### To D10 Labor & Employment (Planned)

**MC13 cross-domain finding (D7 SD7 → D10).** *EPA RRP Rule contractor certification framework.* D7 SD7 covers the housing-quality lens (lead-safe work practices interface with rental licensing and lead disclosure; the documented circumvention of RRP certification in lower-income neighborhoods at G7-SD7-06 with neighborhood-level lead-hazard exposure consequence at F7-SD7-08); D10 owns contractor licensing and OSH Act / EPA intersection at the labor-side. *Cross-reference protocol:* D10 engaging contractor licensing and construction-trades architecture should engage D7 SD7 G7-SD7-06 (RRP Rule contractor certification gap) and F7-SD7-09 (current RRP Rule contractor certification rates in PA-3) without re-deriving the housing-side; D7 SD7 references D10 for the labor-side.

**Additional D7 → D10 carry-forward (D7 SD1 → D10).** *Employer dimension of Standard 10.B anchor triple role.* D7 SD1 documents the real-estate-actor primary role; D10 carries the employer-actor dimension (graduate student organizing, SEIU 32BJ, anchor-institution employment patterns). *Cross-reference protocol:* D10 engaging anchor-institution-as-employer should engage D7 SD1 / SD4 substructure §4 anchor-engagement table per institution; D7 references D10 for employer-side analysis without duplication.

### To D11 Education (Planned) — foregrounded methodologically per lead direction 2026-05-02

**MC19 cross-domain finding (D7 SD6 → D11; foregrounded per lead direction).** *McKinney-Vento Title VII definitional gap (HUD/CoC vs. McKinney-Vento Title VII education definition).* D7 SD6 G7-SD6-03 documents the definitional discrepancy: Title I (HUD/CoC) excludes doubled-up households from the homeless definition; Title VII (education) includes them. Families doubled up are counted as homeless for educational services but not for CoC services. PIT counts and HMIS data systematically undercount housing-instability prevalence; resource-allocation decisions driven by Title I data therefore operate against an undercounted denominator. The finding — *the data infrastructure itself reproduces the distortion under analysis* — is foregrounded methodologically within SD6 as Track 2 substantive finding, with all four Standard 8 Representation Question elements engaged. *Cross-reference protocol:* when the D11 Education cycle runs, the McKinney-Vento Title VII SD should engage D7 SD6 G7-SD6-03 and the substructure §3 cross-reference table for the definitional-gap finding rather than re-derive it. Per lead direction 2026-05-02, this carry-forward is a substantive methodological contribution to the project's representation framework.

**Additional D7 → D11 carry-forward (D7 SD1 → D11).** *Residential segregation → school segregation pathway; Penn Alexander school catchment displacement effect.* D7 SD1 documents the Penn Alexander school catchment as engaged-community anchor with adjacent-property-value-increase consequence (Pathway 3a; Profile A); D11 carries the school-side analysis. *Cross-reference protocol:* D11 engaging school catchment effects should engage D7 SD1 / SD4 (F7-SD4-09 Penn Alexander school catchment property-value-increase analysis) and the substructure §4 anchor-engagement context.

### To D12 Social Welfare (Verified 2026-05-01) — finding to be incorporated at next D12 reverification cycle

**MC02 cross-domain finding (D7 SD3, SD4, SD5 → D12 SD4 — boundary-call infrastructure).** *Housing assistance interface.* D12 SD4 covered HCV / PBV / public-housing administered by PHA, HCV waitlist dynamics, lease-up failure, EHV one-time allocation, coordinated entry, and ERA. D7 takes that as given and extends:
- *D7 SD3 → D12 SD4:* ERA recipient-side benefit administration is D12 SD4; landlord-tenant statute, eviction process, no-cause termination posture (G7-SD3-01), and serial-filer pattern (G7-SD3-06) are D7 SD3.
- *D7 SD4 → D12 SD4:* FHA framework basics and HCV mobility-of-vouchers are D12 SD4; FHA enforcement mechanics, *ICP* disparate-impact framework, AFFH, PHRA source-of-income protection (G7-SD4-04), CBAs, and PA-3 displacement documentation are D7 SD4.
- *D7 SD5 → D12 SD4:* LIHTC mechanics, HOME, CDBG, Section 8 PBA are D12 SD4; LIHTC preservation at affordability expiration (G7-SD5-02), non-LIHTC dev tools, Land Bank disposition (G7-SD5-01), CLTs, abatement, post-2022 reform, *Defying Displacement* are D7 SD5.

*Cross-reference protocol:* D12 reverification engaging housing-assistance-related findings should preserve the boundary call. Where the housing-assistance side meets the housing-law side (e.g., PHA-as-landlord eviction proceedings; voucher-mobility outcomes mediated by PHRA source-of-income protection; LIHTC properties at affordability expiration approaching D12-administered alternative-affordability infrastructure), the cross-domain reference is symmetric: D12 SD4 owns assistance-architecture; D7 SD3 / SD4 / SD5 own law-architecture. F7-SD5-09 (Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio outcome — July 2026 deadline) is a forward-looking event affecting both D12 SD4 (potential alternative-affordability infrastructure absorption) and D7 SD5 (LIHTC preservation outcome).

### To D13 Physical Infrastructure (Verified)

**MC17 cross-domain finding (D7 SD7 → D13).** *Housing infrastructure interface.* D13 owns housing-infrastructure substance (water/sewer hookups; electrical service; gas line aging; lead service line replacement). D7 SD7 references at the housing-quality interface; does not duplicate D13's coverage. *Cross-reference protocol:* D13 reverification engaging housing-infrastructure findings should engage D7 SD7 for the housing-quality enforcement-framework layer where the two interfaces meet (e.g., L&I violations involving service-line conditions; rental-licensing inspection scope where infrastructure conditions affect habitability) without re-deriving the enforcement-framework analysis. The 2022 Phila. Code Ch. 6-800 lead-paint amendments interact with D13 lead-service-line replacement programmatic infrastructure; the cross-reference carries the housing-quality / housing-infrastructure boundary.

**Additional D7 → D13 carry-forward (D7 SD1, SD5 → D13).** *Historical urban-renewal displacement and current infrastructure inheritance.* D7 SD1 documents PRA blight-condemnation history; D7 SD5 documents PRA / URA intersection; D13 inherits the present-day infrastructure-condition consequence. *Cross-reference protocol:* D13 engaging neighborhood-level infrastructure-condition variation should engage D7 SD1 §4 / SD5 §3 historical-pattern documentation.

### Cross-cutting carry-forwards (multi-domain)

**The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) and the D7-Thread A mechanism inventory.** The mechanism inventory documented at synthesis level (anchor-driven acquisition; foreclosure; no-cause termination + serial filing; fair-housing enforcement gaps + gentrification corridors; LIHTC affordability expiration + Land Bank disposition; homelessness as downstream aggregation; selective code enforcement) carries to **all subsequent domains where displacement-related findings emerge**. The substructure-flagged failure mode (coherence-seeking toward any single-mechanism narrative) carries forward as a discipline rather than as a substantive finding — subsequent domains engaging displacement analysis should preserve mechanism-inventory completeness without analytical-assertion closure of relative magnitude. The Standard 18 vector candidates (Cluster H above) feed forward into the temporal-changes sidecar across cycles; resolution is structurally pending rather than analytically deferred.

**The federal-floor-stable-but-administratively-vulnerable-above-floor pattern (Standard 11 territory).** Carries to all subsequent domains engaging federal regulatory architecture — particularly D8 (commercial-tenant protection where federal commercial-tenant-protection floor is administratively vulnerable), D11 (McKinney-Vento Title VII appropriations status; Title I funding), D17 (where any federal civil-rights-enforcement architecture engages), and any subsequent reverification cycle of D2, D9, D10, D12, D13 where the framework-vulnerability calibration shifts.

**The cumulative racial disadvantage geography pattern (DF Thread 2 in D7 form).** Carries to subsequent domains as the structural-underlay finding — D8 commercial corridor displacement geography; D11 school segregation geography; D17 public safety geography all read against the same redlining-and-urban-renewal-displacement historical underlay D7 documents structurally.

**The MC18 Tandem Plan documented-as-unresolvable item.** Carries to Phase 3 verification (D7 itself) as confirmation-of-unresolvability item per substructure §6 lead-direction-2026-05-02 protocol, not as an open analytical claim. If Phase 3 surfaces primary-source identification of a "Tandem Plan" reference, the item resolves; absent surfacing, the item closes as historical-handoff-note artifact rather than as substantive finding.

## Self-Assessment Footer

Per M2 v1.2 §6, this footer is honest, not performative. Issues noted here become Phase 3 verification priorities.

### What this cycle produced well

The Phase 1 + Phase 2 cycle for D7 produced an integrated set of seven sub-domain analyses (~40,841 words across SD1-SD7) covering the full legal-architecture decomposition the substructure prescribed (property rights / land use; mortgage market; landlord-tenant law; fair housing / anti-displacement; affordable housing development; adult homelessness; code enforcement). Each SD followed the M2 §5 nine-section Per-SD Template structurally; each SD's gap analysis received stable per-SD G-flag IDs per Standard 5.E; each SD's verification flags received stable per-SD F- and T-flag IDs per Standard 2.E. The Both/And designations the substructure surfaced (SD3 narrowly; SD4 primary; SD5 narrowly) were honored in their narrow-trigger form rather than as blanket SD characterizations. The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) was preserved through all seven SDs without closure-by-analytical-assertion, and the Phase 2 synthesis articulates the seven-mechanism inventory collectively at synthesis level without choosing among the mechanisms — the substructure-flagged failure mode (coherence-seeking toward any single-mechanism narrative) was refused at the most consequential moment per substructure §8 question 1. The MC19 McKinney-Vento Title VII definitional-gap finding was foregrounded methodologically per lead direction 2026-05-02 with all four Standard 8 elements engaged in SD6. The MC18 Tandem Plan reference was processed per the lead-direction time-boxed protocol and documented as confirmation-of-unresolvability rather than asserted as substantive finding. Cross-domain carry-forwards were named at the substructure-level expected (MC02 → D12 SD4; MC12 → D9 SD3; MC13 → D10; MC14 → D8; MC15 → D9 SD4; MC16 → D2; MC17 → D13; MC19 → D11) plus the displacement-mechanism-inventory cross-cutting carry-forward. The substructure §6 Both/And narrow-trigger discipline produced sharper SD-level analytical work than blanket characterizations would have produced; the synthesis reflects that the framework's protective function and the implementation gap operate together at SD3, SD4, SD5 rather than as either-or.

The Phase 2 OUTPUT assembly produced the integrated Shape A artifact per M2 v1.2 §6 specification rather than the manifest pattern Pattern 14 names. The Step 7.5 Shape A integrity check (per pa3-cycle SKILL.md) ran before Phase 2 was declared complete. The integrity check verified: (a) embedded full per-SD content for SD1 through SD7 — all nine sections of the M2 §5 Per-SD Template embedded verbatim in numerical order, not bullet-card summaries pointing at sibling per-SD files; (b) all five synthesis sections present and named (Section 1 What This Domain Established; Section 2 The Aggregate Finding; Section 3 Confidence Calibration; Section 4 What Requires Ongoing Verification; Section 5 What Carries Forward — Sections 1–3 before SD content; Sections 4–5 after SD content); (c) Self-Assessment footer present with the five M2 §6 elements. Word count is approximately 47,000–48,000 words against the M2 §6 expected 25,000–40,000+ word range for a Shape A integrated artifact across seven SDs — within the expected range. Phase 3 verification operating against the OUTPUT as a single contiguous document per M2 §7 should not encounter the Pattern 14 halt diagnostic.

### What this cycle produced poorly

Three principal issues for Phase 3 verification attention.

*First*, **the SD2 anomalous F-flag and T-flag prefix.** SD2's verification flags used the prefix `F2-SD2-NN` and `T2-SD2-NN` rather than the `F7-SD2-NN` / `T7-SD2-NN` convention applied at SD1 / SD3 / SD4 / SD5 / SD6 / SD7. Per Pattern 14 prevention, SD2 content was preserved verbatim in the embedded section in the Phase 2 OUTPUT; the anomaly was not corrected at Phase 2. The OUTPUT's consolidated Section 4 inventory preserved the anomalous prefix as well.

*Verification update (2026-05-03): Phase 3 normalized the prefix at verified-output construction per lead direction 2026-05-03 — see Material Changes entry MC-10 at the front of this verified file. The 52 occurrences of `F2-SD2-NN` / `T2-SD2-NN` were replaced with `F7-SD2-NN` / `T7-SD2-NN` throughout (SD2 §2/§3/§4/§5/§6/§7 narrative; SD2 §9 verification flag table; Synthesis Section 1 Cross-cutting Finding D reference; Synthesis Section 4 consolidated inventory cross-references; Synthesis Section 4 Phase 3 priority sequencing notes; this Self-Assessment narrative). The principal-anchor Case 3 retain-shorthand-policy disclosure is at SD2 §2 Federal Statutory Layer first F7-SD2-01 occurrence; subsequent occurrences carry the corrected prefix without per-occurrence inline notes per M2 v1.2 Appendix A Case 3 discipline. The analytical content was unaffected. This is recorded as a Standard 9 cross-reference consistency normalization.*

*Second*, **the Track 2 statistical content across SD1-SD7 leans heavily on structural inference rather than directly documented PA-3-specific quantification at tract level.** This is a Standard 5 confidence-calibration issue carried as F-flag inventory across all seven SDs in Section 4 Cluster F. The retrieval surface — current ACS B25003 by race for Philadelphia / PA-3; PA-3-specific HOLC-correlation analyses; current eviction filing rates with tract-level disaggregation; current TOPA / EDP / RTC outcome data; current PIT count and PA-500 demographic breakdowns; current L&I violation tract-level concentration; current LIHTC-expiration inventory; current Land Bank disposition data; current PHDC program-portfolio uptake; current Reinvestment Fund market-typology — is substantial. Phase 3 verification needs to engage this systematically. The substructure §10 Phase 3 boundedness discipline applies: where tract-level retrieval requires institutional-retrieval territory rather than web-search-tractable sources, the boundedness is documented in the unverified-items sidecar rather than improvised.

*Third*, **the held-open magnitude question's structural framing at synthesis level.** The synthesis articulates the mechanism inventory collectively and the structural-consequence finding about formal-procedure-vs-substantive-protection across mechanisms; it does not close the magnitude question by analytical assertion. The discipline holds, but the framing risks reading as inconclusive at synthesis-level engagement; this is the substructure-flagged failure mode's converse risk. The mechanism inventory's completeness at the level of identified pathways and breakdown points is HIGH-confidence; the magnitude assertion across mechanisms is LOW-confidence; the synthesis-level engagement holds these confidence levels apart appropriately, but Phase 3 verification engaging the held-open territory should preserve this two-tier structure rather than collapsing it into a single confidence reading. The substructure §8 question 1 discipline — *no SD's gap analysis or Track 2 finding may force closure on this question* — extends to the synthesis level and to Phase 3.

### Standards followed most cleanly

- **Standard 1 (Data provenance).** Training-data-only regime declared at OUTPUT level and at each SD's Section 1; no mixed-regime drafting; PA-3-specific verified figures explicitly F-flagged at retrieval point.
- **Standard 4 (Advocacy language prohibition).** Synthesis Section 2 stated as structural consequence; "coherent architecture" / "designed to disadvantage" / "not accidental" framings refused. Pattern 4 (advocacy language drift in late SDs) tested against the synthesis specifically; the synthesis composes findings as documented-design-features-producing-distributional-outcomes-predictable-from-mechanics formulation.
- **Standard 5 (Confidence calibration).** Per-SD G-flag findings labeled [D]/[SD]/[SI] and HIGH/MEDIUM/LOW; synthesis Section 3 calibrated against weakest underlying SD finding's confidence; held-open territory explicitly LOW-confidence-as-magnitude-assertion / HIGH-confidence-as-mechanism-inventory.
- **Standard 9 (Cross-reference discipline).** Each cross-reference names domain, sub-domain, and finding (e.g., "D12 SD4 (Verified): HCV mobility-of-vouchers (MC02)"). Section 5 applies this format throughout.
- **Standard 10.B (Anchor institution triple role).** Real-estate-actor primary in D7 per Standard 10.B's domain-specific guidance; employer dimension cross-referenced to D10; fiscal dimension cross-referenced to D9 SD4; the triple role held simultaneously across all SDs without collapsing to single-role analysis.
- **Standard 11 (Administrative vulnerability).** Two-dimensional classification applied at each SD describing federal-regulatory architecture; T-flag inventory carries the temporal-update obligation; AFFH / disparate-impact rule / FHEO / CFPB / CRA / HUD CoC NOFO / Lead Hazard Control / SAMHSA PATH / *Grants Pass* protective-architecture withdrawal each engaged at the SD where they are operative.
- **Standard 14 (Multi-classification watch list).** MC02 / MC12 / MC13 / MC14 / MC15 / MC16 / MC17 / MC18 / MC19 each engaged at the SD where they operate, with Section 5 cross-domain carry-forward documenting the interface per receiving domain.

### Standards followed least cleanly

- **Standard 6 (Geographic specificity).** PA-3 four-sub-area framework applied at each SD's §4 Statistical Profile; tract-level disaggregation deferred to Phase 3 / institutional-retrieval per the Track 2 limitation noted above. The framework is structurally honored; tract-level depth is conditional on retrieval. This is the principal cycle-level limitation per the F-flag Cluster F inventory.
- **Standard 13 (Aggregate / structural pathways convention).** Each SD's §3 Pathway Tracing engages structural pathways rather than confabulating individual-case detail. The discipline holds; some pathway detail (particularly at SD4 anti-displacement-toolkit pathways and SD6 PSH / RRH / Coordinated Entry pathways) leans toward structural-mechanics description; F-flag inventory captures the empirical-detail retrieval needed.
- **Standard 17 (Governmental-score updating discipline).** No CBO-equivalent governmental score for D7's substantive findings emerged in Phase 1 in a form requiring Standard 17 disposition at synthesis level; the discipline is operative at cross-domain interfaces (D9 SD4 anchor PILOT cost; D12 SD4 OBBBA architectural finding) but engages at the receiving-domain level rather than at D7 synthesis. If Phase 3 surfaces governmental-score citations during cross-domain reference, the discipline applies — original score preserved as primary; updated baseline supplementary with explicit comparator.
- **Standard 18 (Temporal data vectors with claim anchoring).** Per substructure §10, vector-format temporal-changes sidecar construction is Phase 3 work, not Phase 2. Phase 2 surfaces vector candidates at Section 4 Cluster H; the explicit T7-SD7-06 / T7-SD2-05 / T7-SD3-05 / T7-SD4-06 / T7-SD5-06 / T7-SD6-06 meta-flag pattern across SD §9 inventories operationalizes the Phase 3 sidecar entry point. The construct is correctly Phase 2-deferred; the Phase 3 sidecar construction is the operational test.

### Specific items requiring verification attention (Phase 3 priority)

In Phase 3 verification-priority order:

1. **AFFH rule current status (T7-SD4-03).** Most administratively-volatile element of SD4 architecture; G7-SD4-01 calibration depends on whether the 2023 rule is operative, weakened, replaced, or in litigation at verification date.
2. **Post-*Grants Pass v. Johnson* litigation development (T7-SD6-01).** G7-SD6-06 protective-architecture-withdrawal finding depends on alternative-protection-substitution status at verification date.
3. **HUD disparate-impact rule current status (T7-SD4-01).** G7-SD4-02 framework operationalization depends on rule-revision status.
4. **2023 CRA Final Rule current implementation status (T7-SD2-01).** G7-SD2-02 CRA-coverage analysis depends on rule-implementation status.
5. **CFPB structural and enforcement-priority status (T7-SD2-02).** Multiple SD2 findings calibrate against CFPB posture.
6. **HUD CoC NOFO and McKinney-Vento appropriations posture (T7-SD6-02).** G7-SD6-02 PSH-to-need calibration depends on funding-cycle status.
7. **HUD AFFH and disparate-impact rule status as a coordinated cluster (T7-SD2-03; T7-SD4-03; T7-SD4-04).** Convergent administrative-vulnerability cluster.
8. **PA legislative action on no-cause termination, rent-control preemption, or security-deposit framework (T7-SD3-02; F7-SD3-07).** G7-SD3-01 / G7-SD3-02 framing depends on session-period activity status.
9. **H.O.M.E. plan multi-year bond-issuance schedule and Year 2+ program implementation status (T7-SD5-02; F7-SD5-06).** Forward-looking analytical territory; G7-SD5-06 calibration depends on current implementation status.
10. **Neighborhood Restorations 925-unit portfolio outcome — July 2026 deadline (F7-SD5-09).** Active exemplar case for G7-SD5-02 LIHTC preservation finding.
11. **MC18 Tandem Plan confirmation-of-unresolvability (F7-SD5-MC18).** Per substructure §6 lead-direction-2026-05-02, Phase 3 confirms unresolvability or surfaces primary-source identification.
12. **Tract-level demographic, housing, and outcome retrieval surface (Section 4 Cluster F).** Substantial overlap at the data-source layer (OpenDataPhilly L&I; ACS; Reinvestment Fund; phila.gov/ohs; preservationdatabase.org); Phase 3 retrieval consolidates against the four-sub-area framework per Standard 6.
13. **Anchor institution CBA documentation surface (Section 4 Cluster E).** Likely institutional-retrieval territory per M2 v1.2 §7 boundedness; documented in unverified-items sidecar where web-search-tractable retrieval is insufficient.
14. **Standard 18 vector-format temporal-changes sidecar construction (Section 4 Cluster H).** Phase 3 sidecar construction operationalizes the cross-cycle persistence structure for D7 findings against subsequent reverification cycles.

### Open methodological questions for project lead

1. **SD2 anomalous flag prefix disposition.** The SD2 file's `F7-SD2-` / `T7-SD2-` prefix versus the `F7-SDn-` / `T7-SDn-` convention applied at the other six SDs. Phase 3 verification can normalize at verified-output construction (a Pattern 12 inline-correction with brief documentation) or preserve as historical artifact (no inline correction; documented in the verified output's verification status note). Either disposition is operationally workable; the lead's preference for Standard 2.E format-discipline normalization vs. archival preservation should direct Phase 3.

2. **Held-open magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) Phase 3 territory.** The synthesis preserves the question per substructure §8 question 1. Phase 3 verification under M2 v1.2 §7 is bounded to web-search-tractable sources; the relative-magnitude question is more likely Track 2 retrieval territory than Phase-3-resolvable. Two paths are operationally available: Phase 3 preserves the held-open territory as documented in the synthesis without attempting closure (most consistent with substructure §8 discipline); or Phase 3 surfaces the empirical-territory retrieval candidates the Standard 18 vectors identify, framed as Track 2 sequel rather than Phase 3 closure (operationally consistent but methodologically borderline). Lead direction would clarify which framing Phase 3 operates under.

3. **D7-Thread B (the "double pattern" of code enforcement) Phase 3 retrieval.** The thread is documented at the structural-analytical level at SD7 with Profile A / Profile B as illustrative typifications. F7-SD7-01 (current L&I OpenDataPhilly violation dataset analysis — tract-level violation concentration, common violation types, resolution rates and time-to-compliance, corporate-vs-individual landlord patterns) is the principal empirical-retrieval surface; the analysis is operationally substantial in Phase 3 if pursued, less substantial in Phase 3 if deferred to subsequent-cycle Track 2 work. Lead direction on Phase 3 priority for D7-Thread B empirical documentation would clarify scope.

4. **Cross-domain carry-forward to D11 Education (MC19 foregrounded).** Per lead direction 2026-05-02, MC19 is foregrounded methodologically with all four Standard 8 elements engaged. The cross-domain carry-forward to D11 is named in Section 5; the substructure §8 question 8 boundary discipline (D7 does not pre-occupy D11 territory) holds. When the D11 cycle runs, the substantive incorporation of D7 SD6's definitional-gap finding into D11's McKinney-Vento Title VII SD is Phase 0 substructure work for D11. The lead may direct whether the cross-domain notes file at finalization records the carry-forward as a substantive item (foregrounded methodologically; affects D11's substructure conversation) or as a standard cross-domain reference per Standard 9 / Standard 15.

5. **Phase 2 Pattern 14 prevention — Step 7.5 outcome.** The Step 7.5 Shape A integrity check passed at this Phase 2 close: embedded SD content for all seven SDs; five synthesis sections named and present in correct positions; self-assessment footer present with the five M2 §6 elements. The completion summary surfaced to the lead documents this. If the lead's Phase 2 audit of the OUTPUT identifies any element that should be re-verified against the integrity check, the surfacing happens at audit rather than at Phase 3 entry; the construction risks documented in M2 v1.2 §10 Pattern 14 worked example (D12 first-attempt OUTPUT) inform what audit attention should target.

### Phase 3 verification cycle's own self-assessment (added 2026-05-03)

**What Phase 3 produced well.** The Phase 3 verification cycle resolved 10 substantive temporal changes as Material Changes Summary entries with inline application at body locations per M2 v1.2 §7 Capability 2 + Appendix A: AFFH rule rescission (MC-01), HUD disparate-impact rule proposed rescission (MC-02), CFPB structural transformation under OBBBA (MC-03), 2023 CRA Final Rule rescission proposed (MC-04), HUD CoC NOFO disruption + FY2026 Appropriations Act bridge protections (MC-05), HUD FHEO enforcement-posture shift (MC-06), Philadelphia RTC expansion to ten zip codes (MC-07), H.O.M.E. plan Year 1 implementation milestones (MC-08), post-*Grants Pass* litigation landscape (MC-09); plus the SD2 prefix normalization as a Standard 9 cross-reference consistency correction at Case 3 high-volume granularity (MC-10). The convergence of Cluster A federal regulatory administrative-vulnerability findings — six of fourteen items resolved with documented regulatory-architecture withdrawal between draft date and verification date — substantially reinforces the Standard 11 administrative-vulnerability calibration the substructure surfaced and the synthesis-level Cross-cutting Finding D framed; the empirical fact of simultaneous regulatory withdrawal across AFFH, disparate-impact rule, CFPB structure, CRA Final Rule, and HUD CoC NOFO operationalizes the "structural feature, not episodic disruption" framing in the direction the substructure anticipated. The held-open displacement-magnitude question (G7-SD1-03) was preserved at Phase 3 per lead direction 2026-05-03 — Phase 3 did not attempt closure by analytical assertion; Track 2 sequel candidates are surfaced in the unverified-items sidecar; Standard 18 vector candidates are constructed in the temporal-changes sidecar as Phase 1 starting points where empirical retrieval was feasible. The MC10 SD2 prefix normalization was applied at Case 3 high-volume granularity per M2 v1.2 Appendix A with the principal anchor + retain-shorthand-policy disclosure pattern; Pattern 12 enforcement at the case-appropriate granularity is satisfied. F7-SD7-01 (D7-Thread B L&I tract-level retrieval) was deferred to subsequent-cycle Track 2 per lead direction 2026-05-03; the structural finding (G7-SD7-01) does not depend on tract-level retrieval for its analytical content per the lead's routing.

**What Phase 3 produced poorly.** Phase 3 verification operated under web-search-tractable boundedness per M2 v1.2 §7. The substantial PA-3 tract-level retrieval surface (Section 4 Cluster F: ~14 items) was not engaged in the Phase 3 web-search budget — these items require either OpenDataPhilly portal-level interaction, ACS table-level retrieval with disaggregation, or Reinvestment Fund / NCRC subscription-level analyses that exceeded the web-search-tractable surface. Cluster F items are documented in the unverified-items sidecar with disposition: deferred to subsequent-cycle work; web-search-tractable status pending. The Cluster G program-portfolio operational data surface (~17 items) was similarly under-engaged: while some items received partial verification through other Material Changes entries (e.g., the Right to Counsel and H.O.M.E. plan retrievals supplied program-level operational context), specific PHDC program-portfolio uptake data, current Land Bank disposition data, current PHARE Philadelphia awards, current TOPA NOIS volume, current EDP outcome data, current PSH inventory, and other operational figures were not retrieved at the line-item level. The Cluster E anchor institution disclosure surface (~6 items) received only structural verification: Penn for PILOTs campaign documentation noted; current Penn $10M/year educational contribution status (not formal PILOT) noted; specific Temple-area CBA, Schuylkill Yards / Drexel CBA, and current CBA-status documentation not retrieved at the institutional-disclosure level — these items are noted in the unverified-items sidecar as institutional-retrieval territory per M2 v1.2 §7 boundedness. A subsequent-cycle Phase 3 reverification engaging these clusters with deeper web-search budget or institutional-retrieval access would refine the calibration of multiple Track 2 G-flag findings (G7-SD1-04 RCO capacity-and-representativeness; G7-SD3-06 serial-filer; G7-SD4-04 FHEO complaint-volume-vs-likely-incidence; G7-SD5-01 Land Bank disposition pace; G7-SD6-02 PSH-to-need; G7-SD7-01 D7-Thread B). The bounding decision is the discipline's intentional shape — "close, not perfect" — but the boundary is documented.

**Standards followed most cleanly at Phase 3.** Standard 1 (regime declaration explicit at internet-access for verification phase, separated from Phase 1+2 training-data regime). Standard 11 (administrative-vulnerability calibration preserved and reinforced via documented regulatory-architecture-withdrawal cluster). Standard 17 (governmental-score handling: no new governmental scores introduced at Phase 3; D12 cross-domain reference to OBBBA architectural finding preserved with the CBO July 2025 → February 2026 baseline discipline carrying forward at the cross-domain interface). Standard 18 (vector-format temporal-changes sidecar constructed in 18.A format with anchor + dated vector points + analytical consequence). M2 v1.2 §7 Capability 2 (every Material Changes Summary entry corresponds to inline edit at body location(s); Pattern 12 enforcement satisfied at the M2 v1.2 Appendix A case-appropriate granularity for each entry — Case 1 for MC-07/MC-09, Case 2 for MC-01 through MC-06 + MC-08, Case 3 for MC-10).

**Standards followed least cleanly at Phase 3.** Standard 6 (geographic specificity at tract level): the four-sub-area framework holds at the structural level but tract-level disaggregation across all SDs remains the principal cycle-level limitation; Phase 3 web-search budget did not engage Cluster F retrieval surface. Cluster G program-portfolio operational completeness: similar bounding. The two limitations together define the principal subsequent-cycle Phase 3 reverification surface.

**Phase 3 verification statistics.**
- F-flags resolved at Phase 3 (web-search verification or partial structural confirmation): ~6 (T-flags primarily; F-flags partially via cross-reference)
- T-flags resolved at Phase 3 with Material Changes inline application: 10 (T7-SD2-01, T7-SD2-02, T7-SD3-04, T7-SD4-01, T7-SD4-02, T7-SD4-03, T7-SD5-02, T7-SD6-01, T7-SD6-02; T7-SD2-03 partial via MC-01 cross-reference)
- T-flags resolved as "no change" at Phase 3: 1 (T7-SD6-05 VAWA — 2022 reauthorization remains operative)
- T-flags partially refined: 1 (T7-SD3-02 — PA SB 546 + HB 72 introduced but not enacted)
- Standard 18 vector-format meta-flags moved to sidecar: 6 (T7-SD2-05, T7-SD3-05, T7-SD4-06, T7-SD5-06, T7-SD6-06, T7-SD7-06)
- Material Changes Summary entries: 10 (MC-01 through MC-09 substantive; MC-10 structural prefix normalization)
- Pattern 12 enforcement granularity: Case 1 (MC-07, MC-09); Case 2 (MC-01 through MC-06 + MC-08); Case 3 (MC-10)
- New sources added during Phase 3: ~30 web-fetched authoritative sources; full citations in Citation Registry sidecar below
- Items deferred to unverified-items sidecar: ~50 (Cluster E ~6; Cluster F ~14; Cluster G ~17; Cluster A residual ~6; Cluster B ~1; Cluster D ~7)
- F7-SD7-01 deferred to subsequent-cycle Track 2 per lead direction 2026-05-03: 1
- G7-SD1-03 held-open territory preserved per lead direction 2026-05-03: 1 (Track 2 sequel candidates surfaced in unverified-items sidecar; not closed by Phase 3 analytical assertion)
